# Norn — Build Log

One line per sub-checkpoint. A checkpoint is ✅ only when its "done when" is fully
true, the suite is green, and it is committed. Gates in `PLAN.md` are ticked only
when every line of the gate is true.

Method (binding): TDD (red → green → refactor), one package = one responsibility,
dependencies point inward (`cmd/` → `internal/*` → stdlib/crypto), interfaces at
boundaries only, no package imports a sibling it doesn't need. The **Python
reference implementation** under `contrib/norn-ref/` is written *from the spec,
first*, and generates the vectors the Go code must match — so the Go code never
becomes its own oracle.

Log fixture (tiles + cosigned checkpoint) moves to Phase 2 where Tessera produces
it and torchwood verifies it — hand-building a tlog in Python adds no assurance.

## Phase 1 — Core library (Go)

Decision after 0.13b: start Phase 1 rather than run a fifth gate. Phase 1 *is* a
second implementation of §4 and §5, so it surfaces spec gaps as a side effect of
being useful, and `PLAN.md` already schedules a cold-model gate at Phase 6
against the frozen spec.

Rule for this phase: the Go code is written **from `SPEC.md`**, and the vectors
are the oracle. Where Go and the Python reference disagree, the spec decides —
and if the spec is silent, that is a defect to record, not a coin to flip.

| # | Checkpoint | Done when | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1.1 | `internal/cbor` | deterministic encoder + strict decoder; every encoding negative rejected, every valid entry round-trips byte-exact | ✅ |
| 1.2 | `internal/entry` | envelope, `kid`, preimage, sign/verify, entry and leaf hashes; all 16 entry mirrors reproduced | ✅ |
| 1.3 | `internal/cjson`, `internal/state` | canonical JSON + state schema; every JSON file in the suite round-trips byte-exact | ✅ |
| 1.4 | `internal/replay` | every `(index, reason)` in the manifest, `state.json` byte-exact, and all 12 `genesis/` aborts | ✅ |
| 1.5 | `internal/note`, `internal/trust` | receipt verifies; fingerprint reproduced; all 14 `trust-bad/` rejected | ✅ |
| 1.6 | Fuzzing | 83.8M executions across three targets, zero failures | ✅ |
| 1.7 | Cross-implementation check | entries rebuilt in Go from their semantic values, signed independently, bytes identical to the shipped vectors | ✅ |

## Journal
- **2026-09-19 · 0.1 repo skeleton** — Go 1.26.4, module `github.com/onembyte/norn`, Apache-2.0, `make ci` (fmt-check / vet / race tests) green on 10 packages, CI matrix ubuntu+macos. Bug found and fixed before the first commit: `fmt-check` used `git ls-files`, which is empty before the first commit, so `gofmt -l` blocked on stdin — now `find | xargs`. Local commit only; GitHub remote not created yet (user decision, see Phase 6).
- **2026-09-19 · 0.2 spec tightened** — added SPEC §4.0 encoding profile (byte-level contract: integer/bytes/text/map rules, `kid`, entry hash, leaf hash, preimage bytes, `sigs` ordering, field constraints), §5.1 closed reason-code list (16 codes) with the precise signature-counting rule, canonical JSON definition in §12, resolved the `labels` open item (D-026). Corrected §4.1: key ordering is RFC 8949 §4.2.1 (bytewise of encoded key), not RFC 7049 length-first; the < 24-byte-key rule (D-027) makes both agree.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.3 Python reference (keys, encoding, signing)** — `contrib/norn-ref/`: `cbor` (deterministic encoder + *strict* decoder — rejects non-shortest lengths, out-of-order/duplicate map keys, floats/tags/null/negatives, trailing bytes), `keys` (named seeds, `kid`), `entry` (envelope, preimage, sign, entry/leaf hash), `canonjson`, `fixture` (one valid entry per type), `gen_vectors`. 19 Python tests green, written before the code. 34 vector files in `testdata/vectors/`. Generation is deterministic (tested by regenerating twice into temp dirs and comparing bytes). pynacl 1.6.2 in a venv; no `cbor2` dependency — writing the encoder is the point, since it is the byte-level contract.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.4 replay + rejection vectors** — `state.py` (state owns its shape and canonical serialisation, nothing else) and `replay.py` (one handler per entry type, registry dispatch, `Reject(code)` mapping 1:1 onto SPEC §5.1). 40-entry fixture log interleaving valid entries with one rejection per code: all 17 rejections land at the expected index, all 15 non-genesis codes covered, `bad-genesis` in its own log because it aborts replay. 36 Python tests green. Vectors: 96 files; `keys.json` gained `spare`/`printer`/`nas2` (pure additions — every existing key and entry byte unchanged, verified structurally).
  - **Security finding → D-028**: my first `signer_remove` refused to drop below `signer_thr`. That would make the last signer key unremovable — precisely the key you must remove when it is stolen (A3). Invariant deleted for signers, kept for roots.
  - Three tests failed on first green and **the tests were wrong, not the code**: the fixture deliberately re-enrols `pi-cam` after revoking it (device-id reuse), and an isolation test assumed rejected entries could simply be dropped — they cannot, because `after` is position-dependent, so dropping entries shifts indices and legitimately invalidates later ones. Rewritten to assert the attempted effects are absent.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.5 trust file, fingerprint, receipts** — `note.py` (c2sp signed-note: key-id derivation, verifier-key strings, sign/split/verify, receipt body) and `trust.py` (build/validate/encode, origin derivation, Crockford fingerprint, deterministic TOML). 62 Python tests green. Vectors now 101 files: `trust.cbor`/`trust.toml`/`trust.fingerprint`, `receipt.note`. Regeneration changed exactly `keys.json` (3 witness keys added), the two `policy` entries (D-030, real tlog-policy text) and `state.json`; every other entry byte unchanged.
  - **Security finding → D-029**: the spec's fingerprint was 32 bits (and self-contradictory), which an attacker substituting a trust file could grind in minutes. Now 80 bits, Crockford base32, no wordlist to freeze.
  - **Bug caught by a test**: verifier-key parsing split on every `+`, but standard base64 key data contains `+`. Go's note package splits at most twice; mine now does too, with a regression test that searches for a key whose base64 actually contains a `+`.
  - **Carried to Phase 2**: these note encodings are so far only self-consistent. Phase 2 must cross-check them against Go's `note` package and torchwood — that is the real verification, and it is now a gate line.

### Decision resolved — 0.6 was approved and run (2026-09-19)

0.6 requires giving a **fresh model** only `SPEC.md` + `testdata/vectors/` and
having it implement an entry verifier that passes every vector. That means
spawning a subagent, which I do not do without an explicit go-ahead.

Why it matters *now* rather than later: I wrote the spec **and** the Python
reference, and I am about to write the Go implementation from the same spec.
All three share my blind spots. A genuinely cold reader is the only thing that
proves the spec is self-sufficient — and a spec gap is far cheaper to fix before
the Go code exists than after.

| Option | Consequence |
|---|---|
| **Run it now** (recommended) | One subagent run; spec gaps found before Phase 1 |
| Defer to Phase 6 | Plan already has a cold-model gate on the frozen spec; Phase 1 will surface *some* gaps, but only the ones I happen not to share |
| Skip entirely | `SPEC.md` never independently validated; the "second implementation" claim in `PUBLICATION.md` §7 weakens |

Loop paused here pending that answer.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.6 cold-model gate — FAILED.** An independent agent got `SPEC.md` + `testdata/vectors/` and nothing else (isolated copy; verified `SPEC.md` contains no reference to the Python reference, and the agent was barred from `~/norn`). It wrote ~2,100 lines of Go, stdlib only, including its own CBOR codec, and passed **227/227** checks — Stage 1 entries 162/162, Stage 2 replay 45/45 with `state.json` byte-identical, Stage 3 notes 20/20. Verdict nonetheless **SPEC INSUFFICIENT**, because several answers were reverse-engineered from the vectors rather than read from the prose.
  - **The gate is NOT passed.** Passing 227 checks is not the bar; the bar is that a second implementer could not have gotten it wrong. They could.
  - 29 defects recorded in `SPEC-DEFECTS.md`. Every Tier-1/Tier-2 item was spot-checked against the real files before being accepted — all confirmed. Sharpest three: §5's state sketch names fields that do not exist (`policy_text`, `valid_until`) and omits three that do (`name`, `net`, `size`), while §5 demands byte-identical state JSON; §12 says "keys sorted" in a document that spends two paragraphs establishing the *opposite* order for CBOR, and following §4.1 there breaks 16/16 entry mirrors; `dup-entry` and `unknown-type` are missing from §5's numbered procedure and their position decides vector 13's reason code.
  - Eleven Tier-3 items are undecided by the prose **and** by the vectors — two honest implementations can diverge on all of them and both pass clean, which is the exact failure a conformance suite exists to prevent.
  - Confirmed sound: §4.0/§4.1/§4.2 and §5.1's signature-counting paragraph. Stage 1 passed 162/162 on the first run with no reference to any vector.
  - Next: 0.7 repairs the spec, then 0.8 re-runs the gate with a *fresh* agent on a *new* isolated copy. The Go core library (Phase 1) waits until the spec it would be built from is sound.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.7a Tier-1 spec repair** — §5 rewritten: replay now states its `(anchor, entries) → state` signature and where the anchor comes from; entry 0 gets its own 8-step procedure including the previously-unstated **genesis bootstrap** (signatures counted against `body.roots`/`body.root_thr`, since there is no earlier state) and abort semantics; entries 1…n get a complete numbered list with `dup-entry` and `unknown-type` in their load-bearing positions, plus a per-type table fixing each rule's internal check order. New **§5.3 canonical state schema** — a normative field table, replacing the sketch that named two fields which do not exist and omitted three that do. §12 now pins canonical JSON key order to **UTF-8 code point** and says explicitly that it is *not* the CBOR order of §4.1, with the worked contrast; documents that schema fields are always emitted; and describes `log/manifest.json` and `bad-genesis/` instead of tiles that do not exist there.
  - **Security decision → D-031** (was open question D15): entry hashes enter the `dup-entry` seen-set **before** validation, so a rejected entry cannot be re-inserted later against a changed state. The concrete attack this closes: re-inserting the original `enroll` of a revoked device to resurrect it — `after` bounds reordering but not re-insertion further along the log. Reference implementation changed to match, with three tests including the resurrection attempt.
  - Also closed while rewriting the procedure, because it had to say something: D13 (genesis bootstrap), D14 (malformed entry 0), D16 (`log_key` appends), D17 (re-enrolment replaces the record), D26 (`size`). D21's *order* is now normative; its vectors are owed in 0.7c.
  - 65 Python tests green. Vector regeneration produced **zero** changes — the rule change altered no existing outcome and the rest was documentation.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.7b Tier-2 spec repair** — **D8 decided and vectored**: §5 and §5.1 disagreed on whether a `rotate` may re-present the device's *own* active `wg_pk`, and no vector covered it, so two implementations could differ and both pass. §5.1's reading wins for a concrete reason — a device must be able to rotate its **identity key while keeping its WireGuard key**, or migrating from a software key to a hardware key would churn every peer's table. Log grew to 42 entries: `40-rotate-device-key-only` (accepted) and `41-bad-dup-wg-rotate` (taking another active device's key → `dup-wg`). **D7**: the `labels` field allowed 1–64-byte keys that the §4.0 map-key cap makes unencodable; now 1–23, and replay actually enforces the ≤32-entry and ≤256-byte-value limits it always claimed (they were never checked — four new tests). **D9**: §9 rewritten from the shipped trust file — normative CBOR field table, honest TOML sample, fingerprint rule; the old sample was wrong on four fields (`net_id`, base64 `genesis_hash`, `"24h"`, missing witness `url`), any of which changes the fingerprint. **D10**: §4.1 no longer reassures the reader that "both readings agree"; it names the order that is wrong and points at §12.
  - 76 Python tests green. Two tests went stale the moment the fixture grew — the extra `nas` rotation they appended was signed by a key that fixture entry 40 had just superseded. That is the rule working, so they became two new tests: the superseded key can no longer sign, and the new one can.
  - Regeneration added the two new entries and touched `keys.json`, `manifest.json`, `state.json`. **No existing `.cbor` byte moved.**
- **2026-09-19 · 0.7c Tier-3 closed** — the eight remaining questions the gate found answerable two ways by the prose **and** unexercised by the vectors are now decided in `SPEC.md` and pinned by vectors 42–48 (log is 49 entries). Seven of the eight already matched the reference implementation — they were simply never *stated*, which is the failure mode the gate exists to catch. The eighth was a real change:
  - **D-032 · proof of possession on `rotate`**: a rotate that installs a new identity key must now be signed by **both** the current and the incoming key. Only the device can rotate, so installing a key it does not hold harms only itself — but irrecoverably: it could never rotate again without a signer-assisted re-enrolment. It holds both keys at that moment, so the second signature costs nothing, and it matches D-008 for `enroll`. Four tests, vectors 40 (accepted) and 46 (rejected).
  - **D-033 · `bad-thr` vs `root-invariant`**: `bad-thr` is about the *value* being outside `1 … len(set)`; `root-invariant` is reserved for a `root_remove` shrinking the set below the threshold. Both previously fired on `root_thr` with `thr > len(roots)`.
  - Also stated and vectored: unauthorised `revoke` → `threshold` (43); `role` outside the enum → `decode`, now explicitly distinguished from a well-formed signature whose `kid` does not resolve (44); `sigs` mandatory, omitted → `decode`, empty → `threshold` (45); and the per-type check order proved by entries wrong in two ways at once — 47 (device signature before `dup-id`) and 48 (root quorum before the per-type rule).
  - 88 Python tests green. Exactly one existing vector byte moved: `log/40`, which now carries the incoming key's signature.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.7d Tier-4 closed — all 29 defects fixed.** §7 now restates the `signed-note` framing normatively (signed text includes its final newline and excludes the blank separator; em-dash signature line; key id binds the *name*; split a verifier key on `+` **at most twice**) so an implementer needs no external spec — the gate had recovered that boundary by hashing three candidates against `receipt.json`. §4.0's preimage length wording fixed. Byte-string renderings reconciled: hex in TOML, raw bytes in CBOR, never base64. **§12 rewritten honestly**, including a new **§12.1 coverage table naming what is *not* vectored** — §6 tiles/bundles/checkpoints, §8 proof bundles, §10 the verifier loop, §11 fork evidence — and why they wait for Phase 2: a hand-rolled tlog would test this implementation against itself and prove nothing. The old §12 promised proof bundles, a `fork/` directory and a tiled log that do not exist.
  - **D-034**: `params.witness_policy` removed. The witness policy now has exactly two homes — `norn.trust.policy` and the `policy` entry — because a third "informational" copy is how two verifiers come to disagree about the quorum guarding the log. Advisory copies get read.
  - 91 Python tests green. No vector changed (`witness_policy` was never used by the fixture).
  - `SPEC-DEFECTS.md` closed: 29/29. Four decisions came out of it — D-031, D-032, D-033, D-034 — three of them security-relevant.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.8 cold-model gate run 2 — FAILED, and it caught two regressions I had introduced myself.** A fresh agent on a fresh copy scored **134/134** and still returned SPEC INSUFFICIENT. Its method was sharper than run 1's: it built **eight variant implementations**, each a different defensible reading, and re-ran the suite against each — a variant that still passes proves the *vectors* do not pin that decision. Six of eight passed, including one that enforces the very invariant **D-028 forbids**.
  - **The headline was my own damage.** §14 and the canonical-JSON rules had both been *deleted by my regex splices* — §14 in 0.2 (an over-greedy three-line substitution ate the heading after the bullet it targeted), the JSON rules in 0.7d (a §12 rewrite spliced between two anchors that bracketed more than intended). Verified against git history. Neither the 91-test suite nor gate run 1 noticed, because **a section with no vectors can vanish silently**. §5.3's central MUST — byte-identical canonical JSON — had been pointing at a table of filenames.
  - **0.9a, the real fix → D-035**: `internal/specdoc` parses `SPEC.md` on every `make ci` and fails on a numbering gap, a duplicate number, a dangling `§N` reference, an empty section, or the absence of any section in a required manifest. Written test-first; it went red on exactly the two defects, plus two of its own false positives I then fixed (references into *other* documents — `RFC 6962 §2.1` — and parent sections whose body is legitimately empty). "Be more careful with sed" was not an acceptable fix.
  - §14 restored and completed: key order, hex byte strings, number and boolean form, the trailing newline, and **the full escaping table** — which was never specified at all. Vectors `49-policy-escaping` and `50-enroll-unicode-labels` now exercise every row: `"` `\` `\b` `\t` `\n` `\f` `\r` and `\u00xx` lowercase; and verbatim `/ < > &` DEL ñ 日本 🔑 א, plus recursive UTF-8 key sorting. Previously no vector contained a single character that would ever be escaped, so an HTML-escaping implementation passed clean.
  - 96 Python tests, 11 Go tests, `make ci` green. No existing `.cbor` byte moved.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.9b-1 run-2 defects E2–E11 closed; §12.1 made accurate.** Spec: `log_keys[].since_index` defined (E2); the false "no other field is populated beyond `net`" sentence replaced with what `bad-genesis/state.json` actually contains (E3); §5.2's `no-device-sig` row no longer claims `revoke`, which is `threshold` (E4); the encoding profile no longer claims to govern canonical state, which it cannot — that state is keyed by `device_id` and 32-byte keys, both over the 23-byte map-key cap (E7); `genesis.params` and `revoke.reason` marked OPTIONAL (E8, D-037); the trust file's TOML→CBOR mapping spelled out, including `[[witness]]` → `witnesses`, that array order is fingerprint-significant, and that absent optionals are omitted (E10); `origin`'s alphabet named at its definition site, in a document that uses two base32 alphabets, plus the fingerprint separator, the empty-log case and the receipt/mirror schemas (E11).
  - **D-036**: `kid` is a constrained field — 16 lowercase hex, else `decode`. A malformed `kid` is a schema violation, not a key that fails to resolve; the two yield different reason codes. `sigs` ordering enforced for the same reason: an unsorted array would give one signature set two canonical forms.
  - **Vectors added (58-entry log): 51** `sigs: []` → `threshold`, the case §4.0 singled out and never tested; **52** malformed `kid`; **53** a `signer_remove` that leaves one signer against a threshold of two — **accepted**, which is the first vector actually defending D-028, since run 2 showed a variant enforcing the forbidden invariant passed clean; **54** the roots' recovery that makes the degraded state workable again, which is the other half of the D-028 argument; **55–57** byte-level patches of a valid entry violating exactly one profile rule each (non-shortest integer, `null`, unsorted map keys) — the profile previously had **zero** negative vectors, so a decoder skipping every check scored a clean pass.
  - All 8 test failures on first run were vector 53 doing its job: with one signer left, nothing needing two signer signatures can be appended. Fixed by vectoring the recovery too, rather than weakening the vector.
  - **§12.1 rewritten to be true** — it had claimed §4 and §9 were fully covered when §4's field constraints had no rejecting vector and §9 had no negative fixture at all. It now lists what is vectored, what is not, and who owes it.
  - 101 Python tests, 11 Go tests, `make ci` green. No existing `.cbor` byte moved.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.9b-3 — the last vector gaps closed; `SPEC-DEFECTS-2.md` at 12/12.** `genesis/` replaces `bad-genesis/` with **all six** abort paths, each a single-entry log with its own anchor and expected state, because an abort stops replay and so the cases cannot share one log: anchor pinning a different genesis · entry 0 not a genesis · a genesis for another network · `after ≠ 0` · `root_thr` above the root set · bootstrap signature shortfall. Previously five of the six were unreachable by any vector. `trust-bad/` adds **ten** trust files that decode cleanly but must be refused — unknown member, wrong version, short `net`, `origin` mismatch, short `genesis_hash`, malformed `log_key`, no witnesses, duplicate witness name, policy without a `quorum` line, missing policy — where §9 previously had a single positive fixture. Entry **51** enrols a contractor with an expiry that survives to the terminal state, so §5.3's `expires` rule finally has a discriminating case; every device in every state had `expires: 0` before.
  - 109 Python tests, 11 Go tests, `make ci` green. Log is 59 entries; 172 vector files. The only `.cbor` to change was the one `bad-genesis/` file, now superseded.
  - §12.1's "not vectored" list is down to the four rows that genuinely wait on Phase 2 and Phase 4 — §6 tiles, §8 proof bundles, §10 the verifier loop, §11 fork evidence — each because a hand-rolled tlog would test this implementation against itself.
- **2026-09-19 · Phase 0 gate bookkeeping.** Three of the four PASS lines verified true and ticked: 12 adversaries (needs ≥ 6), 7 ledger architectures with D-009 recorded (needs ≥ 4), and all **14/14** entry types vectored plus a full replay `state.json`. The fourth, the cold-model gate, stays **unticked** — runs 1 and 2 both returned SPEC INSUFFICIENT and run 3 is pending.
  - Found while checking: the Phase 0 deliverable claims "CI running `go test` + `go vet` + `staticcheck`", but `staticcheck` was only ever a `make lint` target — never in `make ci`, never in the GitHub workflow, and **never actually run**. Ran it: clean, exit 0. Added `make lint` as its own CI step so the deliverable is now true rather than aspirational.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.10 cold-model gate run 3 — PASSED: `SPEC SUFFICIENT WITH GAPS`, 396/396.** Third fresh agent, third fresh copy. It implemented the whole protocol from the prose with **zero corrections** to its reading of §4, §5, §7, §9 and §14, and named the §4.1-vs-§14.1 sort-order warning, the `role`/`kid`/`threshold` distinction, §5.1's numbered order, §5.2's "`no-device-sig` is not for `revoke`" note and §14.2's escaping table as exactly what a second implementer needs. That is the criterion this checkpoint was written against, so the gate passes — and Phase 0's fourth PASS line with it.
  - It set its own bar higher than mine and reported the spec still **not** sufficient outright, for two honest reasons: some rules exist only in the vectors, and **18 defensible-but-wrong readings pass the entire suite**. Recorded as 0.11.
  - **A landmine of my own making, found by its variant method:** `log/` held **66 `.cbor` files for a 59-entry log** — seven orphans left by the 0.9b-3 renumbering, plus five orphan `.reason` files. Because `log/` is a numbered sequence, an orphan sorts *between* live entries, so an implementer who globs and sorts the directory — the obvious reading, which §12 invited by calling it "the replay fixture, N entries in order" — silently loads a longer, wrongly-ordered log. Fixed at the root: `gen_vectors` now tracks every path it writes and **prunes** anything else, with two tests; §12 now says the file names are labels and the sequence comes from `manifest.json`.
  - Other self-inflicted defects it caught: §12 said 49 entries while §12.1 said 59; five negative-vector indices in §12.1 were stale from the renumbering; "all 16 reason codes" is 15, since `bad-genesis` aborts and cannot appear in the log; "all six abort paths" omits the decode path; and the header claim that **every MUST is vectored was simply false**. All corrected, and §12.1 now carries a third table — **"vectored only in part"** — naming the fourteen MUSTs an implementation can skip and still pass.
  - 111 Python tests, 11 Go tests, staticcheck clean. 160 vector files.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.11a — the rules that existed only in the vectors now exist in the prose.** §9.1 states the `witnesses` minimum (≥ 1) *and why*: with nothing to cosign, the policy's quorum can never be met, so an anchor with no witnesses could never accept a checkpoint — `trust-bad/07` enforced a rule §9 never wrote. Also stated: `ts` and the other envelope keys are mandatory (previously an argument from silence); the governance types get an explicit "body well-formed and closed" step, and §5.1 now spells out the consequence that an under-signed governance entry with a malformed body is `threshold`, not `decode`; the entry `.json` mirror follows the **CBOR** absent-field convention, unlike `state.json`.
  - **D-038**: entry bodies are closed schemas. Run 3 shipped a variant that left them open and passed — and so did this implementation, by accident rather than by rule. Agreement without a rule is not agreement. A single `BODY_KEYS` registry now enforces §4.3's key list in one place instead of across fourteen handlers.
  - **D-039**: `thr: 0` is `bad-thr`, never `decode` — §4.0 and §5.2 had described the same input two ways. Both halves of the clause are vectored now: 0 (61) and above-the-set-size (19).
  - Vectors 59–61 added (log is 62 entries): unknown envelope key, unknown body key, zero threshold.
  - **Refactor**: the vector-count assertions were hard-coded and had broken on *every* addition while teaching nothing. They now derive from the fixture, so the test still asserts one file per fixture item and stays quiet as the fixture grows.
  - 118 Python tests, 11 Go tests, `make ci` green. No existing `.cbor` byte moved.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.11b — negative vectors that actually test the rule (D-040).** Run 3's sharpest vector critique was that the encoding negatives were *over-determined*: `57-bad-cbor-null` puts a `null` where a typed `ts`/`after` lives, so any implementation with a typed model rejects it for the type and never exercises the profile rule at all — a decoder skipping every profile check still passed. Acting on that split the rules in two.
  - The **type** bans (floats, negative integers, `undefined`) are untestable by construction: no Norn field has a type that would accept such a value. §4.0 now says so plainly instead of implying a vector covers them.
  - The **encoding** bans are testable precisely because a lenient decoder turns them into a *valid entry* — a tag unwraps, an indefinite-length string concatenates, a duplicate key resolves last-wins. Four such vectors added (63–66), and the fixture ships a deliberately permissive decoder that **proves** each one decodes to the same valid note, rather than the test merely asserting it.
  - Entry **62** is the unpatched control the four are patches of. Without a control, a negative can be rejected for a fault its author never intended and nobody notices.
  - Field-constraint negatives 67–71: `device_id` uppercase and leading-dot, short `pk`, short `sig`, oversized `text` — none of which had a rejecting vector.
  - The new tests first failed with `dup-entry` rather than `decode`, because the negatives now live *in* the log and the tests were appending copies — D-031 doing its job. Rewritten to assert against the log's own outcomes.
  - Log is 72 entries, 185 vector files. 123 Python tests, 11 Go tests, `make ci` green. No existing `.cbor` byte moved.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.11c — the "vectored only in part" table is now empty.** Every MUST §12.1 admitted was unenforced has a vector: `after` as a **floor** rather than an equality (76 accepted with `after` < index — previously *every* `after` in the fixture equalled its own index, so a variant demanding equality passed); a repeat of a **rejected** entry (77), which is the first vector defending D-031's "whether or not it is later rejected"; a signer count strictly **between** zero and the threshold (78), where every earlier `threshold` negative carried zero valid signatures; a signature whose declared role contradicts its key's current role (79); three more genesis aborts — an undecodable entry 0, duplicate roots, and a key in both roles; four more isolating encoding negatives with their own controls (invalid UTF-8, integer > 2⁶³−1, 24-byte map key, unsorted `sigs`).
  - **Found while doing it:** §9.1's "the log key's note name MUST equal `origin`" was **not enforced** by the validator — prose with nothing behind it. Now enforced, with the reason recorded (a checkpoint signed by a key named for another origin must not verify here) and vectored as `trust-bad/11`.
  - **D-041**: vector names now derive from their index, and the duplicate-source entry is looked up by the property it needs rather than by number. Names had drifted twice, and one vector fetched `b.items[67]` — a stale number there would silently point at an *accepted* entry and turn a `dup-entry` vector into something else entirely. Two tests now fail the build on any name/index disagreement.
  - Along the way my own "lenient" decoder turned out not to be lenient about UTF-8, and the isolation test hard-coded `t == "note"` though two negatives patch a different base. Both fixed — the isolation claim is only worth what the proof of it is worth.
  - Log is 82 entries, 213 vector files. 134 Python tests, 11 Go tests, `make ci` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.12 gate run 4 — `SUFFICIENT WITH GAPS`, and it found the worst defect yet.** 335 checks, 0 failures, then **57 variant implementations** to find which decisions the vectors fail to pin.
  - **F1: a signature-blind replay passed the entire suite.** With `ed25519.Verify` stubbed to `true`, all 84 replay outcomes and both other stages reproduced exactly. Every "bad signature" negative was detectable without cryptography — missing, too few, wrong device, mislabelled role — and not one *failed to verify*. Confirmed locally before accepting it. **D-043**: two vectors now carry syntactically perfect, cryptographically wrong signatures, and a test fails the build if stubbing verification ever stops changing the outcome.
  - **F2/F3/F4: four negatives did not test what they claimed.** Two carried `after: 76` at indices 69/70 — rejected for `after`, never reaching their own rule, collateral from my 0.11c renumbering that D-041 had not covered (it derived *names* from the index but left `after` hard-coded). Three patched bytes *inside the signature preimage* without re-signing, so they were rejected for the broken signature; §4.0's claim that a lenient decoder "produces a valid entry" from them was false. **D-044**: a negative must fail for its own rule by one of two constructions — the patch canonicalises away and the original signatures still verify, or it cannot and the entry is re-signed over the patched preimage — and tests now assert one holds for every encoding negative. Checking run 4's claim that `63`–`66` were fine revealed **all eight** needed the check, and that the two routes are genuinely different.
  - **F5: §12.1 cited ~13 wrong vector numbers**, with two different offsets in one table, plus a negative listed as a control. **D-045**: the spec now cites vectors **by name**, and `internal/specdoc` fails the build if a citation stops resolving.
  - Log is 84 entries, 217 vector files. 141 Python tests, 15 Go tests, `make ci` + `make vectors-check` green.
  - F7–F11 remain open in `SPEC-DEFECTS-4.md`: ~20 further unpinned readings, over-determined trust and genesis negatives, and §16 enrollment requests with no vectors at all.
- **2026-09-19 · 0.13b — F8, F9, F11 closed; F7 mostly.** The sharpest result: **D-046, my reference implementation was on the wrong side of my own spec.** §5.1 states that authorisation (step 7) precedes the type rule (step 8, which begins with the body check), and spells out the consequence — an under-signed governance entry with a malformed body is `threshold`, not `decode`. My implementation checked the body first. Run 4 had flagged exactly this as an unpinned variant and I assumed it was merely unvectored; it was a genuine divergence. Implementation corrected and vectored.
  - **F7**: `params` merge-vs-replace is now distinguishable — genesis carries a **non-default `mmd`**, so replacing with defaults no longer coincides with merging. `log_key` gets a second rotation, so append-vs-replace is visible. Device-signed `note` → `threshold`; `revoke` by root quorum accepted, a path §4.3 lists and no vector used.
  - **F8**: trust negatives `13-origin-only` (16-byte `net`, `log_key` renamed to agree with the wrong origin, so only the derivation rule can fire) and `14-missing-name` isolate two MUSTs that previously had only over-determined negatives.
  - **F9**: three more genesis invariants — duplicate signer, `signer_thr` above the set, empty `roots`.
  - **F11 prose**: `sigs: []` yields `no-device-sig` for `enroll`/`rotate`, not `threshold` — the old text over-generalised from the one `note` vector; field constraints are `bad-genesis` at index 0; `dup-wg` scope differs between `enroll` (any device) and `rotate` (another device); each `sigs` element is itself a closed map and "well-formed `sigs`" is now defined in one place.
  - **D-047**: run 4 had to invent a nesting cap "with no basis in the text". §4.0 now fixes depth at 8 and entry size at 65536 bytes, **and the reference enforces both** — including a truncated length header that must fail without allocating. Stating a limit without enforcing it is the defect class this whole exercise keeps surfacing.
  - Log is 89 entries, 235 vector files, 12 genesis cases, 14 trust negatives. 153 Python tests, 15 Go tests, `make ci` + `make vectors-check` green.
  - **Deferred — F10, §16 enrollment requests have no vectors at all.** §16 is a stub added in plan part 7 describing a bech32m request format that nothing implements yet; vectoring it means designing it properly, which belongs with the enrollment CLI in a later phase, not in Phase 0. §12.1 must say so rather than stay silent.

### Decision point after 0.13b — keep hardening, or start Phase 1?

Four gate runs so far, each a fresh agent on a fresh copy:

| run | verdict | headline finding |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | INSUFFICIENT | 29 defects; §5's state sketch named fields that did not exist |
| 2 | INSUFFICIENT | canonical JSON **did not exist** — I had deleted it with a bad splice |
| 3 | SUFFICIENT WITH GAPS | 66 `.cbor` files for a 59-entry log: seven orphans from a renumbering |
| 4 | SUFFICIENT WITH GAPS | **a signature-blind replay passed the entire suite** |

Severity has **not** decreased monotonically — run 4 found the worst defect of
the four. But Phase 0 has consumed the whole session so far, and Phases 1–7 (the
Go library, the log server, `nornd`, gossip, the lab, the demos) are the actual
product. Phase 1 is itself a second implementation of §4 and §5 and will surface
spec gaps as a side effect of being useful.

Current state: 89-entry log, 235 vector files, 12 genesis aborts, 14 trust
negatives, 153 Python tests, 15 Go tests, 47 recorded decisions. Open: the F7
residue (low severity) and F10 (§16, deferred to the enrolment CLI by design).
- **2026-09-19 · 1.1 `internal/cbor`** — deterministic encoder and strict decoder written from §4.0, tests first. All RFC 8949 examples in the profile, the §4.1 map-key trap, 17 non-canonical/out-of-profile rejections, the depth and size limits including a truncated length header that must fail without allocating, and **every valid entry in the suite round-trips byte-exact** (80+ files across `entries/` and `log/`).
  - **First payoff from starting Phase 1 → D-048.** My round-trip test skips anything that fails to decode, which would pass with a decoder that rejects nothing — so I added a test naming each encoding negative and requiring it to fail. `bad-unsorted-sigs` did **not** fail, and shouldn't: CBOR canonicalises *map* key order and says nothing about *array* order, so "sigs sorted by kid" is a schema rule enforced at §5.1 step 3, not something a codec can see. §4.0 now states the layering and §12.1 files the vector under schemas. A misclassification the Python reference could never surface, because there the two layers live in one process.
  - Go: 6 packages, 21 tests, `make ci` + staticcheck green.
- **2026-09-19 · 1.2 `internal/entry`** — typed envelope model, `kid`, canonical encode, domain-separated preimage, sign/verify, entry and leaf hashes. Written from §3/§4.0–§4.2, tests first, green on the first run against the vectors.
  - All **16 entry mirrors** reproduced: entry hash, leaf hash, preimage hash and byte length; every entry re-encodes **byte-exact from the typed model**, which is a real round trip rather than a copy of the input. All 21 Ed25519 keys in `keys.json` confirmed to derive from their seeds and to match their recorded `kid`. Every signature in the catalogue verifies.
  - The model is typed rather than map-shaped, so the compiler enforces what the schema says, and `After` is a pointer — absent is genuinely distinct from zero, as §4.0 requires.
  - Two layering checks that matter more than the happy path: the envelope-schema negatives (unknown key, missing `sigs`, malformed `kid`, `role` outside the enum, short `sig`, unsorted `sigs`) must be rejected **here**, and a **forged signature must still decode cleanly** — refusing it at the decoder would turn a `threshold` vector into a `decode` one and quietly break the D-043 guarantee.
  - Go: 7 packages, 29 tests, `make ci` + staticcheck green.
- **2026-09-19 · 1.3 `internal/cjson` + `internal/state`** — canonical JSON per §14 and the state schema per §5.3, tests first.
  - **The standard library is unusable here, and that is the whole point of §14 existing.** Go's `encoding/json` HTML-escapes `<`, `>` and `&`, and emits `\uXXXX` in **uppercase**. §14.2 forbids both. Either would produce a state that no other implementation reproduces while looking perfectly reasonable in a terminal — precisely the divergence run 2 predicted when it showed an HTML-escaping variant passing the whole suite. So `cjson` is hand-written, with the §14.2 escape table as a literal map and everything else emitted verbatim.
  - Unit tests are only as good as my reading of §14, so the real check is against the artifacts: **every canonical-JSON file the suite ships** — `state.json`, all 16 entry mirrors, all 12 genesis states, the manifest, the receipt, `keys.json` — is parsed and re-serialised **byte-exact**, trailing newline included.
  - `internal/state` keeps byte strings as lowercase hex, because that is how §14 renders them *and* how `active_wg` is keyed; carrying two representations would only invite them to disagree. Tests assert the §5.3 invariants directly: every field emitted even after an abort at genesis, `active_wg` derivable from the active devices, and zero values **present rather than absent** — with the fixture required to carry devices both with and without an expiry, so the rule has a discriminating case.
  - Go: 9 packages, 42 tests, `make ci` + staticcheck + `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 1.4 `internal/replay` — a genuine second implementation, agreeing byte for byte.** The §5.1 procedure followed literally, one handler per type, reason codes mapping one-to-one onto §5.2. **Green on the first run:**
  - every one of the **89 `(index, reason)` outcomes** in the manifest;
  - **`state.json` reproduced byte for byte** — 13 top-level fields, 8 devices, every label, every rejection, in canonical JSON with its trailing newline;
  - all **12 genesis cases** abort and match their own `state.json`;
  - the **empty log** yields the zero state (§5.1's rule, which had no vector until run 4 named it).
  - **D-043 holds in Go too**: the signature verifier is injectable precisely so a test can stub it, and the test asserts that stubbing *changes the outcome*. It does — a blind replay admits exactly entries 87 and 88, the two forged-signature vectors. That guarantee is now enforced in both implementations rather than asserted in prose.
  - Two spec subtleties the code had to get right and did: the **genesis bootstrap**, where signatures count against the roots in the entry's *own* body because no earlier state exists; and **D-046's ordering**, where the root quorum is checked before the body schema, so an under-signed governance entry with a malformed body is `threshold`.
  - Go: 10 packages, 47 tests, `make ci` + staticcheck + `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 1.5 `internal/note` + `internal/trust`** — the signed-note framing and the trust anchor, tests first, both green on the first run.
  - `note`: key ids that bind the **name** (so one key under two names has two ids), verifier keys that **split at most twice** because standard base64 contains `+` — the bug that bit the Python reference in 0.5, caught here by a test that hunts for a key whose encoding actually contains one. The shipped `receipt.note` verifies, and its rebuilt text hashes to `receipt.json`'s `text_sha256`, which pins the signed-text boundary §7 spells out: through the newline ending the last line, excluding the blank separator. Run 2 had to recover that boundary by hashing three candidates; it is now read straight from the prose.
  - `trust`: parse, validate, canonical re-encode, `origin` derivation, Crockford fingerprint. **Re-encoding reproduces `trust.cbor` byte for byte** — which matters because the fingerprint is taken over those bytes, so a field name or type that differs silently changes it. All **14** damaged anchors refused, each having decoded cleanly first, so the refusal comes from validation rather than the codec.
  - Two alphabets in one file and they must never be interchanged: RFC 4648 base32 for `origin`, Crockford for the fingerprint. Both are named at their definition sites and the code comments say why.
  - Go: 12 packages, 61 tests, `make ci` + staticcheck + `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 1.6 fuzzing (in progress)** — three targets written, all passing their first bursts; the gate's full hour is **running in the background** and 1.6 stays open until it finishes.
  - The property that matters is **not** "does not panic". `FuzzDecodeIsCanonical` asserts that anything the decoder *accepts* re-encodes to **identical bytes** — because a canonical form is only canonical if there is exactly one encoding per value. An input that decodes but re-encodes differently would mean two encodings of one entry, both accepted, with **different hashes and different signatures**. That is the bug a "no panic" fuzzer would never find.
  - `FuzzReplayNeverPanics` and `FuzzReplayAfterAValidGenesis` treat replay as what it is: code that consumes bytes an untrusted log operator chose. A verifier that crashes on a malformed log is a denial of service against the network it exists to protect. The second target seeds a *valid* genesis first, because that is where a hostile operator has the most room — there is state to corrupt. It also asserts the §5.3 invariant that `active_wg` never disagrees with `devices`.
  - First bursts: **3.3M executions**, zero failures — 1.87M on the canonical property, 1.28M on single-entry replay, 127k on post-genesis replay.
- **2026-09-19 · 1.7 cross-implementation check — Go and Python produce identical bytes from identical inputs.** Everything before this re-encoded entries that had been *decoded from the suite*, which exercises the encoder only along a path the decoder already vouched for. This test rebuilds each entry **from its semantic values** — the field values out of the `.json` mirror, the signing keys out of `keys.json` — and requires the result to equal the shipped `.cbor`.
  - All **16** match. Signatures are recreated by signing, never copied out of the vector, which works because Ed25519 is deterministic: two implementations given the same key and message produce the same 64 bytes. That makes the comparison meaningful rather than circular.
  - This is the strongest form of the claim Phase 1 was for: it is not "Go agrees about bytes it was handed" but "**Go, given the same inputs, produces the same bytes**". The gate line originally said "vectors re-exported from Go and diffed", which would have meant reimplementing the whole Python fixture in Go — a lot of duplicated fixture code testing the same property less directly.
  - **Fuzz campaign, target 1 of 3 complete: 54,625,215 executions, PASS** on the canonical round-trip property. Targets 2 and 3 still running; 1.6 stays open until all three finish.
  - Go: 12 packages, 62 tests.
- **2026-09-19 · 1.6 closed — 83,791,152 fuzz executions, zero failures.** Canonical round-trip 54.6M · replay on arbitrary bytes 26.8M · replay after a valid genesis 2.4M. The third is slowest per execution because it replays two entries and serialises the state each time, which is the point: it exercises the path where a hostile operator has state to corrupt.
- **2026-09-19 · Phase 1 gate PASSED — all four lines.** Checking the second line by hand rather than assuming found **two named rejections with no vector at all**: "wrong signer" and "signer revoked in an earlier entry". The second is the more serious — nothing in the suite tested that a **removed signer key becomes inert**, which is the entire purpose of `signer_remove` and the recovery path D-028 argues for at length. Now vectored, with an isolation test proving the *same* entry is accepted while signer2 is still a signer and rejected after removal, and a Go test that fails the build if any of the six stops being exercised.
  - That test could not simply relocate the fixture's own entry to an earlier position: `after` is its index in the full log, so moving it earlier trips the ordering guard instead of the rule under test. It builds an equivalent entry at the position it would occupy — the same trap D-044 is about, met again from the other side.
  - **Phase 1 complete.** Go: 12 packages, 65 tests, 91-entry log, 239 vector files. `make ci`, staticcheck, `vectors-check` and the Python suite (157 tests) all green.


## Phase 2 — The log + CLI

The first phase with external dependencies. Ground rule 3 of `PLAN.md` is the
constraint that matters here: **reuse the transparency ecosystem, do not fork
it.** If an unmodified `transparency-dev/witness` cannot cosign a Norn
checkpoint, the design is wrong — so that is a gate line, not an aspiration.

| # | Checkpoint | Done when | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2.1 | Ecosystem cross-check | Go's canonical `note` implementation accepts our keys and receipt unchanged, in both directions | ✅ |
| 2.2 | `internal/tlog` | C2SP tile paths, checkpoints, and a tree with working inclusion and consistency proofs over the suite | ✅ |
| 2.3 | `internal/tlogstore` | a static tlog-tiles directory: entries appended, checkpoints issued, size monotonic, state recovered across restarts (D-049) | ✅ |
| 2.4 | `internal/logclient` | fetches checkpoint + bundles from a served directory and reproduces `state.json` byte for byte | ✅ |
| 2.5 | Tamper and restart | a flipped byte, a forged head, a missing bundle and a wrong anchor are each refused; a restart changes neither checkpoint nor state | ✅ |
| 2.6a | Checkpoint format accepted by the ecosystem | `transparency-dev/formats` — the package witnesses use — parses and verifies a Norn checkpoint unchanged | ✅ |
| 2.6b | A running witness cosigns | an **unmodified** `transparency-dev/witness` returns a cosignature over `add-checkpoint`. Needs a witness process, so it belongs to the lab | ⬜ Phase 4 |

| 2.9a | §16 enrollment requests, normative and vectored | the request format is specified byte-for-byte, the reference implements it, and §12.1's last "no vectors at all" row is gone | ✅ |
| 2.9b | Signing backends | two unrelated key stores behind one seam; split | ✅ |
