- **2026-09-19 · 2.1 ecosystem cross-check — the note encodings are the real thing, not merely self-consistent.** Since 0.5 I have been careful to say these were *only* self-consistent: my signer and my verifier agreeing proves I am consistent with myself and nothing more. `golang.org/x/mod/sumdb/note` is the canonical implementation the whole transparency ecosystem uses, and it now checks **both directions**:
  - it accepts the trust file's **log key and all three witness keys unchanged**;
  - it **opens the shipped `receipt.note`**, and the text it returns is byte-identical to what our own `Split` returns — so the signed-text boundary agrees, and that text still hashes to `receipt.json`'s `text_sha256`. That boundary is the one gate run 2 had to recover by hashing three candidates;
  - we verify what it signs, and **it opens what we sign**;
  - key-id derivation matches across 32 name/key combinations — it exposes the id as a `uint32` where we use four bytes, and they agree every time.
  - `PLAN.md` ground rule 3 is "reuse the transparency ecosystem, do not fork it". That was an intention; it is now a test. The unverified-claim note is removed from `SPEC.md` §7, which now states the cross-check instead.
  - **One dependency, three modules in the graph.** The stdlib-only discipline held long enough that the first external package is the one that proves interoperability.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.2 `internal/tlog`** — the C2SP tiled-log formats, tests first, green on the first run.
  - **The Merkle mathematics is `x/mod/sumdb/tlog`'s, not mine.** That is ground rule 3, and it is also simple risk management: a hand-rolled Merkle tree is exactly the kind of code that looks correct, passes every test its author thinks to write, and is subtly wrong. What this package adds is the part x/mod does *not* implement — the C2SP path layout, since x/mod uses the sumdb scheme instead.
  - The chunked path encoding is the classic interop bug in this format: `N` is written as 3-digit groups with `x` on every group but the last, so 1,234,067 is `x001/x234/067`. Tested at 0, 1, 255, 1000, 1234, 1234067, and with partial widths — wrong paths here look plausible and resolve to nothing.
  - `ParseCheckpoint` refuses anything but the three canonical lines: no trailing-newline omission, no empty origin, no leading zeroes or `+` in the size (Go's `ParseInt` accepts `+42` quite happily), no non-32-byte root, and **no extension lines**, which §6 forbids.
  - First time §6 and §8 touch real data: a tree over the suite's **91 entries**, with inclusion proofs that verify against the head, a **tampered leaf whose proof stops verifying**, and a consistency proof between sizes 20 and 91. All checked with upstream's verifier rather than my own, so agreement is not self-agreement.
  - Go: 13 packages, 74 tests, `make ci` + staticcheck + `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.3 `internal/tlogstore` — and a plan commitment revisited with numbers.** `PLAN.md` named Tessera for the write path and D-002 cited it as a reason to pick Go. Measured before committing: Tessera pulls **135 modules**, including Google Cloud Spanner, Storage, IAM, monitoring and trace, because it ships every driver. Norn's graph is **3**, and Norn's pitch is that it runs on a NAS and a Pi. **D-049** records the trade honestly — including the strongest argument *for* Tessera, that the dependency would land only in `norn-log` and never in `nornd` — and fixes a falsifiable upgrade trigger: adopt it when the log outgrows one directory, needs a second writer, or needs a cloud backend. Ground rule 3 is untouched, because it is about *formats* interoperating, and 2.6 tests that directly.
  - The store writes the layout, recovers from it, and serves it. Entry bundles are the authoritative record; tiles and the checkpoint are derived, so a torn write to either repairs itself. **The checkpoint is written last**, so a crash leaves a log whose advertised size is behind what is on disk, never ahead — the reverse would advertise entries a reader cannot fetch.
  - **Two real bugs, both caught by tests written first.** The partial-bundle path was built by asking for width 0, which produced `…000.p/0.p/*` — a plausible-looking path that matches nothing. And each append wrote a *new* partial file (`.p/1`, `.p/2`, …) while leaving its predecessors, so a reader globbing the directory found several with no rule for choosing, and picked the narrowest. **That is the orphan-file hazard again** — the one that left seven stale vectors in the suite and survived three conformance runs. Partials are now superseded rather than accumulated, with a test asserting no `.tmp` file survives a clean run.
  - Go: 14 packages, 80 tests, graph still **3 modules**.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.4 + 2.5 `internal/logclient` — the verifier loop, end to end.** Taken together because the verifier and its *refusals* are one idea: a verifier that reproduces the right state but accepts a tampered log has not verified anything.
  - **The headline test**: the fixture's own 91 entries are written into a real served tile directory, read back through the same `Fetcher` a node will use, and the state that comes out is **`state.json` byte for byte**. Until now replay was handed a list of entries; this is the first time they arrive the way they actually will — as bundles on a disk somebody else controls. §10 now has a real path through it.
  - **Order matters, and the code says why**: integrity is checked **before** replay. The served entries are rebuilt into a tree and required to hash to the head the checkpoint advertises; only then are they replayed. Replaying first and checking afterwards would mean acting on entries never shown to belong to the log — exactly the trust a transparency log exists to remove.
  - Four refusals, each a different lie a log operator can tell: a **forged head** (root replaced), a **tampered entry** (one byte flipped inside a bundle), a **missing bundle** (advertising entries it cannot serve), and a **wrong anchor** (which must abort with `bad-genesis` and yield no devices, never a populated state built on a genesis the anchor does not pin). Plus a restart that must change neither checkpoint nor state.
  - `Fetcher` is deliberately **one method wide**. Everything a verifier needs from a log is "give me the bytes at this path" — and keeping it that narrow is what will make peer gossip (`GOSSIP.md`) a different `Fetcher` rather than a second verifier.
  - Go: 15 packages, 86 tests, graph still 3 modules.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.6a — the ecosystem reads a Norn checkpoint unchanged.** Checkpoints are now signed notes, with the key name equal to the origin: the same binding §9.1 requires of the trust file's log key, so a checkpoint signed by a key named for another network cannot verify here even if that key is trusted elsewhere. `transparency-dev/formats/log` — the package witnesses actually use — parses ours and reads back the same origin, size and root; it rejects a wrong key, rejects an **origin mismatch** (a valid signature for a different log is still the wrong log), and rejects a checkpoint whose size line was altered. Our own verifier agrees with it in both directions.
  - **Cost, measured as before:** `formats` is **6 modules**; the full witness binary is **80**. The graph went 3 → 7. The witness's extra 73 are its HTTP handler and storage, which are the witness's business; what is Norn's business is whether the bytes are right, and that is what this proves.
  - **2.6 is explicitly NOT closed.** The gate says an unmodified witness *cosigns* a Norn checkpoint, and a cosignature round trip needs a witness **process** answering `add-checkpoint`. That is a lab item, and `GOSSIP.md` already places witness deployment in Phase 4. Marking 2.6 done on the format half would be exactly the partial credit this build log exists to refuse — so it is split: **2.6a ✅, 2.6b ⬜ Phase 4**, and the `PLAN.md` gate line says the same.
  - Go: 15 packages, 91 tests.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.7 — §6, §8 and §10 finally have vectors, and §12 admits where they came from.**
  - **§8 had no format.** It named `c2sp.org/tlog-proof@v1` and never said what the bytes were — the same gap class four gate runs kept finding. §8.1 now specifies a concrete bundle in deterministic CBOR (`norn/proof/v1`: device, index, **the entry itself**, the inclusion proof, the signed checkpoint) and says **explicitly that byte compatibility with `tlog-proof@v1` has not been checked**, with reconciliation as an open item. Claiming that compatibility is exactly the unverified assertion §7 carried until 2.1 tested it.
  - A bundle is self-contained and `Check` **never consults a log**, on purpose: evidence that only checks out while its source is reachable is not evidence. Refusals tested for a swapped entry (genuine proof, genuine checkpoint, wrong entry — the substitution the bundle exists to catch), a foreign checkpoint, a tampered proof hash, and the wrong log key.
  - `served/` is the fixture's 91 entries as a **real tlog-tiles directory** with a signed checkpoint; `proofs/` holds **six bundles, one per active device**, each proving the entry that made *that* device active rather than merely some entry in the log.
  - **§12 now states that the suite has two provenances.** Entry, state and trust vectors come from the Python reference and Go must match them; `served/` and `proofs/` come from Go, because producing a tiled log requires an implementation. Both are validated against libraries neither side wrote — `transparency-dev/formats` parses the checkpoint, `x/mod/sumdb/tlog` checks every proof. **Nothing in the suite is checked only against its own author**, and saying so is the point.
  - §12.1's "not vectored" list is down to **one row**: §6 cosignatures, which need a witness process — the same Phase 4 item as 2.6b.
  - Go: 17 packages, 99 tests.
  - **Caught immediately after committing 2.7:** `make vectors-check` broke, and it was right to. It diffed the whole tree against the Python reference, which does not produce `served/` or `proofs/` — so the reproducibility check and §12's two-provenance statement had drifted apart within one commit. Now each half is checked against **its own** generator: the reference half via `gen_vectors.py`, the Go half via `make vectors-log`. A check that cannot express the structure it guards is not a check.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.8 `norn verify` — and a stale worked example caught in the act.**
  - **HTTP**, tested against a stock `http.FileServer`, because that is what the gate's "served by plain nginx" actually demands of Norn: static files, **no server-side logic**. Reading the same log from disk and over HTTP must give the same state and the same checkpoint, and does. The fetcher bounds every response (a verifier fetches from a log it does not trust, so answering a request must not be enough to exhaust it) and refuses a path that escapes the log root **before sending it** — a verifier should not ask, and a proxy might honour it.
  - `norn verify` takes a trust anchor and **has no flag to skip verification**. It refuses without `-trust`, refuses an anchor that fails §9.1 validation, and refuses an unreachable log rather than reporting an empty network as healthy. The summary prints what was **rejected** as well as what was accepted: a tool that shows only the good news is the wrong shape for this job.
  - **The find:** the CLI printed anchor fingerprint `63Z3-D0ZE-76KT-T87Y` while `SPEC.md` quoted `ZVGR-QRTN-JY0V-H1Z0` in two places. Not a bug in the code — the *spec* was stale. Checkpoint **0.13b** gave genesis a non-default `mmd`, which changed the genesis entry, which changed `genesis_hash`, which lives in the trust file, so the fingerprint moved and the worked example silently became fiction. Exactly the D-045 failure, in the one place my citation test did not look. Fixed, and `internal/specdoc` now checks the fingerprint example too — **a worked example nobody verifies is worse than none, because a reader trusts it.**
  - Go: 18 packages, 106 tests.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9a §16 enrollment requests — the last "no vectors at all" row is gone, and three things were wrong on the way.** §16 was six lines of prose naming a format; it is now normative, implemented twice and bound by 26 vectors.
  - **What the format had to decide.** One `sig`, not §4.1's `sigs` array, because a request carries exactly one signature and the key that made it is *in the object* — there is no `kid` to resolve and no threshold to count. A preimage prefix that differs from §4.2's, so a device signature over a request can never be replayed as a signature over an entry; both implementations assert that directly rather than trusting the prose.
  - **And what it must not claim.** `ENROLLMENT.md` said `norn sign` means "a request that doesn't own its WG key is never even displayed". That is false: `wg_pk` is X25519 and §3 says it never signs, so the signature proves possession of the *device* key only. Fixed in both documents, with the bounded consequence spelled out (`dup-wg` catches a live key; an unheld key denies service to its own requester). **The same class as the §7 claim that stood unverified until 2.1** — a sentence that sounds like a guarantee and is not one.
  - **bech32m (D-050)**, HRP `norn`, checked against **BIP-350's own published vectors** in both implementations, plus **BIP-173's valid bech32 strings, which must all fail** — the two differ only in the checksum constant. The one rule Norn drops, the 90-character limit, is asserted *narrowly*: that single string must decode while every other invalid vector still fails, so the deviation cannot quietly widen. A negative vector re-encodes a real request under bech32's constant, so "we reject bech32" is tested with the same bytes rather than a made-up string.
  - **A live divergence, found by refactoring (D-051).** Extracting the shared §2/§4.0 field rules into `fields` on both sides exposed that the reference accepted a CBOR `true` wherever a uint was required — `isinstance(True, int)` is true in Python — while Go refused it. The two implementations disagreed about whether `{"expires": true}` is a valid `enroll` body, and **no vector covered it, so nothing failed.** Both refuse now, and `requests-bad/bool-expires` binds the next implementation. This is why the oracle gets refactored rather than admired.
  - **`make vectors` was destroying the Go half of the suite (D-052).** Every run deleted `served/` and `proofs/` — eleven files — because prune removes what the run did not write, and 2.7's two-provenance split had reached `vectors-check` without reaching the generator. Pruning is now scoped to declared `OWNED` paths, a path in neither half **stops the run**, and three tests pin the boundary. The orphan hazard keeps coming back from a new direction; this time the fix is a rule that fails loudly instead of a habit.
  - **A third stale worked number.** §12 said the replay fixture was **82 entries**; it has been **91** since 1.x. Same failure as 2.8's fingerprint, one table away. `internal/specdoc` now checks the count against `manifest.json` too — every number SPEC.md quotes from the suite is now either derived or guarded.
  - 26 request vectors: 4 positives (including a 64-byte `device_id` and non-ASCII labels) and **22 negatives across all four layers** — text, CBOR profile, field rules, and signature. Among them `forged-signature`, structurally perfect and signed by another key: without it a package that never calls Ed25519 passes the whole directory, which is exactly what gate run 4 found in the entry vectors.
  - Go: 22 packages, 149 tests. Python: 196 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck, both halves of `vectors-check` green; module graph still 7.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9b-i the signer seam and the ssh-agent — a hand-written client is only allowed if a real agent tests it.**
  - **The seam is a stdlib interface, not a Norn one (D-054).** `entry.Sign` now takes a `crypto.Signer`; `ed25519.PrivateKey` already satisfies it, so **every existing call site compiled unchanged** while a key held by a TKey or 1Password became usable for the first time. Ed25519 is enforced *at* the seam: a P-256 signer — Secure Enclave and TPM 2.0 offer exactly that, so someone will try it — is refused with an error rather than a panic, and a signer that returns the wrong number of bytes leaves the envelope untouched instead of writing a malformed `sigs` entry.
  - **Why the client is ours (D-053), with numbers.** `x/crypto/ssh/agent` measured at **five modules** (crypto, net, sys, term, text) plus the whole SSH transport, for a protocol Norn uses **four messages** of. Norn's graph is 7 and its pitch is a NAS and a Pi; same trade as D-049, and the graph is still 7 after this checkpoint.
  - **And why that is not the usual self-agreement trap.** The real objection to hand-writing a wire format is that the author tests it against their own fake and learns nothing. So the tests drive **OpenSSH's `ssh-agent`**: `ssh-keygen` makes an Ed25519 key, `ssh-add` loads it, and this package lists it, signs an entry through it, and the result verifies as an ordinary §4.2 signature — the same discipline as `transparency-dev/formats` parsing our checkpoints. `NORN_REQUIRE_SSH_AGENT=1` in CI turns "not installed" into a **failure**, so the real-agent tests cannot quietly stop running on every platform at once. The fake agent keeps the cases a real one will not perform on request: signing with the wrong key, announcing a gigabyte, answering with the wrong message type, claiming another algorithm.
  - **A signature from an agent is verified before it is used.** The agent is a separate process, sometimes another vendor's, sometimes hardware. If it hands back something that does not verify, that has to fail in the CLI rather than in somebody else's replay, where it would look like a Norn bug. The fake-agent test that proves it signs with a *different* key and expects a refusal.
  - Keys of other types in the same agent are **skipped, not refused**: the user's RSA login key sitting next to a Norn signer is the normal case, and failing there would make Norn unusable on a real workstation.
  - Go: 23 packages, 169 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green; module graph still **7**.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9b-ii the key file — the measurement inverted the plan, so the plan changed (D-055).** `ENROLLMENT.md` promised tier 0 as an "age-style scrypt" file. Implementing it found three things wrong with that, in ascending order of importance.
  - **The dependency measurement says the obvious answer is not the cheap one.** `x/crypto/scrypt` **alone** pulls the same **five modules** as the whole of `x/crypto/ssh` — the cost is x/crypto's own `go.mod`, not the package you import. "A small KDF" was never cheaper than "the entire OpenSSH key format"; it was the same price for less. Norn's graph is still **7** because neither was adopted.
  - **It breaks a promise the project makes in writing.** `ENROLLMENT.md` part 1 says Norn has "no key format to invent" — and an age-style envelope around an Ed25519 seed is a key format, owned forever. The OpenSSH private-key format already exists, so tier 0 is now literally the file `ssh-keygen` writes.
  - **And it does not fit the threat model.** The file exists because `nornd` signs `rotate` and self-`revoke` **at boot with nobody at the keyboard**; a passphrase would have to sit beside the key. So encrypted keys are **refused**, with an error naming the remedy (`ssh-add`), and a human's signer key belongs in an agent — where a passphrase-protected key ends up anyway, after one prompt.
  - **Tier 0 becomes tier 1 with no conversion, and that is a test rather than a claim:** `ssh-add` loads a key `norn` wrote into a **real agent**, and the agent then lists exactly that key. Both directions of the format are checked against the real tools — `ssh-keygen -y` reads what Norn writes, Norn reads what `ssh-keygen` writes — under D-053's rule: **hand-write wire formats, never crypto primitives.** Unencrypted openssh-key-v1 is pure framing; the encrypted kind needs bcrypt-pbkdf, and refusing it is what keeps the rule intact.
  - **Three copies of the public key travel inside that file, and they must agree.** A file whose halves disagree is damaged or crafted, and signing with it would put an unexpected identity into the log. Also refused: a key file other users can read (the error names the mode), a second `Write` over an existing key (overwriting one destroys the identity `rotate` and self-`revoke` depend on, and the log cannot give it back), and **every truncation of the blob, at every offset** — a key file is no more trusted an input than an agent socket.
  - `internal/sshwire` now owns the RFC 4251 §5 encoding both the agent and the key file speak, for the same reason `fields` owns the §2 rules: the second copy of "check this length before using it as a bound" is the one that is wrong.
  - Go: 25 packages, 183 tests; module graph still **7**. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9c `norn enroll` — the device side, where a format meets a person.**
  - **WireGuard keys are pinned to RFC 7748's own vectors.** Deriving a public key wrongly is the quietest possible bug: the `enroll` entry would be perfectly valid, every signature would verify, and the tunnel would simply never come up. The arithmetic is `crypto/ecdh`'s (stdlib, no dependency), and the tests check Alice's and Bob's keys from the RFC — `wg` is not installed here, so the specification is the oracle instead of the tool.
  - **stdout is the request and nothing else**, so `norn enroll … > request.txt` yields exactly the request; the summary, the guidance and the QR go to stderr, where they cannot corrupt that file. The QR is drawn only when stderr is a terminal, so a pipe never fills with escape codes.
  - **The command checks its own work before a human acts on it:** the request it just built is decoded and verified before printing. A request that does not verify is a bug here, and it must not leave the machine.
  - **The QR carries the uppercase form (D-056).** §16.2 allows uppercase because QR alphanumeric mode has no lowercase letters, and the test **measures** the saving rather than assuming it. An end-to-end test scans-by-proxy: the uppercase text must decode to the same request, or the camera path silently does not work.
  - **What Norn owns in a QR is the part the library cannot check** — the quiet zone and the light/dark mapping. A code with no quiet zone, or inverted, is a valid QR no scanner reads. `Matrix` is therefore separate from the drawing and checked module-for-module against `rsc.io/qr`'s own map, with the quiet zone asserted on all four sides and terminal colours written explicitly (half-blocks alone invert on a light-background terminal). **Honest limit:** no QR *decoder* exists on this machine, so nothing here proves a phone reads it — that is a `PLAN.md` Phase 2 gate line and it stays open.
  - The summary states plainly what the request does **not** prove: the WireGuard key is X25519, so nothing in the request shows the device holds it (§16.1). A tool that overstates its evidence is the failure this project keeps finding in its own prose.
  - A device enrolling for the first time has no trust file, so `-net` takes an origin (`norn/…`) or 32 hex digits, and `trust.NetFromOrigin` is the new inverse of `OriginFor`, pinned by a round-trip property. `-trust` works too for a device being re-enrolled, and **the two disagreeing is a refusal, not a guess**.
  - Go: 27 packages, 208 tests; module graph 7 → **8**. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

## Phase 3 — The enforcement agent

`ENFORCEMENT.md` part 1 is the whole idea: WireGuard decides whom to talk to in
exactly one place, the cryptokey routing table, and Norn enforces **there and
nowhere else**. No packet-path code, no handshake changes, no protocol of its
own — a rogue key is simply never installed, or is removed.

Every line of this phase's `PLAN.md` gate is a **measurement**: a p99 over a
thousand runs, two 24-hour soaks, a real NetBird server, a Tailscale netmap
event. So the code can be built and proven correct here, and the gate cannot
close outside the lab — the same shape as Phase 2, and worth saying before
starting rather than after.

| # | Checkpoint | Done when | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 3.1 | `internal/peertable` | the narrow seam Norn touches — peers in, peers out — with a fake that records what a real interface would have been told | ✅ |
| 3.2 | `internal/reconcile` | observe, owner and watchdog as **pure decisions** over (actual, desired): no I/O, so every case including the A1 swap attack is a unit test | ✅ |
| 3.3 | `internal/endpoint` | providers that supply endpoints and allowed-ips but are **untrusted for keys**: a key they name that is not proven is dropped and logged | ✅ |
| 3.4 | `/run/norn/proven.json` | the verifier→enforcer interface of `ENFORCEMENT.md` part 9, written atomically, with its schema pinned | ✅ |
| 3.5 | `nornd` | the daemon; split | ✅ |
| 3.5a | `internal/agent` | one verify-and-reconcile cycle, and the failure semantics of `ENFORCEMENT.md` part 8 — above all, **a failed verification never widens or empties the peer table** | ✅ |
| 3.5b | `internal/wgdev` | the real interface behind the same seam. **Moved ahead of the daemon:** a `nornd` with nothing to drive cannot be run, and the dependency question had to be answered before a binary depended on the answer | ✅ |
| 3.5c | `cmd/nornd` | the binary: config, signals, the poll loop, and **observe as the default** (D-005) | ✅ |
| 3.6 | Fight detection and quarantine | N evictions in W → `CONTROL_PLANE_FIGHT`; `quarantine` fails closed **by choice, never by default** | ✅ |
| 3.8 | Phase 3 gate audit | each line read against what is actually tested, and what the lab still owes | ✅ |

## Phase 4 — Witnesses, gossip, freshness

`GOSSIP.md` part 1 in one sentence: **witnesses guard against the log; gossip
guards against the witnesses.** Neither alone is enough, and together the only
undetectable attack is a log plus a witness quorum showing *every* node the
same false history — which is no longer a split view but a forgery of signed
entries, and that needs signer keys.

This phase also settles two debts the earlier phases wrote down rather than
hid: **§10 step 3**, the witness-policy check the verifier still does not do
(2.9e-i), and **`freshness: "unknown"`** in `proven.json` (3.4), which stays
unknown until something cosigns. It closes **2.6b** too — an unmodified
`transparency-dev/witness` cosigning a Norn checkpoint.

| # | Checkpoint | Done when | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4.1a | Cosignature keys | witness keys are **cosignature** keys (algorithm 0x04), not plain Ed25519 note keys — today Norn refuses the only kind a real witness publishes | ✅ |
| 4.1b | Policy parsing | `tlog-policy` parses to the real grammar, a quorum evaluates over a set of cosigners, and the trust file's `witnesses` and `policy` must agree | ✅ |
| 4.1c | The verifier enforces the quorum | cosigned vectors, and a checkpoint that does not satisfy the policy is **refused** — §10 step 3, finally | ✅ |
| 4.2a | `quorum_time` | the latest instant the policy is satisfied — the k-th newest cosignature generalised to groups, since a nested policy has no single k | ✅ |
| 4.2b | The freshness machine | `fresh → degraded → hard_stale` on **monotonic** age from witness time, in `proven.json` and `norn status` | ✅ |
| 4.2c | `CLOCK_SKEW` | the wall clock is compared to witness time and never decides anything — an alarm, not a state change (A11) | ✅ |
| 4.2d | `degraded` and `hard_stale` are **acted on** | the 5-minute alarm cadence, and the enforcer dropping every peer past S_hard — with the `nornd` flag that makes the state reachable at all | ✅ |
| 4.3a | `tlog-witness` client | one `add-checkpoint` request in the documented shape, every documented response told apart, and the cosignatures checked before they are believed | ✅ |
| 4.3b | The log pushes | consistency proofs, the `409` resize-and-retry, and `norn-log` serving what the witnesses return — the end of the self-witness crutch | ✅ |
| 4.4 | **2.6b**: a real witness cosigns | an **unmodified** `transparency-dev/witness` returns a cosignature over a Norn checkpoint, and our verifier accepts it | ✅ |
| 4.5a | A node holds what it verified | full replication (GOSSIP part 6): the checkpoint and every tile written down as it verifies, so a node has something to serve | ✅ |
| 4.5b | The gossip server | `/norn/checkpoint` and `/norn/tile/…` on the **WireGuard address only**, serving bytes rather than claims | ✅ |
| 4.5c | §10 against the stored head | a checkpoint must be **no smaller** than the one held, and must contain it — the step gossip makes reachable by anyone | ✅ |
| 4.5d | The gossip client | pull from `r` random **proven** peers and adopt a better checkpoint — trust is identical because the verification is the same code | ✅ |
| 4.5e | Degraded polls harder | every source every 10 s while degraded (deferred from 4.2d, where there was only one source) | ✅ |
| 4.6a | Fork evidence | the artifact and `norn verify-evidence`: two signed heads and a proof binding them, checkable offline by anyone holding `norn.trust` | ✅ |
| 4.6b | A forked node freezes | §11's four duties, including the one that outlives a restart: refuse every inconsistent checkpoint **forever**, until an operator resets | ✅ |
| 4.7a | Read a `.ots` proof | parse the format and walk the ops offline — a tampered checkpoint fails without asking anybody | ✅ |
| 4.7b | `norn verify --anchored` | the `anchors/` layout and the trust file's `anchor` table — what a node checks, offline, about a proof somebody else obtained | ✅ |
| 4.7c | Stamping | obtaining the proof: the calendar protocol, `anchors/<size>.ots` written by a monitor. Needs a network round trip, so it is a lab item (T8) | ✅ |
| 4.8 | Phase 4 gate audit | each line against what is actually tested, and what the lab still owes | ✅ |
| 4.9 | Evidence propagation | `GET /norn/evidence`: a node freezes on a fork **another node** proved, which is T1b and the one gate line with nothing behind it | ✅ |

## Phase 5 — Attack demos + monitor

Phase 5 has two halves and they are not alike. **`norn monitor` is software**,
and every rule in `LAB.md` §3 can be written and tested here. **The demos are a
lab**: `make lab-up`, containers, a patched Headscale and NetBird, a phone
receiving a push, and tapes rendered for a README. Nothing in this repository
can produce those, so the checkpoints below build the monitor and the gate
audit says plainly what the lab still owes.

The monitor is also the one component that is *allowed* to be wrong without
anyone being harmed: it never sits on the enforcement path (`LAB.md` §3). That
is why it can poll witnesses, hold a receipt inbox and talk to `ntfy` — none of
which a node may do.

| # | Checkpoint | Done when | ✔ |
|---|---|---|---|
| 5.1 | The rules engine | `LAB.md` §3's table as code: an observation in, a level and a sentence out, with the thresholds a homelab can change | ✅ |
| 5.2 | Watching a network | the log, the witnesses and each node's `/norn/checkpoint` and `/norn/evidence` — read-only, over the tunnel, as an enrolled peer | ✅ |
| 5.3 | The receipt inbox | a receipt whose deadline passed with no inclusion is **non-repudiable proof of A2**, and the monitor is what notices | ✅ |
| 5.4 | Outputs | stdout JSON always; `ntfy`, Home Assistant, Alertmanager and email optional and each failing alone | ✅ |
| 5.5 | The status page | read-only HTML on the tunnel address: sizes, ages, witness times, the device table and the last entries in plain words | ✅ |
| 5.6 | `norn monitor` | the command, its config, and one run that produces a notification per rule | ✅ |
| 5.7 | Phase 5 gate audit | each line against what is tested, and the demos named as the lab's | ✅ |
| 5.8 | `norn audit --by` | what a stolen signer key did: every entry a given key signed, since a given time — `ENROLLMENT.md` R2's first step | ✅ |

## Phase 6 — Spec v1 + publication

Phase 6 is three gate lines and two of them need **people**: somebody outside
this homelab running the lab and filing an issue, and somebody who has run at
least two of Tailnet Lock, Nebula and NetBird reviewing the comparison table.
Nothing here can manufacture either, and pretending otherwise would be the
exact failure the cold-model gates were invented to prevent.

The third — *"cold-model gate repeated on frozen spec: independent verifier
passes all vectors"* — is a **process**, and it is the one the project has run
three times already. It needs a reader with no memory of writing the thing,
which is the whole point.

So: freeze the spec honestly, make the vectors usable by somebody who does not
have this repository, write what a stranger needs to decide whether Norn is for
them, and then run the gate.

## Decision taken — 2.9d: an `enroll` needs a device signature the device cannot make

**Answered 2026-09-19: option C.** The loop resumed at 2.9d-i.

**Raised 2026-09-19, before writing a line of `norn sign`.** The loop stops here
by its own rule: this changes the normative protocol and regenerates the whole
vector suite, so it is not mine to decide quietly.

**The contradiction.** `SPEC.md` §4.3 requires an `enroll` entry to carry a
`device` signature, and §4.2 says every signature is over *the entry's*
preimage — an envelope whose `ts`, `after` and body the **signer** chooses.
`ENROLLMENT.md` part 3 has the device print one QR and go quiet: it is headless,
it never sees that envelope, and `norn sign` is supposed to produce a **full**
`enroll` entry in one step. Both documents cannot be right.

**Why four gate runs and 208 tests missed it.** The fixture holds *every* private
key, so it signs one envelope as the device and again as a signer:
`_signed(env, [(device, "device"), ("signer1", "signer")])`. That is a signature
no real participant could produce, and nothing in a conformance suite can tell
the difference — the entry is valid either way. **The oracle was able to forge
consent, so the protocol was never asked to provide it.** The same shape as the
signature-blind replay of gate run 4, one level up.

Only `enroll` is affected: `rotate` and self-`revoke` are built **by** the
device, so it signs those envelopes itself.

| # | Option | What it costs |
|---|---|---|
| A | **Round trip.** Device → request → signer builds the entry → entry back to the device → device signs → submit. §4.3 unchanged. | Needs a return channel to a headless Pi, which is the thing this design exists to avoid. Kills the Phase 2 gate line "device QR → `norn sign` → submit → active, under 3 minutes". |
| B | **The device builds the entry.** `norn enroll` emits an `enroll` envelope already signed by the device; the signer appends signer signatures, which is legal because the preimage excludes `sigs`. §4.3/§5.1 unchanged. | An `enroll` can then never carry `after` — the device cannot know the log size — so the §4.4 ordering guard is lost for exactly the entry type that creates an identity. The signer can only accept or reject the device's labels and expiry. §16's request format, specified and vectored in 2.9a, is replaced by a partial entry. |
| C | **Embed the request (recommended).** The `enroll` body becomes the request bytes verbatim; §5.1's device check becomes "the embedded request decodes, its signature verifies under its own `device_pk`, and its `net` matches the entry's". | Normative change to §4.3, §5.1 and §5.2's wording; every `enroll` vector, `state.json`, `served/` and `proofs/` regenerate; both implementations change. Two or three checkpoints. |

**Why C is the recommendation.** It is not a workaround for A — it is a
*stronger* guarantee. A device signature over the envelope proves the device saw
some envelope; an embedded request proves the device consented to **exactly**
this `device_id`, `device_pk`, `wg_pk`, labels and expiry, and the signer cannot
alter any of them without destroying the proof. Meanwhile `ts` and `after` stay
where they belong, with the signer, who is the only party that knows the log.
The device keeps one job — say what it wants and prove it holds its key — and
the signer keeps one job — decide, and place the entry in the log.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9d-i `enroll` carries the request (D-057) — the protocol change the blocked checkpoint asked for.**
  - **What was wrong.** §4.3 required a `device` signature on the `enroll` envelope, and that envelope is built by a **signer**, who chooses `ts` and `after`. The device is headless and one-way: it prints one QR and is never heard from again. The signature the spec demanded was one the device could not make.
  - **Why four gate runs and 208 tests missed it.** The fixture holds every private key, so it signed one envelope as the device and again as a signer. That is consent no real participant could give, and no conformance suite can tell it from the real thing — the entry is valid either way. **The oracle was able to forge consent, so the protocol was never asked to provide it.** The same shape as gate run 4's signature-blind replay, one level up: there the tests never exercised a signature, here they never exercised *who could produce one*.
  - **The fix is stronger than what it replaces.** A device signature over an envelope proves the device saw *some* envelope. The embedded request proves it consented to exactly this `device_id`, `device_pk`, `wg_pk`, labels and expiry — and a signer **cannot alter any of them** without destroying the proof, where before it could have restated them freely. `ts` and `after` stay with the signer, the only party that knows the log.
  - **Consequences taken rather than hidden.** The log is **90 entries, not 91**: §4.0's 23-byte map-key cap moved to `requests-bad/oversize-label-key`, because after this change **no entry body has a free-form map left**, and putting that key anywhere else would let a closed-schema check fire first and hide a missing cap. The field negatives (`bad-device_id-uppercase`, `bad-device_id-leading-dot`, `bad-pk-length`) now sit *inside* the request, wrapped in entries that are well formed and properly signed by two signers — so **an implementation treating `request` as opaque bytes accepts all three**, which is exactly the property they now test.
  - **The fixture stopped lying.** Every `enroll` in the suite now carries signer signatures only. Leaving the device-role signature in place would have kept working and kept the fiction.
  - **Three of my own guards fired during the change** and each was right: §12.1's citation test caught a vector name that had moved, the log-size guard caught §12 still saying 91, and staticcheck caught `labelsOf` going dead. The label rules themselves needed no edit at all — `fields` owns them, so labels moving from the entry to the request changed nothing about how they are validated. That is the D-051 extraction paying for itself two checkpoints later.
  - Go: 27 packages, 209 tests. Python: 196. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green; module graph 8.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9d-ii `norn sign` — the admin side, proved by the log rather than by the exit code.**
  - **The headline test runs the whole path and then asks the log.** `norn enroll` makes a request, `norn sign` signs it with one fixture signer, and that entry is **replayed into the 90-entry fixture log**, which must refuse it `threshold` — because `signer_thr` is 2 there. A second `norn sign` appends the other signature, and replay then shows the device **active at index 90**. A test that stopped at "the command exited 0" would have passed on an entry no log would take.
  - **A test found a real bug in the first draft.** Signing an *existing* partially-signed entry demanded the log size, but an entry that already carries signatures has its `after` fixed: rewriting it would change the preimage and **invalidate the signature already on it**. The size is now needed only when building a new entry, and the input's shape — bech32m request or CBOR entry — is what decides, because a signer pastes or scans whatever they were handed and should not have to say which it is.
  - **Every signature it makes, it verifies.** `norn sign` re-checks its own output before writing it, for the same reason `internal/sshagent` re-checks the agent's: a signature this command cannot verify is a bug here, and it must not reach a log where it becomes somebody else's replay failure.
  - **The summary is UX rule 1 made literal** — device, device-key kid, WireGuard key, every label, expiry, `after`, the entry hash, **and which key is about to sign, named by its file or its agent comment**. The first draft printed "signing as … (this key)", which is filler; a signer with two keys in an agent needs to know which one is being used.
  - **It says what it does not know.** With the log reachable it reports "2 of 2 signer signatures. Ready for `norn submit`"; with the log unreachable it says the threshold is **unknown here** rather than implying success. The log's own view is used only for advice — a `dup-id` or `dup-wg` warning before submitting, never a decision, because §6 says operator validation binds nobody.
  - **The agent path is tested end to end with a real `ssh-agent`**: a key file Norn wrote, loaded by `ssh-add`, selected automatically because it is the only one, used to sign, and the signature verifies inside the entry. That closes the loop across 2.9b-i, 2.9b-ii and this checkpoint.
  - **`-trust` is required and there is no flag to skip verification**, as in `norn verify`: signing means deciding for a network, and the anchor is what names it. A request for another network, or one whose bytes were altered, is refused before a human sees it (§16.1).
  - Go: 27 packages, 214 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9e-i the verifier checks the log's signature — a defect found by trying to build the next thing.**
  - **What was wrong.** `logclient.Verify` had never used `norn.trust.log_key`. It fetched an **unsigned** `checkpoint`, rebuilt the tree from the served entries, and required them to hash to the head *in that same file*. Internally consistent, signed by nobody. §10 step 2 says "verify log signature" in as many words, and `tlog.Verify` had existed since **2.6a, unwired**.
  - **Why the tamper tests missed it.** Every forgery 2.5 tried — a replaced root, a flipped byte, a missing bundle — is caught by recomputing the tree. None of them needed a signature to catch, so none of them noticed there was no signature check. The attack that *does* need one is a mirror, a gossip peer, or anyone on the transport serving a log whose head no operator ever signed. Without the check `log_key` was decoration, and §11's fork evidence had nothing to point at: two conflicting **signed** checkpoints is what makes a fork non-repudiable.
  - **How I found it.** Not by reading the code — by asking what `norn-log` would have to *write* at `checkpoint` for `norn submit` to trust a receipt about it. The layout answered: an unsigned file plus a `checkpoint.signed` sibling, which is not what §6 describes. Building the next thing is a better spec review than reading.
  - **The fix removes the foot-gun rather than papering over it.** `tlogstore.Open` takes the signing key and writes the signed note **at `checkpoint`**; there is no unsigned sibling any more. A file a verifier is not allowed to believe should not exist where somebody might read it by mistake. `logclient.Anchor` carries verifier keys — plural, because a `log_key` rotation makes old and new both valid for `overlap` — and an anchor with no key is **refused**, not quietly skipped.
  - Four new negatives, each a log whose entries still hash to its head so that **only the signature separates it from the real one**: unsigned, signed by another key, signed under another origin's name, and an anchor with nothing to check against.
  - **Said out loud in §10 itself:** the witness-policy check is still not implemented. It needs a cosignature, which needs a running witness — Phase 4. Norn verifies *who wrote* the head, not that the head was shown to anybody else, and the spec now says so at the step rather than in a footnote.
  - **A gap in my own subdivision, too:** `cmd/norn-log` is in `PLAN.md`'s file index and its Phase 2 description, and this table never had a row for it. Added as 2.9e-ii.
  - Go: 27 packages, 218 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9e-ii `norn-log` — the one thing a file server cannot do.**
  - **The read side stays files.** Tiles, bundles and the signed checkpoint are served by `http.FileServer` here and by nginx in the lab; the binary exists for `POST /add` (§7) and because running one process is easier than two. That keeps `PLAN.md`'s gate honest: the verifier's path never needs this program.
  - **`internal/logserver` is the handler, `cmd/norn-log` is the wiring.** The decision procedure reads as §7 describes it, and the HTTP is around it rather than through it.
  - **The courtesy check says it is a courtesy.** A submission the log's own replay would reject gets `409` **with the reason code and the word "advisory"** in the body, because §6 says a verifier must not rely on this and an implementer reading a 409 should not be able to mistake it for authority.
  - **Re-submitting is not an error.** A client whose connection dropped after the append holds an entry in the log and no receipt; answering `dup-entry` would be true and useless. The same bytes get the same treatment and a fresh receipt, and the log does not grow.
  - **The deadline comes from the network, not from a flag.** `mmd` is read from the log's own replayed state (§4.5), because the length of the promise a receipt makes is a protocol parameter — a log using its own number would be promising something the network never agreed to.
  - **The wrong key is refused at startup**, not at the first submission: §9.1 binds the log key's note name to the origin, so a key the trust file does not name would sign checkpoints nobody on that network can verify.
  - **Concurrency is a test, not a comment.** Eight simultaneous submissions all land, the size is exactly right, and the log still verifies end to end — the store appends to a tree *and* rewrites files, so two writers would interleave into something that is not a log.
  - **A slip worth keeping.** A test helper read the manifest into a field named `GenesisHash` with no JSON tag, and Go matches keys case-insensitively **without ignoring underscores** — so `genesis_hash` never arrived and the anchor pinned nothing. Replay then aborted every entry with `bad-genesis` and returned a state with no devices and **no error**, which reads exactly like a healthy empty network. `logclient.Verify` now refuses an anchor with no genesis pin, so the next person loses a minute instead of an afternoon.
  - The end-to-end test runs **two binaries over HTTP**: `norn-log` serving, an entry submitted, and `norn verify` reading the result back and naming the new device. That is the lab's shape, minus the lab.
  - Go: 29 packages, 230 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9e-iii `norn submit` — and the whole of Phase 2 in one test.**
  - **The headline test is four programs' worth of protocol.** `norn enroll` on a device, `norn sign` twice with two different signer keys, `norn submit` to a running **`norn-log`**, and then `norn verify` — a separate process reading over HTTP — reporting the device active. Nothing in that chain is a double: the log is the real binary, the signatures are real, and the receipt is a real signed note.
  - **A receipt is evidence, so `internal/receipt` treats it like evidence.** It must verify under the anchor's log key, name this origin, and **bind the leaf that was actually submitted** — a receipt for someone else's entry verifies perfectly and promises nothing about yours. A deadline already in the past is refused too: a promise about the past promises nothing.
  - **The receipt is written to disk before the wait begins**, because §7 says keep it until inclusion is verified and a process that dies mid-wait must not take the evidence with it. An **unusable** receipt is never written — a file that looks like evidence and is not is worse than no file.
  - **Waiting re-verifies the whole log every time**, signature and tree and replay. Inclusion in a log that no longer checks out is not inclusion, and a poll that only looked for the leaf would be exactly that mistake.
  - **When the deadline passes without inclusion, the tool says what it has**: the receipt is now proof of log misbehaviour (A2), and the message says to keep it. That is the one moment this project's thesis becomes a CLI message.
  - §7's status codes are translated rather than echoed: a `409` is reported **as the log's own opinion** ("advisory, §6"), because a verifier would not share it and a submitter should not mistake it for a verdict.
  - `logclient.Submit` stands apart from `Fetcher` on purpose. Reading a log needs only "give me the bytes at this path", and keeping that interface narrow is what will let a peer serve a log over gossip without also being able to accept submissions.
  - `Verify` now returns the entries it served. A caller that has just proven a set of bytes belongs to a head should not have to ask for them again.
  - **Gate audit still owed.** Several Phase 2 gate lines look true now — the tamper test, the restart, the `note` cross-check — but "looks true" is how partial credit gets marked as PASS. They get read one at a time against what was actually tested, after 2.9g.
  - Go: 29 packages, 239 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9f-i `internal/genesis` — refusing networks that cannot work, and one gap closed on the way.**
  - **Why a package and not CLI code.** The two artefacts a network starts from must agree: `norn.trust.genesis_hash` is the leaf hash of *that exact entry* (§9.1). And a network built wrong is not something anyone notices later — a root set the thresholds cannot satisfy, a log key named for another origin, a quorum over witnesses the file never lists. Each produces something that looks finished and can never accept a checkpoint.
  - **Build checks its own work by replaying it.** The entry it just made, under the anchor it just made, must replay clean. Same move as `norn enroll` verifying its own request and `norn sign` verifying its own signature: the cheapest possible proof that what left the function is what the rest of the system will accept.
  - **Fifteen ways to describe a network that could not work**, each refused with the reason: a threshold above its set, a key in both roles, a duplicated root, no signer at all (no device could ever enrol), a quorum larger than the witness set. Two more are about *who signs*: one signature short of `root_thr`, and a stranger's signature that does not count however many there are.
  - **The log key is derived, not accepted.** §9.1 binds its note name to the origin, and the origin is generated in the same call — so a caller cannot get that wrong by construction rather than by validation.
  - **A gap found and closed: §9.2's TOML rendering did not exist in Go.** The normative human form was implemented only in the Python reference, so `trust.toml` was a file one implementation produced and the other could not read back or write. Go renders it now, and the **reference's own vector is the oracle** — byte for byte, first run. The escape table is TOML's, not canonical JSON's (§14.2): they agree on most of it and not all, and conflating them is how a policy with a tab in it stops round-tripping.
  - **Said plainly rather than implied:** the generated policy follows the shape the suite has used since Phase 0, and byte compatibility with the published `tlog-policy` grammar is **not** claimed — same caveat §8.1 carries for proof bundles, and it is checked against a real witness in Phase 4 or not at all. Paper roots stay out: turning 24 words into a seed needs a wordlist decision, and inventing one quietly would be worse than leaving tier 3 unimplemented and saying so.
  - Go: 30 packages, 251 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9f-ii `norn init` — a network from nothing, and a device inside it.**
  - **The test that matters uses no fixture, no vectors and no key that existed before it started.** `norn init` makes the network, `norn-log` starts on an **empty directory**, the genesis entry is submitted like any other entry — it is entry 0, not a special case — a device enrols, the signer signs, and a verifier holding only `norn.trust` reports the device active in a two-entry log. If that passes, Norn can be **adopted** rather than merely inspected.
  - **A root that is not in the room still joins the network.** `-root FILE` both joins the set and signs; `-root-pub HEX` joins without signing. That is how a paper or hardware root becomes part of a 2-of-3 while sitting in an envelope, and the bootstrap test uses exactly that shape.
  - **Key files are created when absent, and the summary says which** — the same behaviour as `norn enroll`'s device key. A mistyped path then shows up as an unexpected "created" line rather than as a network you cannot sign for.
  - **The fingerprint is the loudest thing on screen**, with one sentence saying why: every node compares it out of band before trusting the file, and it is the only thing in Norn taken on human authority. A ceremony that buried it would be the wrong shape of tool.
  - **A second `norn init` in the same directory is refused**, because initialising over an existing network would strand every node already holding the old trust file — and the test checks the original file is still byte-identical afterwards.
  - **The two files it writes have to agree**, and a test checks it directly: `norn.trust.genesis_hash` is the leaf hash of `genesis.cbor`, and that entry replays clean under that anchor. Nothing else in the system would catch a mismatch — a node would simply refuse every log forever.
  - Seven refusals, each before anything is written: no name, no log URL, no roots, no signers, no witnesses, a root threshold nobody can meet, a malformed witness.
  - Go: 30 packages, 255 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.9g `norn status` — and the one number it refuses to print.**
  - **Freshness is reported as UNKNOWN, with the reason on screen.** `GOSSIP.md` part 5 is explicit: **witness time is the clock**, because a node's own wall clock is untrusted (A11). A `tlog-checkpoint` carries no timestamp of its own, so until a witness cosigns, "how old is this view" *has no answer*. Printing the local clock's opinion would invent precisely the number the whole freshness design exists to avoid inventing — so `status` says it cannot compute fresh/degraded/hard-stale at all, and says why. A test asserts it never claims otherwise.
  - **`internal/plainlog` reads the log back in words** (UX rule 5). An operator who cannot read their own log will not notice the entry they did not expect, and an audit trail nobody reads is one that does not work. A property test requires **every type in the closed set** to get a real sentence — the fallback is allowed to fire only for the suite's deliberate `unknown-type` negative, which it must still describe truthfully.
  - **No invented identities.** A log binds keys, not people, so entries read as "signer 83eba1f8 enrolled pi-cam". Putting a friendly name on a key would present an identity nobody vouched for; a test looks for exactly that mistake.
  - **Refused entries are marked as refused** in the readback. Showing an entry that never took effect as though it had would make the plain-words view worse than no view.
  - **The find: the suite's two halves disagreed about the log key.** `norn-genlog` signed `served/checkpoint` with a seed of its own while the shipped `trust.cbor` named the reference's `logkey` — so `norn verify -trust testdata/vectors/trust.cbor -log testdata/vectors/served` **failed**, and had been wrong for six checkpoints. **Nothing could have noticed before 2.9e-i**, because until then no verifier checked a checkpoint signature. The generator now reads the key out of `keys.json`, and a new test requires the served checkpoint *and every proof bundle* to verify under the shipped anchor's log key. A suite whose two provenances disagree is a suite a second implementation cannot follow.
  - Go: 31 packages, 268 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 2.10 + 2.11 the Phase 2 gate audit, and the line it turned out was false.**
  - **The audit found a gate line that had been quietly untrue.** "Flip one byte in a tile → the verifier refuses." It did not. `logclient.Verify` fetched the checkpoint and the entry bundles and **never fetched a hash tile at all** — I confirmed it by flipping a byte in `tile/0/000.p/90` and watching `norn verify` report a healthy log. Rebuilding the tree from bundles is *stronger* than trusting tiles, which is why this looked defensible and why nobody noticed; but it leaves a log free to serve corrupt tiles to **every monitor, witness and client that fetches proofs**, while the one program guaranteed to be looking says nothing.
  - **Fixed in 2.10, and the fix put the computation in one place.** `tlog.Tree.Tiles()` now says what tiles a tree implies; the store publishes exactly those, and the verifier checks exactly those. A reader and a writer that each work out "what belongs in tile 3" separately are two implementations of one rule, and the second one is the one that is wrong — the same lesson as `fields` (D-051) and `sshwire`.
  - **Three of seven gate lines are now ticked, and four are not.** The tamper line (closed by 2.10), the restart line, and the `note`/torchwood cross-check. Left open: nginx has literally never run — only Go's static file server, which proves the *property* the line is about but is not what the line says; the witness cosignature (2.6b, Phase 4); the phone-camera enrolment, whose software half runs end to end and whose physical half — a camera, a hardware key's touch, a stopwatch — is untested; and the runbooks.
  - **The restart line needed one more test before it was true.** Reopening an untouched log and getting the same checkpoint proves nothing was lost. It does not prove the log **keeps counting**: a store that recovered its entries but restarted its size would publish a checkpoint claiming fewer entries than it serves. Now tested — the entry after a restart lands at the index the old checkpoint ended on.
  - **Each open line now says precisely what is true and what is missing**, in `PLAN.md` itself. "Half done" with the halves named is worth more than an unticked box, and much more than a ticked one.
  - Go: 31 packages, 272 tests. `make ci`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **2026-09-19 · 3.1 `internal/peertable` — the seam, and why it is this narrow.**
  - **It exposes no private key, no listen port and no fwmark.** `nornd` never writes those, and an interface it cannot express is one it cannot break by accident. The three writes are exactly the three `ENFORCEMENT.md` part 2 names: replace the table (owner), remove a key (watchdog), refresh endpoints without adding keys (UpdateOnly).
  - **It names no implementation, and that is the point.** The enforcement decisions worth testing are the ones that only happen *while an attacker is writing to the table at the same time*. Against a kernel those are slow and flaky; against this seam they are function calls, so the reconciler's whole behaviour — including the A1 swap — becomes unit-testable in 3.2.
  - **The fake records what the interface was told, not just what it ended up holding.** "Removed the rogue key" and "rewrote the whole table" leave the same table and are different acts: one is watchdog mode and one is owner mode, and a test that could not tell them apart would let either pass for the other.
  - **`Inject` is how a test plays the other writer** — a key appears without Norn having asked for it, which is the entire situation watchdog mode exists for. It deliberately does not count as a write Norn made, so "did observe mode touch the interface" stays answerable.
  - **Routing and reachability are separate comparisons.** The key and its allowed-ips decide *who may send what*; the endpoint and keepalive are what an endpoint provider supplies and may legitimately change. Conflating them would make a roaming peer look like a security event — so `SameRouting` ignores the endpoint and `SameEndpoint` is its own question.
  - `UpdateEndpoints` **refuses to add an absent key**, in the fake as well as in the contract. A fake that quietly added one would hide the exact bug a kernel would have caught.
  - Go: 32 packages, 280 tests; `make ci` and staticcheck green. Still no new dependency: keys are bytes and addresses are `net/netip`.
- **3.2 `internal/reconcile` — the attack cases are unit tests, and three rules the design had not written down.**
  - **Decide touches nothing; Apply decides nothing.** The behaviour worth testing is what happens *while somebody else is writing to the table*, and against a kernel those cases are slow, racy and hard to arrange. As pure functions they are ordinary tests — `ENFORCEMENT.md`'s **A1 swap attack** is a paragraph in the design and a test case here: owner mode evicts the attacker and restores the device, watchdog evicts the attacker and **deliberately does not install** the device, because availability belongs to whoever owns the table (G4 non-goal). Saying that in a test keeps it a decision rather than an omission.
  - **Owner mode alarms on what it evicts.** The pseudocode only reports in the observe branch, so a `ReplacePeers` would have corrected an intrusion *silently*. The attempt is the thing worth knowing about; correcting it quietly is how an operator never learns their control plane is compromised. `ENFORCEMENT.md` now says so.
  - **A rewrite keeps an endpoint the interface has learned** where the provider supplies none. This one is not a refinement, it is a bug I would have shipped: WireGuard learns a roaming peer's endpoint from its handshakes and it exists nowhere else, so rewriting the table with a zero endpoint would take that peer **offline until it happened to handshake again** — an outage caused by enforcement, on a peer nobody doubted. Tested directly.
  - **An endpoint change is a refresh, not a rewrite.** `actual ≠ desired` means the keys or their allowed-ips differ; a peer that roams is not a security event, and owner mode that rewrote the table every poll would be untestable against the gate's 24-hour zero-divergence diff.
  - **The hijack that keeps a proven key** is covered too: the control plane leaves the key and widens its allowed-ips to `0.0.0.0/0`. Same key, entirely different authority — so `SameRouting` is what owner mode compares, and the rewrite restores the proven routes.
  - **An unknown mode is `Invalid`, not a quiet no-op** — which would look exactly like a healthy observe run.
  - Go: 33 packages, 293 tests; `go test` and staticcheck green.
- **3.3 `internal/endpoint` — "untrusted for keys" made structural rather than checked.**
  - **`Hints` has no key field.** A provider cannot name a peer at all, so there is nothing to validate: `Desired` walks the **proven** set and asks the provider to decorate each device. `ENFORCEMENT.md` part 5 says a control plane is demoted to an endpoint directory; this is what makes that true by construction instead of by a check somebody could forget. The test is named for the property — *a provider cannot add a peer* — and the `file` provider, the escape hatch any control plane can write to, gets the same test again.
  - **What a provider says about devices the log does not prove is reported, not ignored.** A control plane quietly trying to add peers is precisely the event worth seeing, so those ids come back in the report by name.
  - **A proven device nobody configured is still installed, and reported as unrouted.** Dropping it would be enforcement removing a peer the log vouches for — the failure mode that turns a security tool into an outage.
  - **A typo in a signed label must not evict a peer.** Labels are signed by a signer, which makes them authenticated but not authoritative: they say where a device is expected to be, not who it is. So a malformed `endpoint` is reported and the device keeps the routes that did parse, rather than the whole device failing over one bad field.
  - **Hints files are keyed by `device_id`, not by public key**, and that is the point: identity comes from the log, so an adapter has to map its control plane's idea of a peer onto a device the log proves. Doing that mapping in the adapter is what keeps the control plane out of the business of naming keys.
  - No TOML dependency yet: `Static` takes a map, so the `nornd.toml` decision belongs to the daemon that parses it (3.5), not to this package.
  - Go: 34 packages, 306 tests; `go test` and staticcheck green.
- **3.4 `internal/proven` — the file everything else reads, and the boolean it refuses to be.**
  - **In `nornd` the verifier and the enforcer share a process, so this file is not how they talk** — it is how the monitor, Home Assistant, a support bundle and an operator with `jq` find out. That makes it an output to be as careful with as a protocol message: a number here that nobody can justify is a number somebody will act on.
  - **`freshness` is a state, not a boolean.** Part 9 specified `"degraded": false`; that asserts all is well, and with no witness cosignature a node has no clock to know it with (A11). The field is now `fresh | degraded | hard_stale | **unknown**`, `cosigned_at` is `null` until a witness signs, and the local clock is named `written_at_local_clock` so nobody mistakes it for evidence. A test asserts the word `degraded` never appears as a boolean.
  - **Every `proof` names a bundle that is actually written**, and each is checked in the test under the trust file's log key — checkable by someone holding only `norn.trust`, which is the whole point of §8. A `proof` field pointing at nothing would be worse than no field: an alarm would cite evidence that does not exist.
  - **"Proven" now has one definition.** Active and proven differ by exactly an expiry check, and `norn status` had its own copy. Two copies of that difference is how an expired contractor's laptop stays in somebody's peer table, so `proven.IsProven` is the only one and status uses it.
  - **The file is replaced atomically, and a test races 200 reads against 50 rewrites.** It is rewritten every `T_poll` forever while something else reads it; a reader that caught it half-written would see a peer set that never existed.
  - `logclient.Result` now keeps the **signed** checkpoint, not just the parsed one. A parsed checkpoint is a fact; the note is the evidence for it, and a bundle has to carry evidence.
  - Go: 35 packages, 313 tests; `go test` and staticcheck green.
- **3.5a `internal/agent` — the cycle, and the failures it is not allowed to turn into outages.**
  - **The cycle is a method, not a loop.** What is worth testing is what happens when the log is unreachable, when it serves a tampered checkpoint, when somebody else is writing to the table — and as a function call each of those is a test rather than a race against a timer.
  - **A log going down is an outage of Norn's ability to learn new facts. It is not a reason to disconnect a network.** Tested in all three modes: an unreachable log leaves the peer table byte-for-byte as it was and writes **nothing**. The tempting bug is owner mode "reconciling" against an empty proven set and calling `ReplacePeers` with it — a total outage, caused by the security tool, protecting nobody.
  - **And an intruder must not be able to wait for the log to go down.** Once something has verified, a later failure keeps enforcing *those* facts: the log is deleted mid-test, a rogue key is injected, and watchdog mode still evicts it. That the facts are older than they look is said out loud (`ENFORCING_ON_STALE_STATE`) rather than left for an operator to infer.
  - **A tampered checkpoint is not a log outage — it is worse — and the table survives it too.** Appending a line to the signed note makes verification fail; the interface is untouched.
  - **A failed cycle does not republish `proven.json`.** A stale file would be worse than none, because it would look current.
  - **Observe is the default when no mode is set** (D-005), and a missing or unusable trust file means **refuse to start** in every mode — a node that started without an anchor would enforce nothing while looking like it worked.
  - Everything the agent touches is an interface, so these tests substitute a directory for a log and a fake for a kernel. Not one of them needs a WireGuard device.
  - Go: 36 packages, 325 tests; `go test` and staticcheck green.
- **3.5b `internal/wgdev` — the kernel, paid for once and fenced in (D-059).**
  - **Reordered before the daemon on purpose:** a `nornd` with nothing to drive cannot be run, and the dependency question had to be answered before a binary depended on the answer.
  - **The number is bad: 16 new modules**, including gvisor and wintun — wgctrl requires 18, five of which were already present, so the graph goes **8 → 24**. It lands in the component that runs on a Pi. Taken anyway, because the alternative is hand-rolling **generic netlink** — family resolution, nested attributes, dumps — on a machine with no netlink to test against. That is the neighbour of D-053's rule: hand-write wire formats, never crypto primitives, and **never a kernel protocol you cannot test**. A mis-parsed peer list is a security failure, not a formatting bug.
  - **What makes it affordable is the fence, and the fence is tested.** `internal/depgraph` is an architecture test: `cmd/norn`, `cmd/norn-log`, `internal/reconcile`, `internal/agent`, `internal/peertable` and `internal/endpoint` must not reach a kernel driver. So the CLI stays light, the log server stays small, and **every enforcement decision including the A1 swap attack remains a unit test** rather than a lab run. It also pins the verifier: a verifier needs a log and a trust anchor and nothing else.
  - **The package is almost entirely conversion, and that is where the bugs live.** Two wgctrl fields are pointers where nil means *leave it alone* — so "the provider gave no endpoint" now reaches the kernel as **nil**, not as a zero endpoint that would take a roaming peer offline. The 3.2 property finally meets the layer that could break it, and is tested there too.
  - **Allowed-ips are set exactly, never added to** (`ReplaceAllowedIPs`), or a route Norn did not put there would survive a rewrite. A refresh carries **no routes at all** — `UpdateOnly` with the allowed-ips deliberately empty — because refreshing reachability must not touch authority.
  - A v4 endpoint arrives from the kernel as a 16-byte mapped address and must come back as `192.0.2.1:51820`, not `::ffff:…`. Tested, because a peer table where one address is spelled two ways is a peer table that never converges.
  - Go: 38 packages, 336 tests; module graph **24**. `go test` and staticcheck green.
- **3.5c `cmd/nornd` — a thin binary, and the defaults that are load-bearing.**
  - **The mode is announced before anything can fail.** An operator reading a startup error should already be able to see which mode was intended, because *"it did not start"* and *"it started enforcing"* are very different things to be wrong about. Observe also says what it will **not** do — "this node will alarm and write nothing" — and a test asserts owner mode never prints that line.
  - **A near miss is an error, not a fallback.** `-mode enforce`, `-mode Owner`, `-mode watch` are all refused. A misspelling that quietly resolved to observe would be a node not enforcing while its operator believed it was, which is the worst of the three outcomes and the easiest to ship.
  - **Unknown settings in `nornd.toml` stop the daemon.** A misspelled `mode` that was shrugged off is the same failure through a different door, and `[[peer]]` blocks are rejected outright if they try to carry a `public_key` — keys come from the log, and there is a test named for it.
  - **A failed cycle logs and the loop continues.** A daemon that exited on a log outage would stop evicting intruders at exactly the moment somebody might be counting on it; 3.5a already guaranteed the peer table survives such a cycle untouched.
  - **The poll interval is bounded at both ends.** Zero is a busy loop, and a very long one is the eviction window an intruder lives inside — `ENFORCEMENT.md` part 2 calls 200 ms free, and that is the default.
  - **The binary stays thin on purpose**, so its tests are about wiring and refusals while the behaviour lives in `internal/agent`, tested against a fake interface. Adding `BurntSushi/toml` for `nornd.toml` cost **one module with no transitive dependencies** (graph 24 → 25), and `internal/depgraph` still holds the line that `cmd/norn` and `cmd/norn-log` carry neither it nor a kernel driver.
  - Go: 38 packages, 347 tests; `go test` and staticcheck green; module graph 25.
- **3.6 fight detection — telling a misconfiguration apart from a disagreement.**
  - **One eviction at a time, the two look identical.** A control plane that keeps putting the same key back is not misconfigured; it *disagrees* with the log about who belongs on the network, and an operator needs to be told that in those words. Three evictions in sixty seconds is the line (`ENFORCEMENT.md` part 3), and the message names the key, the count and the window rather than quoting a default that may not apply.
  - **A fight is declared once, not every poll.** A caller that takes an interface down on the signal must not do it five times a second. A run that dies down lapses, so a fresh run is a fresh fight — and Norn **keeps evicting throughout**: alarming is not giving up.
  - **A failed eviction is not evidence.** If the interface refuses the write, the key has not "come back" — counting it would blame the control plane for Norn's own inability to write. Evictions are recorded only after they succeed, and there is a test that runs five failing cycles and expects silence.
  - **Quarantine never happens by default.** `alarm` keeps the network up; `quarantine` takes the interface out of service until a human intervenes. Failing closed is a **choice**, and an agent configured to quarantine with no way to do it is **refused at startup** — finding that out mid-incident is too late. The daemon also warns at boot when the policy is armed.
  - **Quarantine runs a command an operator can read.** `ip link set {iface} down` on Linux, and nothing assumed anywhere else — taking a network down is a break-glass action, so the exact argv is in the config, in the logs, and replaceable with `wg-quick down` or a firewall rule. On a platform with no safe default, Norn says what to configure instead of guessing.
  - **The detector is bounded.** `nornd` runs for months on a Pi and an attacker can mint keys all day, so quiet keys are forgotten and the map is capped at 4 096 — tested by minting more than that.
  - Go: 39 packages, 364 tests; `go test` and staticcheck green.
- **3.8 the Phase 3 gate audit — no line ticked, and one that was never tested.**
  - **Every line of this gate is a measurement**: a p99 over a thousand runs, two 24-hour soaks, a real NetBird server, a Tailscale netmap event. None can be ticked here, and saying that is the honest outcome rather than a disappointment. What the audit is *for* is the other half: stating precisely what is proven in software, and finding the lines whose substance was never tested at all.
  - **One was.** "A `Revoke` entry drops the device within one checkpoint interval." Every enforcement test so far started from a **fixed** proven set — so nothing anywhere showed the peer table *following the log when the log changed underneath it*. The timing belongs to the lab; whether it happens at all did not, and was untested. It is now: a real `revoke`, signed by the fixture's two signers, appended to a real log between two cycles, in **both** enforcing modes. It passed first try — the behaviour was right, the confidence was unearned.
  - **Three more software halves, written rather than assumed.** Fifty unchanged cycles must produce **zero writes** after the first, because a daemon that rewrites the table every poll could never pass a continuous 24-hour diff — that is what the soak would really be measuring. Twenty-five observe cycles, each with a fresh intruder, must produce **zero writes and an alarm every time**. And an injected key must be gone after **exactly one cycle**, which is what makes the eviction window equal to `T_poll` instead of some multiple of it.
  - **One line is not started, and says so.** Tailscale observe needs a LocalAPI watcher: `tailscaled` exposes no UAPI socket, so it is a different *source of truth* feeding the same reconciler, not another backend behind `peertable.Device`. It stays unwritten rather than half-written.
  - Every open line now names what is true and what is missing, in `PLAN.md` itself. Two phases in a row have ended this way, and the pattern is worth naming: **a gate made of measurements cannot be closed by a test suite, but it can be made smaller by one.**
  - Go: 39 packages, 368 tests; `go test` and staticcheck green.
- **4.1a cosignature keys — Norn could not have accepted a real witness, and found out by reading the spec first.**
  - **The defect, in three parts.** `c2sp.org/tlog-cosignature` says a witness key is algorithm **0x04**, and the byte is part of the key id — so the same public key under 0x01 and 0x04 is **two different verifiers**. Norn encoded witness keys as 0x01 (both implementations); `ParseVerifierKey` **refused** anything that was not 0x01, so `trust.Validate` would have **rejected a trust file naming a real witness**; and `note.Split` required a 68-byte signature line, so a cosigned checkpoint — which §6 says a checkpoint *is* — **would not have parsed at all**. Gate line 4.4 was not merely unimplemented, it was unreachable.
  - **Found before writing code, by fetching the format instead of recalling it.** Same habit as BIP-350 and RFC 7748: the spec named the algorithm byte, and the byte did not match what the project shipped.
  - **The cross-check was green because both sides were wrong the same way.** 2.1's ecosystem test required `x/mod/sumdb/note` to accept *every* key in the trust file, witnesses included — and sumdb only knows note keys. It passed because Norn was handing it note keys where witness keys belonged. It now splits honestly: the **log key** is checked against sumdb, the **witness keys** against `transparency-dev/formats/note`, which is what a real witness uses — and sumdb must **reject** them, or we are back where this started.
  - **Cosignatures verify against the ecosystem in both directions**, using the package a witness runs: their generator's key rendered by our renderer must be byte-identical, their signer's cosignature must verify here, and ours must open there. That is most of 4.4's substance, available without a witness process.
  - **A cosigned checkpoint must still verify under the log key alone** — the log's signature says this is the log, the cosignatures are additional — so unknown and cosignature lines are skipped rather than failed.
