An unknown witness is ignored (somebody else's business); a *trusted* witness's line that does not verify is an error (a claim that this network's witness said something it did not).
  - **Both rules are now normative and vectored**: §9.1 states the algorithms, and `trust-bad/15` and `16` are files that parse perfectly as verifier keys and are refused only by the algorithm rule.
  - The fingerprint moved, the guard from 2.8 caught it, and `SPEC.md`'s worked example was updated rather than left to rot.
  - Go: 40 packages, 375 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.1b `internal/policy` — the grammar, verified rather than remembered, and a consistency rule the file needed.**
  - **The published grammar turned out to match the shape Phase 0 guessed** — `log`, `witness`, `group <name> all|any|<k>`, exactly one `quorum`, `none` to opt out. That is a relief rather than a formality: 4.1a had just found a key encoding that was wrong in precisely the way an unverified guess is wrong, so the policy was checked against the specification before a line of parser was written.
  - **The parser is strict because a policy is where a network says how much corroboration it wants.** A line nobody understood is a requirement nobody is enforcing, so an unknown keyword, a forward reference, a duplicate key, a threshold larger than its group and a missing or second `quorum` are each errors. Fourteen refusals are tested by name.
  - **A cosigner nobody named counts for nothing**, however valid its signature — which is the property that makes a quorum mean anything. Two of three, `all`, `any` and nested groups are all exercised, and a repeated name is not two witnesses.
  - **`witnesses` and `policy` must now name exactly the same parties**, with the same keys. They are not redundant: `witnesses` is where a node learns a witness's *name and address*, `policy` is where it learns whether that witness *counts*. A file where they differ configures a quorum over parties the node cannot reach, or lists parties nothing requires — and either way the operator believes they set something they did not. Normative in §9.1, enforced in both implementations, and vectored as `trust-bad/17` and `18`.
  - **A vector that failed for the wrong reason was caught and fixed.** The first `17` used a key string edited by hand, so it was rejected for a broken key id rather than for the rule it is named after — the D-044 trap. It now carries a perfectly valid cosigner key under a name the policy never mentions, so only the agreement rule can reject it.
  - The shipped policy is checked to be usable *and* to need more than one witness: a homelab default whose quorum one machine could meet alone would have lost the only thing it was for.
  - Go: 41 packages, 383 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.1c the verifier enforces the quorum — and enforcing it proved Norn could not produce one.**
  - **§10 step 3 is real now.** A checkpoint is accepted only if every cosignature from a witness the policy *names* verifies, and the quorum is satisfied. Five refusals are tested by name: one cosignature short, none at all, cosignatures only from witnesses the policy never named, a forged line carrying a named witness's key id, and — the other direction — `quorum none` accepting an uncosigned head, because a network is allowed to ask for no corroboration and Norn does not get to overrule it.
  - **Each refusal was checked against a disabled check.** Stubbing `checkQuorum` to return "fine" turns all five red. A test that passes with the feature removed is a test of nothing, and this is the check most worth not accidentally disabling.
  - **Then it broke every end-to-end path, and that was the finding.** `cmd/norn`, `cmd/norn-log` and three package suites went red at once, all for the same reason: **nothing in Norn could produce a cosignature**, so every log it runs served heads that no correctly-configured node would accept. The verifier was not wrong; the product was half-built, and only enforcement made it visible. Shipping the enforcement without the other half would have been a release where `norn init` produces a network that cannot be verified.
  - **So `norn-log` learned `-self-witness name=keyfile`** — GOSSIP part 3's "same host as the log" row, which is independent of *nothing*, is allowed as a 1-of-1 in observe mode, and now says so on stderr every time it starts. It is the bootstrap and lab configuration; pushing to witnesses that run elsewhere is 4.3.
  - **It validates against the policy at startup**, because the failure it prevents is silent: a witness name the policy never mentions, or the right name under the wrong key, produces cosignatures nothing counts — a log that looks healthy to its operator and is refused by every verifier on the network. Both are refused before the listener opens. The flag takes the *policy's* short label (`pi`) and signs under the note name inside that witness's key (`norn-witness/pi`); they are two names for one party, and signing under the wrong one produces a line nothing matches.
  - **The store gained a `Cosigner` seam, which is the shape 4.3 plugs into.** `tlogstore` knows that a published head passes through corroboration; it does not know whether that is an HTTP round trip or a key in the same process.
  - **And it republishes its head on open** — a bug found by the E2E failure, not by a test. A log that gains a witness, or is restarted with different keys, was otherwise serving the checkpoint written under the *old* configuration until its next append: indefinitely, on a quiet log. The corollary is worth knowing: restarting `norn-log` without its `-self-witness` flags republishes an **uncosigned** head straight away, which is why it warns.
  - **A cosigner that fails fails the publish.** Writing the bare log-signed head instead would serve a checkpoint the network's own policy rejects, while looking exactly like a successful append. What a log should *do* about an unreachable witness — retry, serve stale, degrade — is 4.3's question; refusing to pretend is this one's.
  - **`norn-genlog` stopped cosigning by hand** and publishes through the same seam, so the fixture and a real log now take one path rather than two that must be kept in agreement. The regenerated `served/checkpoint` is byte-identical.
  - **The docs stopped overclaiming in one place and underclaiming in another.** §10's `# NOT YET IMPLEMENTED — Phase 4` is gone because the line is now true. §6 moved out of §12.1's "not vectored" table, with the caveat stated rather than skipped: the cosignature *format* is vectored and cross-checked against `transparency-dev/formats`, but these cosignatures come from keys the generator holds, so they prove the format is right — not that an unmodified witness will produce one. That is still 4.4.
  - **No Phase 4 gate line flips.** "Witness quorum lost → node refuses the new checkpoint, keeps last good, alarms" is a lab line: it needs witnesses that can be taken away. The software half is now true and tested, and the line stays open until the lab runs it.
  - Go: 40 packages, 392 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.2a `quorum_time` — the project's own specification was under-defined, and the test that proves it is four hundred seconds wide.**
  - **`GOSSIP.md` said "the *k-th newest* cosignature timestamp".** That is exactly right for a flat `group home 2 pi vps friend`, and it has **no meaning** once groups nest: `group top 1 inner friend` has no single k. The definition that survives both is *the latest instant T at which the witnesses who have signed by T satisfy the policy* — same answer for the flat case, defined for the rest. The doc now says that, with the k-th-newest reading kept as the intuition rather than the rule.
  - **The difference is a real number, not a quibble.** With `group inner 2 pi vps`, `group top 1 inner friend`, and signatures at pi=500, vps=400, friend=100: at 500 only pi has signed, so `inner` is short and `friend` is absent — nothing is satisfied. The answer is **400**, while "the 1st newest timestamp" says **500**. A node using the naive reading would believe a checkpoint is a hundred seconds fresher than any quorum vouched for. That case is a test.
  - **`quorum none` has no clock, and getting that backwards would be the dangerous way round.** A network that asked for no corroboration has nobody vouching for the *time* either — so freshness is `unknown`, not perfect. The easy mistake is to treat "policy satisfied by everyone, always" as "always fresh", which would hand a node a clock made of nothing.
  - **A witness the policy never named cannot move the clock**, however recent its cosignature. Same property as not being able to make up the quorum, and it needs its own test because it is a *different* code path: the quorum check happens once, the clock reads the same lines again.
  - **One mapping, not two.** A cosignature carries `norn-witness/pi` while the policy files that witness under `pi`; `logclient` had a private copy of that translation, and the clock needed it too. It is now `policy.Label`, where the two names already live side by side.
  - **A latent test bug surfaced the moment a pointer stopped being nil.** `back.Checkpoint != set.Checkpoint` compares a struct holding a `*int64` — which is address equality. It agreed for as long as both were `nil`, and would have gone on agreeing about nothing; it compares by value now.
  - `proven.json` publishes the instant the witnesses vouched for. The **state** computed from it — fresh, degraded, hard-stale — is 4.2b, so `freshness` still reads `unknown`: the reading exists, the machine that turns it into a claim about *now* does not yet.
- **4.2b the freshness machine — the wall clock is kept out by the type signature, not by a rule.**
  - **`Clock` is given durations, never timestamps.** It has no way to ask the operating system what time it is, so a node whose clock is wrong — or is moved while it runs — computes exactly the same age as one whose clock is right. That is A11 expressed as an API instead of as something a future edit has to remember, and it is the difference between a property and a comment. The agent test proves it from the outside: the wall clock lurches an hour forward between every cycle, and the view ages `fresh → degraded → hard_stale` on the monotonic reading alone.
  - **An older observation never moves the clock back.** A peer may legitimately serve a valid but older checkpoint; adopting its timestamp would drag the node's estimate of now backwards and make everything it holds look *newer* than it is — the one direction a freshness check must never fail in.
  - **A checkpoint older than the best one known is older**, and the age says so. Age is a property of what the node is *enforcing*, not of the best thing it has heard about, which matters precisely when a node is holding the last state that verified.
  - **The boundaries come from the log, and the fixture proved the point.** `staleness` is a §4.5 parameter, so a network agrees on when a view is too old; this fixture's log sets it to **900s** with a `params` entry, not §4.5's 600. The first version of the test asserted the default and failed — the log's own value is what a node must use, or it disagrees with the rest of its network about when to alarm. `S_hard` is the opposite: it drops every peer, so it stays a node-local decision (the same argument D-034 makes for the witness policy).
  - **`hard_stale` is computed but cannot yet be reached by a deployed node**, and that is deliberate rather than an oversight: `S_hard` is opt-in, defaults to off, and `nornd` has no flag for it. The state has no meaning apart from what the enforcer does about it, so the flag and the enforcement land together in **4.2d** — a state that drops every peer must not be reachable before the code that drops them exists.
  - **`proven.json` publishes the age as well as the state**, because a consumer that can only read a label cannot decide anything for itself, and the monitor's `norn_checkpoint_age_seconds` needs the number. Absent, not zero, when there is no clock.
  - **`norn status` stopped printing a hardcoded UNKNOWN.** It reports the instant a quorum vouched for as fact, and labels the age it derives as *this machine's clock* — a one-shot command has no monotonic history, so the honest thing is to name whose opinion the number is and say that the running daemon is what tracks the state.
  - **One set of state names.** `fresh`/`degraded`/`hard_stale`/`unknown` are defined where the machine is and aliased in `proven`, rather than spelled twice — two copies of a string constant is how a consumer matching on them quietly stops matching.
  - **A build failure that was not the code.** One suite run failed with `no required module provides package golang.org/x/mod/sumdb/tlog`, which passes in isolation; the module cache had been purged down to 16 MB because this machine's disk is at **99%** (3.2 GiB free). Worth writing down so the next flake of this shape is recognised as the disk rather than chased through the code.
  - Go: 42 packages, 411 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.2c `CLOCK_SKEW` — the documented formula reports a clock problem every time the log goes quiet.**
  - **`GOSSIP.md` said `|wall − quorum_time| > Δclock`.** That is the right number only at the instant a freshly cosigned head is fetched directly from the log. Away from that instant it measures the log's **staleness** as well: a log that has not been cosigned for two hours produces two hours on a node whose clock is perfect, and the node reports a clock fault. An operator following that alarm would go and "fix" the wrong machine while the actual problem — an unreachable log or a dead witness — went unnamed.
  - **The two are separable with what the node already has.** If its clock were right, `wall − quorum_time` would equal the monotonic age it has been measuring since adoption. The difference between them is the skew and nothing else, and it reduces to the documented expression exactly when the age is zero. `GOSSIP.md` now states that form, with the original kept as the special case it is.
  - **`GOSSIP.md`'s own T5 depends on the distinction**, which is how the defect was visible at all: a skewed node must raise `CLOCK_SKEW` and **not** go degraded. Its converse is the test that matters more — a genuinely stale log must go degraded and **not** raise `CLOCK_SKEW` — and both are now agent-level tests, not just unit ones.
  - **Both tests were run against the naive formula.** Substituting `wall.Sub(quorumTime)` turns the unit test and the agent test red, at both levels. A test that cannot tell the two implementations apart would have been decoration.
  - **The alarm changes nothing, and nothing downstream reads it.** Not the freshness state, not the proven set, not the peer table. A node that altered what it enforced because of its own clock would be doing the precise thing A11 forbids, so the only output is a line for a human.
  - **Δclock cannot be tight, and the reason is worth writing down.** At a direct fetch the newest cosignature is already one checkpoint interval plus a witness round trip old, so a threshold near zero would alarm on every healthy network. Five minutes absorbs that and still catches what this exists for: a dead RTC battery, a missing NTP server, a wrong time zone — failures measured in hours.
  - **It runs on the direct-from-log path only**, and the code says so where gossip will arrive: a checkpoint pulled from a peer may legitimately be older, and its age is the peer's business rather than evidence about this machine's clock.
  - **`norn status` still cannot report skew, and that is the honest answer.** A one-shot command has no monotonic history, so for it the ambiguity is real and irreducible — which is exactly why it prints "age by this machine's clock" and points at the running daemon.
  - **The one thing not shipped: the operator knobs.** `MaxSkew` and `Hard` are plumbed through `agent.Config` but `nornd` exposes neither, so Δclock is the 5-minute default and S_hard is off. The alarm is complete without a flag; a state that drops every peer is not, so both flags land with **4.2d**.
  - Go: 42 packages, 419 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.2d the freshness states do something — and hard-stale needed no new enforcement path at all.**
  - **`hard_stale` is "the proven set is empty".** `GOSSIP.md` part 5 spells out *owner: ReplacePeers []; watchdog: evict all*, and that is precisely what the existing reconciler already does for an empty desired set. So the state is implemented by passing one, not by a second enforcement path that could drift from the first. Both modes are tested, and observe still writes nothing however stale it gets.
  - **It says why, once.** An operator who found a dozen `UNPROVEN_PEER` lines and an empty interface would have no idea the cause was the clock, so `HARD_STALE` is emitted with the age before anything is dropped.
  - **The `degraded` alarm is on a cadence, and recovery resets it.** A daemon polling every minute must not produce a page a minute — but a degraded node that simply went *quiet* is indistinguishable from one that recovered, so it repeats every five minutes for as long as it lasts. Resetting on recovery is the part worth testing: carrying the suppression across would silence the next degradation for up to five minutes, which is exactly the transition an operator needs to see.
  - **An ordering bug, found by writing the test.** The cycle reconciled *before* observing the clock, so freshness could not have influenced enforcement at all — hard-stale would have dropped peers one cycle late, every time. How old the view is decides what reconciling is allowed to do, so it is now established first.
  - **A node's first sight of a checkpoint is age zero, and there is no way around it.** It has no history to measure against and its own clock is not evidence (A11), so a node that boots and fetches a three-day-old head calls it fresh. What it *can* say is `CLOCK_SKEW`: at a direct fetch, a large gap between wall time and witness time with an age of zero means either the clock is wrong or the log is ancient — and the node genuinely cannot tell which. That is an honest limit of refusing to trust the local clock, and the tests start the agent *running* rather than pretending otherwise.
  - **Opt-in twice.** `nornd` leaves S_hard unset unless asked, and **refuses a value below §4.5's `staleness` default** — that would drop every peer before the network had even called the view degraded, which is a typo with an outage attached. The flags are validated before the interface is opened, so a mistyped duration is reported as a configuration error rather than after a missing-interface one.
  - **Not done, and it belongs to gossip:** `degraded` should also "poll all sources every 10 s". With one source that is just polling the log harder; the sentence only means something once there are peers to poll, so it moves to **4.5**.
  - Go: 42 packages, 427 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.3a the `tlog-witness` client — checked against the specification's own bytes, and my first rule was wrong.**
  - **The protocol was fetched, not recalled**, which is the habit that found the algorithm-byte defect in 4.1a. `POST <prefix>/add-checkpoint`, body = `old <size>`, zero to 63 base64 consistency hashes, an empty line, then the checkpoint exactly as the log signed it.
  - **The test that matters is the document's own example request**, reproduced byte for byte. Everything else in the package checks it against itself; if the separator, the ordering, the base64 alphabet or a trailing newline were wrong *the same way* in the writer and the reader, only bytes somebody else wrote would ever notice.
  - **My own guard was wrong, and a test I wrote caught it.** I required a consistency proof whenever `old > 0`. But a proof from a size *to itself* is legitimately empty — which is exactly what resubmitting a head the witness has already cosigned looks like. The rule is keyed on the checkpoint's own size now: a proof is required when `0 < old < size`, and an `old` **ahead** of the checkpoint is this log's bug, refused here rather than sent to collect a `400`.
  - **`409` is the only refusal that carries data**, and it is the one the whole retry loop turns on: the witness answers with the size it last cosigned, so the log learns where to prove consistency from. It is a typed `ConflictError`, not a message, because a log that dropped the number would retry the same rejected request forever. A `409` whose body is *not* a size — a gateway error page — is an error rather than a guess.
  - **Every status is told apart, because each is a different thing to go and fix**: `404` the witness was never configured for this network, `403` it is configured for a different log key, `400` this log miscounted, `422` consistency could not be verified. That last one names the possibility worth checking first — **the log has forked and the witness is holding the other branch**.
  - **What comes back is verified before it is believed.** The protocol does not require a submitter to check, and a log that skipped it would publish whatever arrived: a head every verifier then refuses, with nothing log-side to say why. So a witness answering about some *other* checkpoint, or a middlebox rewriting one, is caught where it can still be reported — as is a `200` with an empty body, which is not success.
  - **An architecture test, because this is a boundary worth keeping.** `logclient`, `agent` and `nornd` may not import this package. A verifier reads cosignatures off the checkpoint it already has; one that dialled a witness would put a network round trip and a third party's availability on the path that decides whether a peer stays in the table.
  - **No new dependencies**: net/http and the packages Norn already has.
  - Go: 43 packages, 442 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.3b the log pushes — and a rule I set two checkpoints ago turned out to be wrong.**
  - **4.1c said a failing cosigner fails the publish. `GOSSIP.md`'s T6 says otherwise**, and T6 wins: *"2 of 3 witnesses down → log cannot reach quorum; nodes hold last good; `degraded` after S; recovers when witnesses return."* The log keeps publishing and the **nodes** decide what an under-witnessed head is worth. My rule was right for a cosigner holding local keys, where a failure is a bug; it is wrong for a network call, where a failure is Tuesday. A log that refused to write would turn a witness outage into a log outage — taking the network down to protect it from being slightly less corroborated. The store still fails on a *broken* cosigner, which is a different thing and now says so.
  - **`409` is how a log finds out where a witness is**, so there is no discovery request: submit from zero, be corrected, prove from there, and remember it. A witness that keeps conflicting is left for the next checkpoint rather than retried in a loop — whatever is wrong will not be fixed by asking again immediately, and a log that spun there would stop publishing.
  - **My own test expectation was wrong, and the code was right.** After a witness accepts size N, the next proof starts at N — not at the older size an earlier conflict named. Writing down what I *expected* made the difference visible.
  - **An ordering bug that would have hit every restart.** The store republishes its head on open (4.1c), but the witness client needs consistency proofs from the tree the store holds — so one has to exist before the other. The head therefore opened uncosigned, and on a **quiet** log nothing would have asked again until somebody submitted an entry. `Store.Republish` exists for that, and the log calls it once its proofs are wired.
  - **Which surfaced the bigger one: an idle log has to keep asking.** Witness time is what every node measures freshness against, so a log that cosigns once and goes quiet makes its whole network `degraded` after `staleness` with nothing actually wrong — the quietest networks first, which is exactly backwards. `norn-log` republishes every `checkpoint_interval` (§4.5's T_c, 60s), and the test drives a real binary against a real HTTP witness and waits for the witness time on the served head to **move**.
  - **A silent bug in my own wiring, caught before it shipped.** With both remote witnesses and `-self-witness`, the second `WithCosigner` replaced the first — an operator who configured both would have lost their remote witnesses without being told. `tlogstore.Cosigners` chains them, with a test that both reach the checkpoint.
  - **A log is not configured twice.** The witnesses' addresses come from the network's own `norn.trust`, which already names them; a witness with no `url` is skipped rather than refused, since somebody else may still collect its cosignature.
  - **Submissions run concurrently, and the output does not depend on who answered first**: the lines are emitted in the order the network lists its witnesses, so a checkpoint does not change shape because of a race.
  - Go: 43 packages, 452 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
  - **And one more outage I built by hand and the suite caught.** Doing that first refresh *synchronously* held the listener closed for the whole witness timeout — so a log whose witnesses were down could not start serving at all, exactly when an operator is trying to bring it back. Two unrelated end-to-end tests went red on it. Refreshing now runs in the background from the first moment, which is T6 applied to startup rather than only to steady state.
- **4.4 an unmodified `transparency-dev/witness` cosigned a Norn checkpoint — ground rule 3, proven rather than asserted.**
  - **This is the claim nothing else in the repository could establish.** A log can sign anything it likes, including two histories for two nodes; what it cannot do is get independent witnesses to cosign both at the same size. That argument is worth nothing unless a witness *somebody else wrote* will cosign what Norn publishes. `internal/interop` now drives the real thing at a pinned commit: its binary, its key from its own `generate_keys`, no Norn code in it and none of its code in Norn's `go.mod`.
  - **The first assertion lands before anything is running.** `generate_keys` prints a **cosignature v1** vkey — algorithm `0x04` — and Norn re-renders it byte for byte. That is 4.1a's defect answered by the other implementation rather than by this one: the code that shipped before 4.1a could not have parsed this key at all.
  - **And the negative half is what makes the positive one mean something.** With the witness's line stripped, the same checkpoint is **refused**, naming the quorum. Without that, step six would have proven only that verification succeeds — not that the cosignature was why.
  - **Two usability defects that only doing it for real could find**, both in the command that creates a network:
    - **`norn init` could not accept a published witness key.** A witness operator hands you `norn-witness/x+244b01f9+BOkU…` — that is literally what `generate_keys` prints — and Norn wanted raw hex. Every step of that conversion has a wrong answer available. It takes the published key now, and **refuses a plain note key** where a cosignature key belongs, which is 4.1a's defect wearing an operator's hat.
    - **`norn init` could not record where a witness lives.** So a real deployment had to hand-edit the trust file afterwards — which changes the fingerprint every node was provisioned to compare against (§9.3). `-witness-url name=url` closes that, and a URL naming a witness that was never declared is refused: that typo would otherwise leave the real witness unreachable and say nothing.
    - A witness this network did not create has a **name of its own**, so `genesis.Witness` carries it: the short label the policy files it under and the name inside its key are two different things, and signing under the wrong one produces a line nothing matches.
  - **The dependency stays outside the module, deliberately.** `transparency-dev/witness` pulls Cloud Spanner, gRPC, OpenTelemetry and sqlite — none of which belongs anywhere near a verifier's dependency graph. `make witness` builds it in its own module under `build/`; there is no `go.work` either, because the point is that the two share **no code at all**.
  - **It skips unless built, and CI can forbid the skip** — `NORN_REQUIRE_WITNESS=1`, the same arrangement as the OpenSSH `ssh-agent` tests, so a cross-implementation test cannot quietly stop running everywhere at once. Both paths are exercised.
  - **A third defect the leftovers gave away.** After a red run, a stray keyfile named `norn-witness/plain+caa6cd29+…` was sitting in the repository: `-witness` generates a keypair when handed a path that does not exist, so a published key with one character lost in copying had quietly become a **brand-new witness identity** whose private half was a file nobody knew about. The network would have been created trusting it, and the mistake would have surfaced weeks later as "the witness never cosigns anything". A value that contains `+` and is not an existing file is now refused as a mangled key rather than read as a path.
  - **One piece of console noise fixed on the way:** the store republishes as it opens, before the witness client has a tree to build proofs from, so every start logged a failure that was expected and harmless. It is recognised and silenced at the one place it is expected, rather than by lowering the volume on real ones.
  - **`PLAN.md` gate line closed:** *"An unmodified `transparency-dev/witness` cosigns a Norn checkpoint (ground rule 3 proven)"* — the first Phase 2 gate line to close since 2.10, and the one the ground rules named.
  - Go: 44 packages, 457 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.5a a node keeps what it verified — because gossip works by serving data, never claims.**
  - **The reason full replication is not an optimisation.** A node that remembered only the checkpoint could tell a peer what it believes and could not prove it. Because a node keeps the tiles, the receiver recomputes the consistency proof from bytes it fetched — so a lying *peer* is caught by exactly the code that catches a lying *log*, and gossip needs no trust model of its own. It is also what buys liveness under a log freeze (T2): one node that got the new checkpoint is enough for the whole connected mesh, but only if it kept the bytes.
  - **The test is not "files appeared".** It is that the directory a node wrote **verifies on its own, with the log gone** — signature, quorum, tiles, tree and replay, through `logclient.Verify`, the same call a node makes against a real log. Cosignatures included: a head stripped of them is one no correctly-configured node accepts, so a mirror that dropped them would be a peer nobody could use.
  - **Unchanged means no writes.** A node polls on `T_poll`; a mirror that rewrote every file each cycle would turn the 24-hour soak into a disk benchmark. Five repeat calls leave every modification time untouched — the same property `PLAN.md` already requires of the peer table, applied to the one other thing a node writes continuously.
  - **The checkpoint arrives parsed, which is the type saying the caller verified it.** `Write` takes `tlog.Checkpoint` rather than only the note, so a mirror built from something merely *fetched* is not expressible. And a head that does not describe the entries handed over is refused: writing it would produce a node whose own tiles contradict its head, which every peer reading it would report as a broken log — naming this node.
  - **Observe mode mirrors too**, and that is the point rather than an oversight: a node that only watches is exactly the one you want serving the mesh, because it is the one nobody is worried about.
  - **Three copies of the entry-bundle codec became one.** The log wrote bundles, the verifier read them, and a mirror would have been the fourth. They were identical line for line — which is luck, not design, and two decoders that disagree about a *malformed* bundle disagree about what the log contains. `tlog.EncodeBundle`/`DecodeBundle` now own the framing, with the malformed cases tested once.
  - **A mirror failure is reported, not fatal.** Nothing a node enforces depends on being able to serve its peers, so `MIRROR_FAILED` is an alarm and the cycle carries on.
  - Go: 45 packages, 467 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.5b the gossip server — thirty lines, because the design left nothing for it to do.**
  - **`internal/gossip` depends on no other Norn package at all.** Not the verifier, not the enforcement side — `go list -deps` shows only itself. That is the design claim made structural rather than stated: a peer serves bytes, and whoever reads them decides what they mean using the same `logclient.Verify` they would run against the log. `Peer` satisfies `logclient.Fetcher` **without importing it**, because Go interfaces are structural. An architecture test holds the line: the day this package needs the verifier is the day gossip has grown an opinion about what to believe.
  - **So the test that matters is `TestAPeerVerifiesAsALogDoes`** — point the real verifier at a peer's HTTP endpoints and get the fixture's 90 entries, cosignatures and all. No gossip-specific verification exists to test, which is the outcome worth having.
  - **"On the WireGuard address only" is a property of the binding, so it is made unexpressible otherwise.** There is no `-gossip-listen`; `TunnelAddress(iface, port)` derives the address from the interface, and the flag is a **port**. An operator cannot expose a node's mirror to the internet by typing the wrong thing, because there is nothing to type — and a test asserts no such flag exists, so a future convenience cannot quietly add one.
  - **A peer answers questions and takes no instructions.** Anything but `GET`/`HEAD` is `405`. Everything a node might push — an entry, a claim about a size — goes to the log, which is the only thing that can decide anything.
  - **The mirror is the whole world the handler can see.** A node's `proven.json`, its proof bundles and its fork evidence are siblings on disk, and none of them is a peer's business; four traversal attempts are tested, and directory listings are off so a peer cannot ask what else is there.
  - **A tunnel that is not up is a warning, not a refusal to run.** Gossip is how a network survives a *log* outage, not how a node enforces — refusing to start because peers cannot reach us would trade the second for the first.
  - **The fixture corrected a guess.** I wrote the tile paths as `tile/entries/000`; a 90-entry log serves `tile/entries/000.p/90`, because partial tiles are the normal case for any log that is not a multiple of 256 entries. Better found by a test than by a peer.
  - Go: 46 packages, 476 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.5c §10's comparison with the stored head — a live rollback hole, found while building the thing that would have made it reachable by anyone.**
  - **Neither half was implemented.** §10 says plainly: *"if stored_cp exists: require cp.size >= stored_cp.size; verify consistency proof stored_cp → cp; on failure → FORK"*. Nothing anywhere compared a fetched checkpoint against the one a node already held. Writing the gossip client is what surfaced it, because the first question a peer raises is "what stops a peer serving me an old view" — and the answer was **nothing**.
  - **The attack is not subtle, and every signature checks out.** Serve a node the same log, earlier. It is correctly signed by the log key, correctly cosigned by a real quorum of witnesses, internally consistent, and it replays perfectly. The node replays it and rolls its peer table backwards: **a device revoked at entry 80 is active again** if somebody serves the log as it stood at entry 70. Against a log this needs the log operator; once a node also asks peers, it is a move anyone in the mesh can make.
  - **The consistency half needs no proof from anybody.** Full replication (4.5a) means the node holds every entry, so it recomputes what the head at the stored size *must* have been and compares — stronger than checking a proof somebody else supplied, and exactly what `GOSSIP.md` part 6 means by "computed locally from tiles… so a peer serves data, never claims". `tlog.Tree.HeadAt` is tested against independently rebuilt prefix trees at seven sizes rather than against itself.
  - **A mismatch is a fork, and is named as one** — same size, different history means the two are not the same log (§11), which is a different finding from "this log went backwards" and deserves its own words.
  - **The check is only worth anything if something uses it**, so the agent passes what it holds on every cycle, and there is a test at *that* level too: a node offered its own log truncated refuses it, reports it, and leaves the peer table it was already enforcing untouched. Without that test the feature could have shipped complete and unused — the same shape as the quorum gap 4.1c closed.
  - **Nil means "first contact", and is now a documented hazard rather than a default.** Passing nil when a node does hold a head disables the one check that stops this.
  - **`SPEC.md` §10 now says what to compute, not what to trust.** The line used to say "verify consistency proof stored_cp → cp"; a node with every entry has no reason to take a proof from the party it is checking.
  - Go: 46 packages, 482 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.5d the gossip client — a second source, not a second trust model.**
  - **There is no new verification, and that is the deliverable.** A peer's answer goes through `logclient.Verify` under the same anchor as the log's — signature, quorum, tiles, tree, replay, **and §10's comparison with the head this node holds** (4.5c). So a lying peer is exactly as dangerous as a lying log: caught. The test suite for this checkpoint contains no cryptography at all, only *where a node looks* and *what it does with the answer*.
  - **T2, demonstrated:** a node with a dead log and one healthy peer recovers, adopts the peer's checkpoint, and says so — the log being unreachable is news even when gossip covered for it.
  - **And the attack it does not enable:** a peer serving the same log, earlier, cannot roll a node back. That test exists because it is the question a reviewer should ask the moment a second source appears, and it would have failed a checkpoint ago.
  - **Peers are asked only when the log could not be used.** A node polling every 200 ms that also asked three neighbours each time would put a great deal of traffic on the very link it is protecting, to learn nothing. A test pins it: three healthy cycles, zero peer polls.
  - **Random, not fixed.** A node that always asked the same three peers would let those three decide what it hears — and in a network small enough to fit in a house, "the same three" is often "all the ones an attacker owns". Each node also gets its own ordering, so two nodes started together do not walk the mesh in step.
  - **"Unproven peers are not polled" is tested with the intruder *routed*.** The operator has written a route for it, so the only thing keeping it out of the round is that the log does not prove it. Asking it for a checkpoint would be asking the intruder what the network looks like.
  - **A proven peer with no route is unreachable, and Norn does not invent an address for it.** Allowed-ips come from the endpoint provider, so gossip reaches exactly as far as the operator's routing does — which my first draft of the test got wrong, and the fixture corrected.
  - **`nornd` has no second list to keep in step.** The peers it may ask are the ones the agent itself has proven, read back through `PeerAddresses`, so an unproven peer can never appear on a list somebody forgot to update.
  - **One broken neighbour is not an outage:** a peer that answers with nonsense is reported and skipped, and the node keeps the log's own checkpoint.
  - Go: 46 packages, 492 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.5e a degraded node polls every source — and "every source" is the half that matters.**
  - **The sentence deferred from 4.2d was "poll all sources every 10 s", and the important word is *all*.** A node whose log is answering perfectly well with something **old** is exactly the node that needs its peers: the log is up and stuck, which is precisely T2, and only a peer can say so. Asking peers solely when the log is *unreachable* would leave a whole mesh stale behind a log that never stopped answering — every request served, every answer current-looking, nothing moving.
  - **So the trigger is the freshness of what was fetched, not whether the fetch worked.** `isStale` reads the clock **without advancing it**: whether to reach past the log is a question about the head just received, and adopting it as "now" before deciding would answer the question with itself.
  - **A peer with nothing newer changes nothing.** Adoption needs a strictly larger checkpoint, or a degraded node would churn its peer table against an equal head every ten seconds for as long as the log stayed quiet.
  - **The 10-second rule only ever speeds a node up.** A naive reading — "degraded means poll every 10 s" — would *slow* a node already polling four times a second, at the exact moment it should be trying hardest. `nextPoll` takes the faster of the two, and the table test pins both directions. The rule earns its keep on a node deliberately polling slowly, which is reasonable on a laptop right up until the log goes quiet and nobody notices for five minutes.
  - **A node that has not run a cycle reports `unknown`, not `fresh`.** The daemon reads the state to decide how hard to try, and starting out claiming to be fine is the wrong default for the one moment a node knows least.
  - **Phase 4's gossip work is now complete** — hold (4.5a), serve (4.5b), the rollback check that made it safe (4.5c), pull (4.5d), and try harder when stale (4.5e).
  - Go: 46 packages, 496 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.6a fork evidence — written for a sceptical stranger, not for the node that produced it.**
  - **`GOSSIP.md` says where this file goes**: "the witness operators, the log operator's boss, or a blog post". So the bar is not that the accusing node is convinced. It is that somebody holding only `norn.trust` — no running network, no reason to trust whoever is complaining — can check it offline and see for themselves. Every design decision here follows from that one sentence.
  - **Two signed heads are not evidence.** At *different* sizes, different roots are exactly what a healthy log produces. What makes it a fork is a consistency proof showing the larger head's own tree had a **different root at the smaller head's size** than the smaller head claims — and because that proof chains to a head the log signed, the complainant cannot fabricate it. At equal sizes no proof is needed: the two signatures say it alone.
  - **The quorum is checked on *both* heads, and that is the point of the artifact.** A log signing two things by itself is a log misbehaving in private; what makes a fork provable to a third party is that **independent witnesses vouched for both**. So the file carries the policy, and a reader sees which witnesses were meant to prevent this — because they are who they go to next.
  - **A node cannot accuse a log of forking with its own earlier self.** `Build` refuses two consistent heads, which is the mistake an over-eager detector would make and the one that would destroy the artifact's credibility the first time somebody checked it.
  - **Four fabrications are tested, each of which a weaker check would pass**: a head signed by another key, a head no quorum cosigned, a made-up consistency proof, and evidence held up against another network's anchor.
  - **`norn verify-evidence` reports "does not prove a fork" as an outcome, not only as a failure.** For a reader checking somebody else's accusation, that is the *important* direction — and a command that could only say "error" would leave them unable to tell a bad file from a bad log.
  - **`Build` takes the conflicting head already parsed**, the same discipline as `mirror.Write`: the node reaching this point got it through the verifier, and re-parsing it here would be this package deciding, with no keys, what it is looking at.
  - Go: 47 packages, 504 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.6b a forked node freezes — and the freeze had to outlive the process, or "forever" means "until somebody reboots".**
  - **§11's four duties, each with its own test**: write the evidence, keep the stored checkpoint and peer table unchanged, raise `FORK`, and refuse every later checkpoint — **including the original log, once it looks sensible again**. That last one is the point: the node holds proof this log signs whatever suits it, and "it seems fine now" is not a reason to believe it.
  - **A real gap, found by a test that failed for the wrong reason.** A log that forks by serving a **shorter** branch was being refused as an ordinary rollback — safe, but recorded as the wrong thing and leaving **no evidence**. The cause: at a smaller size the *served* tree cannot say what this node's branch had, so "consistent prefix" and "different history" are indistinguishable from the served side alone. The verifier now compares at `min(both sizes)` using the node's **own** tree, and checks that *before* the size rule — so the two are told apart whichever way round the sizes fall.
  - **Evidence is built from this node's branch, never the served one.** The served tree is the one under suspicion; evidence drawn from it would be the accused certifying the accusation.
  - **A node checks its own accusation before filing it.** `recordFork` runs the same `forkevidence.Check` a stranger would, and reports the failure loudly if its own file does not prove a fork. Evidence that does not prove anything is worse than none — it is an accusation somebody will check and dismiss.
  - **The freeze is a file, not a flag.** Its presence in `fork/` is the state, so a daemon an attacker reboots comes back frozen. And it is filed **per network**, which is exactly §11's escape hatch working as written: a new trust file is a different network to this node, so the freeze lifts — while the evidence stays on disk, because it is still what an operator takes to the witness operators.
  - **A frozen node touches nothing and says so on every cycle.** Not the peer table, not `proven.json`. It repeats because a frozen node doing nothing looks exactly like a working one, and this is the one state where silence would be indistinguishable from health.
  - Go: 47 packages, 508 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.7a reading OpenTimestamps proofs — and being precise about the half that can be checked without a blockchain.**
  - **The vector is 146 bytes `python-opentimestamps` wrote**, not this project. A parser checked only against its own writer agrees with itself and with nobody, and 4.1a is the standing reminder of what that costs. Its shape is the realistic one: two branches from one digest, one confirmed in a Bitcoin block and one still pending at a calendar — and both parse, with the block height and the calendar URI coming out right first time.
  - **The reference implementation would not import**, because `python-opentimestamps` pulls `python-bitcoinlib` for code paths the serializer never touches. A six-line stub for the names it imports was enough to get *their* serializer to write the file. Installing a package tree on a disk at 99% to obtain 146 bytes would have been the wrong trade.
  - **What a node can establish offline, stated exactly:** that the operations really do chain from *this* checkpoint's digest to whatever each attestation commits to. A log that edits a checkpoint after stamping it has a proof that no longer chains, and that is visible with no Bitcoin node, no calendar and no trust in the party under suspicion. That is the half of T8 that does not need a chain, and it is tested by tampering.
  - **What it cannot, said in the code rather than glossed:** confirming that a Bitcoin attestation's commitment is really in the block it names needs a chain. `Walk` returns the commitments and stops there. Claiming more would be exactly the borrowed confidence D-009 rejected on-chain registries for.
  - **A pending attestation proves nothing and says so.** It is a calendar's promise to include a value, not evidence that anybody did — and a reader who treated the two alike would think an unconfirmed stamp meant something.
  - **Every length in the file is somebody else's number**, because a `.ots` arrives from a calendar over the network: depth, branch count, operand size and calendar URI are all bounded, and five malformed files are refused rather than parsed — including trailing bytes glued onto a valid proof.
  - **An architecture test for D-009's own words.** "Never touches the handshake path" is a claim about what can influence whether a peer is proven, so `logclient`, `agent` and `nornd` may not import this package at all. Anchoring answers "was this backdated" — a question for an operator reading evidence, not for the code deciding what goes in the peer table in the next 200 milliseconds.
  - **No new dependencies.** `ripemd160` comes from `x/crypto`, already in the graph; `go mod tidy` only reclassified things that were mislabelled indirect. staticcheck objects to the deprecated import, and rightly — so the suppression carries the reason: *the format contains this operation; reading it is not using it*. A reader that refused to compute a `ripemd160` op would simply fail to read proofs other people's tools legitimately write.
  - **The vector suite gained a third provenance, and the D-052 guard caught it immediately.** `anchors/` is produced by neither of Norn's implementations, so `make vectors-check` refused to run until it was declared — which is exactly the check that was added after `make vectors` silently deleted eleven files. It is `KEEP`, not `OWNED`: nothing regenerates it, because regenerating it would defeat the point.
  - Go: 48 packages, 514 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.7b `norn verify --anchored` — and deciding exactly which bytes get stamped.**
  - **`SPEC.md` said "OpenTimestamps proof for the checkpoint at `size`" without saying *which bytes*.** The answer is `sha256` of the checkpoint **body** — origin, size, root — and not the signed note. Two reasons, and the second is the interesting one. A note-level digest would be **unstable**: cosignatures are appended as witnesses answer, so the stamp would stop matching the moment one replied. And it would add nothing — the log is the party an anchor exists to constrain, so binding the claim to the log's own signature proves nothing extra about *when the tree existed*.
  - **The summary is written to be impossible to misread.** Norn walked the operations; it did **not** ask a blockchain whether the commitment is in the block, because it has no blockchain to ask. So the line says `(that the block contains it is not checked here — it needs a bitcoin node)` every time, and a test forbids the phrasings that would sound like more: "confirmed on", "proven at", "verified in". Saying it every time is cheaper than one person concluding otherwise once.
  - **A pending attestation is reported as "not yet anchored".** It is a calendar's *promise* to include a value, not evidence that anybody did, and a reader who treated the two alike would think an unconfirmed stamp meant something.
  - **`--anchored` never fails a verification, and that is D-009 rather than laziness.** A missing, unreadable or mismatched proof is reported and nothing more. A node that refused a perfectly good log because an optional extra was absent would be enforcing the opposite of the decision — and would hand anybody who can delete one file a way to stop a network working.
  - **But a mismatched proof is said loudly**, because "this proof is for a different document" is exactly what a checkpoint edited after stamping looks like. Advisory means it changes nothing the log proves; it does not mean it goes unmentioned.
  - **The vector generator is committed**, so the third-party bytes are reproducible rather than mysterious: `contrib/ots-vector/` holds the script, the six-line `python-bitcoinlib` stub and the reason it exists. There is deliberately **no serializer** in `internal/ots` — Norn reads `.ots` files and never writes them, so the pending-only test prunes a parsed tree rather than growing a writer whose only user would be a test.
  - **The gate line still needs 4.7c and the lab**: *"Checkpoint N has a verifiable OpenTimestamps proof in `anchors/`"* requires somebody to obtain one, which is a calendar round trip (T8's "monitor OTS-stamps checkpoint N"). The checking half is done and the producing half is named rather than assumed.
  - Go: 48 packages, 521 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
- **4.7c `norn stamp` — obtaining a proof without growing a serializer.**
  - **The calendar protocol is asymmetric, and that asymmetry is the whole design.** `POST <calendar>/digest` answers with a **bare timestamp tree** — no magic, no version, no file-hash op, because the client knows which digest it asked about. A detached `.ots` is a fixed header followed by exactly those bytes. So building one is **concatenation**, and several calendars merge with the format's own branch marker: `0xff` before every branch but the last.
  - **Which means `internal/ots` still has no serializer.** That was deliberate in 4.7a and it held: a writer built only for this would be checked against its own reader and nothing else. Instead what the concatenation produces is **parsed back and walked** before it is returned — by the reader that was validated against the reference implementation's own bytes. A proof that does not chain is never written, because a proof that does not chain looks exactly like evidence of an edit.
  - **A third-party vector for the calendar's side too.** `calendar-response.bin` is a bare tree written by `python-opentimestamps`, so the fake calendar in the tests answers with bytes this project did not compose.
  - **One calendar down is not a failure**, which is the only reason to list several; **every** calendar down is, because then there is nothing to write. And a calendar that answers with nonsense is refused by name rather than written to disk.
  - **`norn stamp` refuses to stamp a log that does not verify.** A timestamp over a checkpoint this network rejects would be a timestamp on somebody else's document — and it would sit in `anchors/` looking like corroboration.
  - **A test pins the digest rule against the committed vector.** The fixture proof was built by the reference implementation over `sha256(checkpoint body)`; if this build's rule ever drifts, that file stops matching and the test says so. It is the cheapest possible guard on a decision that is otherwise only prose.
  - **Norn does not *upgrade* proofs, and that is a boundary rather than a gap.** Replacing a pending branch with a block attestation means rewriting the tree, which needs the serializer this package is better off without — so `norn stamp` says plainly that the `ots` tool does it. Norn's business is obtaining a proof and reading one.
  - Go: 48 packages, 529 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
  - **The gate line stays open, and precisely why:** `PLAN.md` wants "a verifiable OpenTimestamps proof in `anchors/`", and T8 spells out *"after the Bitcoin proof upgrades"*. Both halves work — obtaining against a calendar, checking against a checkpoint, and failing on a tampered one — but a fresh stamp is a promise, and it becomes evidence a few hours later on the public internet. That is the lab's to run.
- **4.8 the Phase 4 gate audit — two gate lines were false, and one of them was a spec violation in the code that decides the peer table.**
  - **Gate line 1 said "detected via gossip". It was not.** A peer serving a second history went through `askPeers`, failed verification, and was filed as `PEER_REFUSED` — one neighbour being unreachable. So the split-view scenario gossip exists for produced **no fork, no evidence and no freeze**. §11 does not care which source a second history arrived from; a forked peer now raises `FORK`, writes evidence and freezes exactly as a forked log does. The test names the scenario the gate line describes.
  - **Gate line T5 said "proven set unchanged". It was not — and the cause was a normative line ignored.** SPEC §5.3 says in as many words: *"Entries whose `expires` is earlier than **the checkpoint time** are treated as `revoked` at evaluation time… so state stays clock-independent."* The code used **the node's wall clock**, in `proven.FromResult` and, worse, in `Agent.desired` — the function that decides what goes in the peer table. A machine with a fast clock evicted a device early; a slow one kept an expired device; neither said anything. Both now judge expiry against the checkpoint's own time, which is witness time, which is what A11 has said all along.
  - **The fixture had the evidence sitting there.** With the wall clock a year ahead, `contractor` disappeared from the proven set three months before witness time reached its expiry. That is the whole bug in one line of test output, and nothing before this audit had ever asked the question.
  - **With no witness time, expiry is not evaluated at all.** There is no clock, so there is no judgement: the node keeps what it last knew and its freshness reads `unknown`, which is the alarm. Falling back to the wall clock there would have been the one place A11 leaked.
  - **One line closed outright: "Replayed old checkpoint → rejected".** Both mechanisms are tested at three levels — in the verifier, at the node with its own log truncated, and through gossip where a peer cannot roll a node back. T4's `REPLAY` counter is a monitor metric and belongs to Phase 5, which the note says.
  - **One line has nothing behind it, and now says so.** T1b wants a node to freeze on evidence **another node** proved. `GOSSIP.md` lists `GET /norn/evidence`; nothing serves or consumes it. That is a missing feature rather than an untested one, so it is 4.9 rather than a footnote.
  - **The rest are measurements or real hardware**, and each now records exactly which half is true: two log instances and 2·T_g timing, witnesses actually taken down, a real log server killed, a machine with a two-hour offset, a latency in milliseconds, and a calendar round trip that takes hours.
  - Go: 48 packages, 533 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.
  - **The pattern held for the third phase running:** a gate made of measurements cannot be closed by a test suite, but auditing it against the code finds the lines whose *substance* was never checked — and two of them were wrong.
- **4.9 evidence propagation — the one place gossip is needed *because* everything looks fine.**
  - **T1b is not a variation on checkpoint gossip, it is the opposite case.** Checkpoints are fetched from peers when the log cannot be used. But node3 in T1b has a **healthy** log: it is being served one consistent history, has no reason to doubt it, and under the checkpoint rule would never ask anybody anything — while two other nodes have already proved that history is a fork. Evidence is needed precisely when nothing looks wrong, so it runs on its own schedule (`T_g`, 30 s) whatever the log is doing. Writing the test first is what made that obvious: it failed exactly as predicted, because a healthy node never asked.
  - **A peer's evidence is believed only because it is checked** — by the same `forkevidence.Check` a stranger would run against the same trust file, which is why 4.6a was built for a sceptical stranger in the first place. Rubbish from a neighbour cannot freeze a network, and a test proves it: otherwise anybody on the tunnel could take the network down with a file of junk. Genuine evidence means there genuinely is a fork, whoever hands it over.
  - **`/norn/evidence` is an exception to 4.5b's rule, and it is an exception by being named.** A node's evidence lives beside its `proven.json` and its proof bundles, none of which is a peer's business — so a *single file* is served from that directory, never the directory. Two traversal attempts are tested.
  - **Newest wins.** A node freezes on the first fork it proves, so a second file means an operator reset with a new trust file and it happened again; the later one is about the network now running.
  - **A node that froze keeps a copy**, so it can pass it on. That is what makes propagation transitive rather than a single hop, and it is what T1b's `≤ 3·T_g` is counting.
  - **A node that learns of a fork before it ever enforced anything enforces nothing at all**, which the test asserts directly: the interface stays empty rather than being filled from a history already proved false.
  - **`T_g` is not the poll interval, and now says so.** Polling the local peer table four times a second is cheap; asking three neighbours over the tunnel that often is neither cheap nor useful.
  - Go: 48 packages, 538 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.1 the monitor's rules — every alert is a sentence, because a page at three in the morning is read by a person.**
  - **`LAB.md` §3's table is the specification, and the tests are it read back.** Governance entries (`signer_*`, `root_*`, `log_key`, `policy`, `params`) are critical because they change *who may say what*; everything else adds or removes a device, which matters less than changing who decides. Every other entry is still reported at info — "a homelab has few", and the one nobody was told about is the one that matters.
  - **Not codes.** `UNPROVEN_PEER on node1: … Something is putting keys on an interface that the log does not prove.` A monitor that pages with `LEVEL=2 CODE=7` has failed at the only job it has, so every rule carries a sentence and a test looks for the node's name in it.
  - **A missing heartbeat says what stops happening**, not that it stopped: *"in watchdog mode that makes its eviction window unbounded"*. Without that an operator files it under "probably fine", which is exactly what it is not.
  - **Anchor lag is info and only ever info**, and the test says why: D-009 makes anchoring advisory, and the monitor is precisely where that would quietly stop being true. A page about a missing anchor trains an operator to treat it as load-bearing.
  - **The escalation is a real distinction.** A checkpoint past `staleness` is a warning; past **3·S** it is critical, because a freeze that has run three times too long is a different event from one that just started — and the alert says which it is rather than repeating the same line louder.
  - **Every alert names its rule**, because the gate line asks for "one notification **per rule**" and nothing can count that otherwise. A test requires the names to be distinct, so a rule that cannot be told from another cannot hide inside it.
  - **Thresholds are fields, not constants.** A homelab changes the heartbeat window or the anchor-lag tolerance without editing Go, and a test sets both to prove it.
  - Go: 49 packages, 548 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.2 watching a network — the monitor is an enrolled peer, not a privileged one.**
  - **It verifies everything it reads with the same `logclient.Verify` a node runs, under the same anchor.** That is not code reuse for its own sake: a monitor that believed something a node would refuse would be reporting on a different network than the one that exists, and its alerts would be about nothing. A node's gossip endpoints are read through `gossip.Peer`, which is the same Fetcher a node uses on a neighbour — so the monitor has no privileged path to anything.
  - **Nothing in a round returns an error.** A monitor that fell over when the thing it monitors fell over would be worse than no monitor, because somebody is relying on it. Every failure becomes an alert and the round carries on; a test runs one with the log gone and every node unreachable and requires alerts to come out.
  - **An unchanged log is silent.** Ninety entries repeated every thirty seconds gets a monitor turned off within the hour, and then it is monitoring nothing — so the log size and the rejection count already described are remembered, and a second round over the same log says nothing new.
  - **The fixture's rejected entries turned out to be a feature.** It contains entries replay refuses, and a log publishing those is its operator misbehaving or running something buggy — the one thing *only* a monitor would ever notice, since every node simply ignores them. The test requires them to be reported.
  - **Evidence is read before the checkpoint**, because a node that has frozen may not be answering anything else usefully, and what it is holding is the loudest thing it could possibly say.
  - **A monitor starting up does not accuse everybody of being down.** A node never yet seen is recorded as seen now, so the heartbeat rule measures silence rather than ignorance.
  - **An architecture test for §3's own words.** "It never sits on the enforcement path": `agent`, `reconcile` and `nornd` may not import the monitor at all. The monitor is allowed to do things a node must not — poll witnesses, hold a receipt inbox, talk to a push service on the internet — and that is safe exactly because nothing it concludes can reach the peer table.
  - Go: 49 packages, 557 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.3 the receipt inbox — and a `uint64` cast that made every receipt look expired.**
  - **SPEC §7 decides the design in one clause**: *"A receipt whose leaf is absent from every checkpoint **cosigned after `deadline`** is non-repudiable proof of log misbehaviour."* **Cosigned after.** The comparison is against witness time, never the monitor's clock — which is exactly what makes the finding non-repudiable rather than an accusation: it does not depend on the accuser's clock being right. A monitor running fast cannot slander an honest log, and a witness outage produces **silence** rather than a false alarm, because with no cosignature nothing can be said to be late. Both are tests.
  - **A latent bug, found by needing the behaviour §7 implies.** `receipt.Check` compared `deadline <= uint64(now.Unix())`. `Unix()` is negative before 1970 and the zero `time.Time` is year 1, so that cast **wrapped** to something enormous and every receipt looked expired — silently, and in the direction that turns a valid promise into a refusal. It compares as signed now, and a zero clock explicitly means *do not judge the deadline*, which is what a caller with no clock it trusts for the purpose actually needs.
  - **Receipts are checked at the door**, because anybody can forward one — so anybody could forward a forgery. A receipt that is not this log's word, or is for another network, is refused and not kept: otherwise the monitor could be made to accuse an honest operator by anyone who can reach it.
  - **A proven misbehaviour stays pending.** The proof does not stop being true because it has been mentioned once, and an operator who was asleep still has to find it in the morning. Honoured receipts are forgotten, which is the only thing that should ever clear one.
  - **The alert says what was promised and what the witnesses vouched for**, with both instants — so the reader can check the claim rather than take it.
  - Go: 49 packages, 565 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.4 outputs — "all optional; stdout always", and the last word is the one worth defending.**
  - **A monitor whose only record of an alert was a push that failed to send has no record at all**, and the operator finds out by not finding out. So stdout is not a fallback, it is unconditional: one JSON object per line, because a log shipper, `jq` and a person at three in the morning can all read that without a parser.
  - **Each sink fails alone, and the test is the point of the file.** A fanout with a broken sink between two working ones must leave both working ones untouched — the phone being unreachable is *exactly* when the file on disk matters. Failures are returned as a list rather than an error, because there is nothing the caller should do differently: the alert has already happened.
  - **A hanging sink is a failing sink.** Every network output is bounded, and the test blocks a server forever to prove the monitor does not block with it.
  - **A fork must not arrive on a phone looking like an enrolment.** `ntfy` priorities are mapped from the level — `urgent`, `high`, `default` — because the whole point of a push is that the important one wakes somebody, and a test requires an info and a critical not to look alike.
  - **The rule name goes in the Home Assistant payload and the Alertmanager labels.** "Turn the office light red on a fork" has to match on *which rule fired*, not parse a sentence; `alertname` and `severity` are the names Alertmanager's own routing trees expect.
  - **Email is split where the honesty is.** The message it builds is tested — headers, the blank line, a subject triageable from a lock screen — and the five-line `net/smtp` call is a seam, because there is no logic behind it worth mocking and rather a lot of configuration only a real server can validate. Header values have their newlines stripped: an alert is not attacker input, but it is *generated* text, and a stray newline in a header is how one message becomes two.
  - **A deadlock in my own test, worth naming because it is the usual one.** `httptest.Server.Close` waits for outstanding handlers, so a handler blocked on a channel closed by a *later* defer hangs the test rather than the code. Defer order is release-then-close.
  - Go: 49 packages, 575 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.5 the status page — and the fixture had a device built to break it.**
  - **The security property is not obvious until you look at what is on the page.** A device's labels are chosen by **whoever enrolled it**, and the suite ships a device called `escaped` whose labels contain `<`, `>` and `&` — a fixture written in Phase 1 for the canonical-JSON vectors, sitting there ready. A page that let a label become markup would be a network where *enrolling a device is enough to rewrite what everybody reads*. Alerts are the same problem from another direction: a subject is a peer's name, a message carries a log operator's origin.
  - **`html/template` is the feature, not a formality**, and the test proves the difference rather than the spelling: swapping it for `text/template` turns both escaping tests red, naming the exact strings that escaped. The awkward label must also still be **readable** — escaped rather than dropped — because an operator who cannot see what a device is actually called has lost the thing the page is for.
  - **Read-only, like every surface Norn puts on the tunnel.** Anything but `GET`/`HEAD` is `405`: a status page answers questions and takes no instructions.
  - **A monitor with nothing to show says so.** "nothing verified yet — this monitor has not managed to read the log since it started" beats a blank screen, which is indistinguishable from a broken one.
  - **The device table is judged at the checkpoint's time**, the same rule §5.3 gave the enforcement path in 4.8 — so the page and the peer table cannot disagree about who is expired.
  - **The recent entries use `plainlog.Describe`**, the renderer `norn status` already uses, so the page and the terminal never describe the same log differently.

- **5.6 `norn monitor` — and one round over the fixture fires four of §3's rules.**
  - **A real run, not a claim:** `monitor -once` against the shipped log produces `new-entry` × 59, `governance-entry` × 16, `rejected-entry` × 54 and `checkpoint-age`, each as a sentence — *"enroll: signer 83eba1f8 enrolled pi-cam (wireguard 651e98a3)"*. The gate line wants one notification per rule during a lab run; this is as close as a repository can get, and it is what makes that countable at all.
  - **A misspelled setting is refused rather than ignored.** `ntfyy = "…"` stops the monitor from starting, because a typo in an output URL is otherwise a monitor that **silently never pages** — and the operator finds out during the incident. The same `Undecoded` check `nornd` uses.
  - **The zero config is a working monitor.** Every output is optional and stdout is unconditional, so the smallest `monitor.toml` that does anything is a trust file and a log.
  - **A dead output does not stop a round, and is not swallowed either.** The failing sink is named on stderr while stdout still gets everything — an output nobody knows is broken is an output nobody is reading.
  - **Failing to bind the status page *is* fatal**, and the asymmetry is deliberate: an operator who asked for a page and did not get one goes looking for it during an incident, which is the worst possible moment to discover a typo in a port. A failed *delivery* is news; a page that was never going to exist is a misconfiguration.
  - **A name collision the compiler caught**, worth a line only because it is the shape of thing a test file can hide: the test's helper and the config struct were both `monitorConfig`.
  - Go: 49 packages, 586 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **5.7 the Phase 5 gate audit — one line closed, one command missing, and the rest honestly a lab's.**
  - **The phase divides cleanly and the audit says which side each line falls on.** Five gate lines: one is pure lab (`make demos` — `lab/` and `demo/` are empty directories, and their contents are containers, a patched Headscale and NetBird, and rendered tapes), three have complete software halves, and one names a command that does not exist.
  - **`norn audit --by` is missing, and two documents name it.** `ENROLLMENT.md` R2 spells it out — *"`norn audit --by <kid> --since <t>` lists everything that key signed"* — and `LAB.md` D2 uses it in the demo. It is the first thing an operator runs when a signer key is stolen: not "revoke it", but **"what did they do with it"**. That is a missing feature rather than an untested one, so it is 5.8 rather than a footnote — the same call the 4.8 audit made about `/norn/evidence`.
  - **D3 closes outright**, which is the first Phase 5 line to close: a node with an unreachable log adopts a peer's checkpoint (4.5d), a fork produces evidence on the node that caught it *and* on every node it tells (4.6, 4.9), and `norn verify-evidence` checks that evidence offline against a trust file alone, refusing four kinds of fabrication (4.6a). Running it with tapes is the lab's, but no line of it is unproven.
  - **D1's software half is complete and was built in Phase 3**, before the demo existed to ask for it: owner mode never installs an unproven key, an injected key is gone after exactly one cycle, `CONTROL_PLANE_FIGHT` fires on a key that keeps coming back, and quarantine takes the interface out of service. What the demo adds is the *measurement* and the three control planes.
  - **D2's point survives the missing command.** "A valid-looking enrolment is still loud" is already true: the monitor reports every entry at info and every governance entry as critical, so the rogue enrolment appears whether or not anybody runs `audit`. The command is for the *next* question.
  - **Nothing was ticked on the strength of a plausible-sounding half.** Four lines stay open with a sentence each saying exactly which half is true and which needs a container, a phone or a stopwatch.
  - A documentation checkpoint: no code changed, and the suite stands at 49 packages, 586 tests, green.

- **5.8 `norn audit --by` — the question a revoked key cannot answer.**
  - **R2 puts this *before* the `signer_remove`, and that ordering is the point.** Removing a stolen key stops the next thing; this finds the last things. It is also the one answer a transparency log exists to make possible: a control plane with an admin console can tell you the current state, but it cannot tell you what a key did three weeks ago, because nothing kept the receipts. Over the fixture it reports *"entry 11 · signer 83eba1f8 revoked pi-cam (decommissioned)"* and seventeen others.
  - **A malformed key id is refused, and that is the most important line in the file.** `"This key signed nothing"` is the most dangerous wrong answer this command can give, and a typo would produce it **every time** — quietly, reassuringly, and at exactly the moment somebody is deciding whether they have a problem. Sixteen hex characters or an error.
  - **`--since` carries a warning about itself.** It filters on `ts`, which is set by *whoever signed the entry* — so if the key was stolen, the thief controls that field and can place an entry outside the window. The command says so on screen and tells the operator to run without it. A convenience that can hide evidence has to admit it.
  - **Rejected entries are shown under `-all`, marked as such.** A stolen key's *attempts* matter whether or not they worked, and "it is in the log, and it changed nothing" is a different fact from "it never happened" — the log carries both, which is the whole reason it is a log.
  - **A key that signed nothing gets a plain sentence**, not an empty list. After a scare, that is the answer somebody is hoping for, and it should read like an answer rather than like a command that failed to find its input.
  - Go: 49 packages, 592 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green.

- **6.1 freezing `SPEC.md` — two promises the document was still making, and one it could finally keep.**
  - **§13 said "Resolution is pending a decision" eight checkpoints after the decision.** The `enroll` device-signature problem was settled in 4.1c — the device signs its own **request**, the signer embeds it verbatim — and §4.3, §5.1 and §16 were all rewritten that way. Only §13 never heard. A reader implementing from the frozen spec would have found a live open item pointing at a `BUILD-LOG` entry that closes it.
  - **§12.1 still owed a fork-evidence vector "by Phase 4".** Phase 4 shipped without it, so the last thing `GOSSIP.md` sends to witness operators and blog posts was the one artifact only the implementation that reads it had ever produced. `fork/evidence.cbor` and `fork/conflicting.checkpoint` now ship: a second branch of the same network diverging at entry 40, signed by the same log key and cosigned by the same witnesses, checkable with nothing but `trust.cbor`.
  - **The generator's first attempt was wrong, and 4.6a's guard caught it.** I built the "fork" as `entries[:40]` — a **prefix**, not a fork — and `forkevidence.Build` refused it by name: *"these two heads agree at size 40; a log and its own earlier self is not a fork"*.
