That guard was written precisely so a node could not make this accusation; it turns out it also stops the project shipping a vector that proves nothing.
  - **"Not vectored" is now an empty list**, and what replaced it is two caveats rather than a silence: the fixture's cosignatures prove the *format* (an unmodified witness is proven separately, in `internal/interop`), and the fork evidence proves the *artifact is checkable* — it is not evidence that any log has ever equivocated. A vector proves a format, not a fact about the world.
  - **A mechanical guard, so this cannot happen again.** A spec marked `Status: **v1**` may not contain `NOT YET IMPLEMENTED`, `pending a decision`, `TODO`, `TBD` or an `owed by | Phase` row. Both of the things this checkpoint found were of exactly that shape, and both had been true when written — which is what makes them invisible. The test names the line number.
  - **Frozen is defined where a reader will look**: "a second implementation may be written against this document and the vectors beside it, and neither will change under it."
  - Go: 49 packages, 595 tests; `go test`, staticcheck and both halves of `vectors-check` green — the Go half now covering `fork/` as well.

- **6.2 the vectors stand alone — and the document a second implementer reads first was wrong.**
  - **The suite's README said the whole directory came from the Python reference.** By then `served/`, `proofs/` and `fork/` came from this project's Go and `anchors/` from `python-opentimestamps`. Provenance is the *first* thing a reader needs, because it decides how much a vector proves — a file the Go must match is a different kind of evidence from one the Go produced — and the one sentence stating it had quietly become false. Three provenances, in a table, with what each is worth.
  - **Sixteen of seventeen top-level entries were undescribed.** The README was thirteen lines, twelve of which were about `anchors/`, because that was the last thing added and the only thing anybody had thought to explain. A test now fails if anything in the directory is unnamed in the README: *a file a second implementer cannot identify is one they cannot use*.
  - **`SHA256SUMS`, for the reader who does not have the generators.** `make vectors-check` regenerates and diffs, which catches a vector that drifted from its generator; checksums catch one edited by hand — and fail with the *filename*, which a diff of 293 files does not.
  - **The generator hashed its own temporary file.** `xargs … > SHA256SUMS.tmp` creates the file before `find` lists the directory, so the first run produced 294 entries for 293 files, and the guard caught it immediately. The temp file now lives outside the tree being hashed.
  - **The guard's first catch was the sentence documenting it.** I wrote the `SHA256SUMS` section into the README *after* generating the sums, so the README's own hash changed and the test failed by name. Which is the behaviour asked for, arriving about ninety seconds after it was written.
  - **And `vectors-check` refused the new file until it was declared**, exactly as it refused `anchors/` in 4.7a. `SHA256SUMS` is `KEEP`: nothing generates it from the vectors, it is generated *from* them.
  - **"Using them" is an ordering, not an inventory.** Start where nothing is needed but Ed25519 and SHA-256, then replay, then tiles — and the half most implementations get wrong is named as such: `trust-bad/`, `requests-bad/` and `log/*.reason` are files that **must be refused**, for the stated reason.

- **6.3 the README — the one document nothing was checking.**
  - **It said "planning complete, not yet code" through six phases of work.** Written 2026-09-18 and true for about a day. By the time anyone read it there were 49 packages and 610 tests, a frozen spec and a vector suite with three provenances, and the first line a stranger saw said none of that existed. A spec is checked by its vectors and a plan by its gates; the README had nothing, so it drifted in the one direction nobody notices — *downwards*, understating what was there, which is the polite failure but still a false statement.
  - **So the countable claims are now checked on every build** (`internal/readmedoc`). The package counts, the test count, and the **14 of 38 gate lines** ratio are parsed out of the prose and compared against the tree and against `PLAN.md`. A README that states no countable claim fails too: silence is how the last one drifted.
  - **It caught its own drift inside a minute.** Writing the checks added eleven test functions, so the number the README had just been given — 609 — was wrong before the file was saved. 610. That is the whole argument for the package, delivered unprompted.
  - **The first checker was brittle about line wrapping** and demanded `14 of 38 gate lines` on one line with no emphasis markers. That is a checker dictating prose. The regexes now tolerate wraps and `*`/`_`, because the author of a README should be free to wrap a sentence.
  - **The commands in it are run, and their output is checked.** `Transcripts` parses the `console` blocks; a test builds `cmd/norn` into a temp dir, runs every `./norn …` line from the repo root, and fails if one errors or stops printing a line the README promises. A quickstart that no longer works is worse than none — it teaches a stranger to expect the wrong thing, at the exact moment they are deciding whether to keep reading.
  - **The quickstart leads with the fork, not the happy path.** `norn verify` on the served vectors is table stakes; `norn verify-evidence` on `fork/evidence.cbor` prints **FORK PROVEN** offline, from a trust anchor alone, needing neither the network running nor the accuser trusted. That is the thesis in one command, and it runs in a fresh clone with no daemon.
  - **Five mutations, five reds**: a broken vector path, an output line drifted by one entry, "blockchain" in the tagline, a rival renamed out of the comparison, and `production-ready` added while gates are open. Restored, green.
  - **The comparison table is by product, not by camp.** `PLAN.md` argued in camps — coordination servers, offline PKI, Tailnet Lock — which is the right shape for deciding what to build and the wrong one for a reader asking *should I use this instead of what I have*. Eight questions against Tailnet Lock / Nebula / NetBird / Norn, sourced to `PRIOR-ART.md`, and the last row is **"would I run it on something that matters today?"** — where Norn's answer is **no**, and the other three say yes.
  - **The gate line stays open.** It asks for review by somebody who has run at least two of them; I have run none of the three. The table says so in the text underneath it rather than quietly hoping nobody asks.
  - **"Who this is not for" is five paragraphs of reasons to close the tab**, including the one that costs most: Norn's failure direction is to refuse peers it cannot prove, so a log outage plus a strict policy is an outage. A reader who needs the network up more than correct should learn that from me, in the README, and not from their own network at 03:00.


- **6.4 `PUBLICATION.md` — the plan had aged into a set of false directions.**
  - **It sent reviewers to `contrib/verify-py/`, which was never created**, and told them to run `make demos`, which has no target and, per the Phase 3 gate, *cannot* have one until `lab/` exists. The Python reference implementation it meant is `contrib/norn-ref/`, and has been since Phase 1. Written 2026-09-18 when there was no code, nothing it promised could be wrong yet; by Phase 6 every one of its directions was a small trap laid for the exact people it was written to impress.
  - **So the inventory is now checked in both directions.** A table of what a reviewer can touch, ✅ or ⬜, where ✅ fails if the thing is missing and **⬜ fails if the thing exists**. The second half is the one that matters: understating is how `README.md` spent six phases claiming there was no code, and it is the failure mode nobody reports because it reads as modesty.
  - **An empty directory is not an artifact.** `lab/` and `demo/` have existed since the repository was created and contain nothing, so a plain `os.Stat` would have called them present and let the document promise six recorded demos that are not there. Directories count only when something is in them.
  - **`internal/readmedoc` became `internal/docclaims`.** The same question — does this document still describe the thing it claims to describe — asked of a second document. One package, two subjects, rather than two packages that drift apart.
  - **A foreign repository is not one of our paths.** The first pass flagged `transparency-dev/witness` as a missing directory, which is a checker inventing work. A mention is checked when its first segment is something this repository actually has at its root, so `contrib/verify-py/` stays caught and somebody else's project stays out of it.
  - **The comparison table is deleted from here.** It had drifted into a *different* table from the README's — six columns against four, different rows, written a day apart — and two comparison tables in one project eventually disagree in public. A test now fails if any table in `PUBLICATION.md` gives a column to more than one rival. The first version of that test flagged the *audience* table, which legitimately names Headscale and NetBird in one cell; the check is on header rows now, because a table with a column per product is a comparison and a sentence naming two of them is a sentence.
  - **The sequence is gated on preconditions, not on weeks.** Gate A is "the spec can be handed to a stranger" and is not passed until a verifier built from `SPEC.md` alone passes every vector — 6.5. Gate B is "the numbers are measured", which is every open gate in Phases 3–5. A week number slips quietly; a precondition does not, and the one thing that would waste the single first impression is publishing adjectives where numbers were promised.
  - **Ten obvious questions, with answers, tested for not being shrugs.** A question heading with fewer than fifteen words under it fails, and the three that always get asked first — is it a blockchain, why not Tailnet Lock, who do I have to trust — must be among them. The answers concede the things that are true: no adversary has attacked it, a log outage plus a strict policy is an outage, and the threat model should be checked rather than believed.
  - **Six mutations, six reds**, after the first attempt at one of them mutated the inventory row instead of the prose and so proved a different test. Claiming a missing thing exists, claiming an existing thing is missing, a pitch that drifts from the README's tagline, a second comparison table, an answer reduced to "No.", and prose pointing at a path nobody created.
  - **Three Phase 6 gate lines remain open and none of them is mine to close**: the cold-model verifier is 6.5, one needs a person outside this homelab, and one needs a reviewer who has run at least two of Tailnet Lock, Nebula and NetBird. I have run none of the three, and both documents now say so in the text rather than hoping nobody asks.


- **2026-09-20 · 6.5 cold-model gate run 5 — `SPEC SUFFICIENT WITH GAPS`, and the frozen spec has to be reopened.**
  - **The setup, as for runs 1–4:** a fresh agent, a sealed copy holding `SPEC.md` and `testdata/vectors/` and nothing else, barred from `~/norn` and from searching for it. Go, standard library only, its own CBOR codec, ~2,800 lines, 58 variant switches.
  - **597 of 597 shipped checks passed, all 293 files matched `SHA256SUMS`, and all 107 negatives were refused for the reason they name.** The core — encoding profile, entry schema, preimages, replay, reason codes, canonical JSON, trust file, requests, bech32m — came back byte-exact from the prose on the first attempt. That is the part of the spec that works.
  - **It still fails, because passing was never the bar.** `PLAN.md` has said since run 3 that the bar is *a second implementer could not reasonably have got it wrong*. **G1 is that, observed rather than hypothesised**: §9.1 requires `policy` to parse as `c2sp.org/tlog-policy`, a grammar this document never defines, never lists among its normative references (§1 names six, not this one), and illustrates only through a §9.2 example that elides the two lines that matter behind a `…`. Written strictly from the prose, their parser **rejected this project's own `trust.cbor`**. A second implementer did get it wrong, on the reference artifact, at the first opportunity.
  - **G2 is run 4's defect one level up.** `NORN_NOTE_SIGBLIND`, `NORN_COSIG_BLIND` and `NORN_KEYID_BLIND` each pass 597/597, and pass jointly: **nothing in the shipped suite forces a verifier to check a single signed-note or cosignature signature.** §12.1 advertises those negatives — an unsigned checkpoint, one signed by another key, one under another origin, a cosignature short, a forged one carrying a named witness's key id — and every one of them lives in a Go unit test rather than in a file a second implementer receives. The entry layer is now strong (signature blindness fails 14 checks there, a missing domain prefix 134); the note layer above it is exactly where the entry layer was before D-043.
  - **G5 decides when a device stops being admitted, and the document says two things.** §5.3: expiry is judged against *the checkpoint time*. §10: `expired(now)`. And `SPEC.md` **never defines the checkpoint time** — a `tlog-checkpoint` body carries no timestamp, the cosignatures do, and the word that connects them is not in the document. The code and §5.3 already agree with A11 that witness time is the only trusted clock; the spec simply never says so, which leaves a second implementer to use the wall clock and quietly disagree about who is on the VPN.
  - **Verified before accepting, as run 4's claims were.** G1's missing reference, G2's absent negatives (the suite has `requests-bad/` and `trust-bad/` and no third), G5's two clocks, G7's "nine abort paths" against the twelve that ship, G11's `uint ≥ 1` against §5.2's "never `decode`", and G12's *"Reconcile before freezing v1"* still sitting in §8.1 of a spec whose §13 says "None." — each checked against the file rather than taken from the report.
  - **So v1 is reopened before it is published, and that is what a freeze is for.** `PUBLICATION.md` Gate A already blocks publication until a verifier built from the prose alone passes; nothing has been published, no second implementer is depending on these bytes, and a freeze that cannot take errata before first release is a pose. The defects are closed in 6.5a–6.5e and the gate is re-run in 6.5f, exactly as 0.7 and 0.9b and 0.13 followed runs 1, 2 and 4.
  - **The report is worth reading whole** (`SPEC-DEFECTS-5.md`): 13 defects, **30 decisions the prose did not make for it**, five things it asked not to be broken — §4.1's map-key-order warning, §4.0's negative-vector construction rule, all of §14, §5.2's counting paragraph, and §6's OTS digest definition, which it reconstructed from prose alone and checked against `anchors/checkpoint-90.ots` — and a plain statement of what it could not cover: `fork/`, the `anchors/` chain, §10 beyond `served/`, and the proven set, because `GOSSIP.md` and `ENFORCEMENT.md` are not part of the sealed copy.
  - **Severity still has not decreased monotonically.** Run 3 passed with gaps, run 4 found the worst defect to date, run 5 found the same class of defect one layer higher and a prose defect that breaks the reference artifact. Five runs in, the lesson holds: the vectors get stronger where they were attacked last, and the attack moves up a layer.


- **6.5a G1 and G5 — the two defects that produced divergence rather than doubt.**
  - **G1 was not a missing sentence, it was a missing dependency.** §9.1 requires `policy` to parse as `c2sp.org/tlog-policy`, and that specification was not among the six this document lists as the ones it reuses. Run 5 implemented `log <key>` and `witness <name> <key>` — the only reading §9.2's `log …\nwitness pi …` supports — and its parser rejected the suite's own `trust.cbor`. The real lines carry a URL.
  - **Fetched, not recalled.** The grammar now restated in §9.1 comes from `c2sp.org/tlog-policy` itself: four directives, whitespace that is ASCII space and tab **only**, `any` = `k = 1`, `all` = `k = n`, exactly one `quorum`, and the reserved name `none`. It also answers the question run 5 had to guess: the `witness` directive is matched on its **own short name** (`pi`), not the note name inside the verifier key (`norn-witness/pi`).
  - **Both URLs are optional in the grammar and present in every Norn policy**, which is the sharp edge: a parser that stops reading a line after the key *parses successfully* and has misread the file. That sentence is now in the spec, because it is the specific way a second implementer gets this wrong without noticing.
  - **§9.2 now shows the policy the suite ships**, byte for byte, and a test compares the two. Eliding a hash in an example costs a reader nothing; eliding the one field that carries grammar cost run 5 its first attempt.
  - **`TestEveryReusedSpecificationIsListed` closes the class, not the instance.** Every `c2sp.org/…` the body requires must appear in the head list. Had it existed in 6.1, the freeze would have caught this.
  - **G5: the document specified two clocks for the one decision that ejects a device from the VPN.** §5.1 said "the checkpoint time", §10 said `expired(now)`, and — worse than the contradiction — **neither was defined**, because a `tlog-checkpoint` body carries no timestamp at all. The only timestamps in the artifact are the `tlog-cosignature` lines, and the document never connected them to expiry.
  - **The code was right and silent about it.** `freshness.QuorumTime` already computes the newest instant whose cosignatures satisfy the policy, `proven.EvaluationTime` already uses it, and `proven.IsProven` already treats `expires == T` as expired. §5.3 now defines `T` in those terms — quorum time, derived from the cosignatures, `expires != 0 && expires <= T`, and **no clock at all** when the policy is satisfied at no instant, including `quorum none`. Six phases of implementation decisions that the spec had never written down.
  - **The boundary was already pinned** (`proven_test.go` asserts `expires == now` is not proven), so prose and code agree today. **It is still not vectored**, and a second implementer receives vectors rather than this repository's Go tests — which is G2's lesson exactly one defect early. 6.5d now carries G5's fixture.
  - **Four mutations, four reds**: dropping `tlog-policy` from the reuse list, re-eliding §9.2's policy, returning §10 to the wall clock, and weakening the definition so it no longer derives `T` from anything.


- **6.5b G2 — the note layer had no negatives at all, and §12.1 said it did.**
  - **Eight refusals were claimed and zero shipped.** §12.1 promised an unsigned checkpoint, one signed by another key, one under another origin, a short quorum, an uncosigned head and a forged cosignature. Every one of them existed — as a Go test in this repository. A second implementer receives `testdata/vectors/`, not `internal/logclient/client_test.go`, so what they received was a suite in which **every note was valid**. Run 5's `NORN_NOTE_SIGBLIND`, `NORN_COSIG_BLIND` and `NORN_KEYID_BLIND` each passed 597/597, and passed jointly.
  - **`checkpoints-bad/` now ships the same eight refusals as bytes**, generated by `cmd/norn-genlog` from the body the store actually signed. Each is served in place of `served/checkpoint` over the honest tiles under the shipped `trust.cbor`, so a refusal can only come from the checkpoint.
  - **The generator asserts it reproduced the served signature before building anything on it.** Re-signing the extracted body must byte-match what the store published; if it does not, every negative below it would be a negative against a checkpoint the suite does not serve. Ed25519 is deterministic, so this is cheap and it is the difference between a vector and a guess.
  - **Only two of the eight are cryptographic, and they are the two that matter.** `04-forged-log-signature` and `08-forged-cosignature` carry signature lines of the right shape, under the right key id, over the right body, that do not verify. The other six — missing, wrong key, wrong origin, too few, none, unnamed — are all reachable without ever calling Ed25519, which is precisely how the entry layer looked before D-043.
  - **So each of the two is proved load-bearing rather than merely refused.** `internal/tlog` asserts `04` and `served/checkpoint` share a body, an origin and a key id and differ only in signature bytes; `internal/cosign` asserts `08` carries two lines naming witnesses the policy lists — a threshold of two, met by counting — while verification refuses it. A vector that is refused for some *other* reason proves nothing about signature checking, and that is exactly the trap G3 and G4 describe elsewhere in the same report.
  - **The strict rule was already specified and already implemented.** §10 says every cosignature by a named witness *must verify*, and `cosign.Verify` refuses the whole checkpoint rather than merely not counting the bad line. My first test expected the lenient reading and was wrong; the spec settled it in two lines I had read too quickly.
  - **A test helper had been writing a policy the grammar does not describe**: `log <origin> <vkey>`, where `tlog-policy` says `log <vkey> [<url>]`. Harmless — the log line's key is not what the quorum counts — but it taught the wrong grammar to anybody reading the tests, three days after 6.5a put the right one in the spec. Fixed.
  - **Three mutations, three reds** — an un-forged `04`, an un-forged `08`, a case dropped from `cases.json`. **And the restore failed**: `git checkout` cannot restore files that are new and untracked, so the "restored" run came back red and stayed red. `make vectors-log` rebuilt all eight byte-identically, which is the argument for a deterministic generator stated more sharply than any comment could.
  - **§12.1 now cites the files by name, and says plainly what is still missing**: `quorum none` accepting an uncosigned head needs a second trust anchor and is **not vectored**. Claiming coverage that does not exist is the defect this checkpoint closed; ending it with a fresh claim of the same kind would be a poor joke.


- **6.5c G3, G4 and G8 — three negatives that were refused for the wrong reason.**
  - **The defect is not that they were accepted; it is that they were refused anyway.** Run 5 deleted §4.0's map-key cap, §16.1's `expires > ts` and D-032's proof-of-possession outright, and passed 597/597 each time. All three vectors were patched *after* signing, so Ed25519 refused them before the rule they were named for was ever reached. A negative that cannot distinguish a conforming implementation from one missing a rule is decoration.
  - **Our own implementation refuses two of them by the right rule, which is what made this easy to dismiss.** `norn`'s decoder enforces the CBOR profile and the field rules before it verifies a signature, so `expires-at-ts` and `oversize-label-key` come back with exactly the message §12.1 promises. That is an accident of check ordering in *this* implementation, and run 5's verified the signature first. The vector has to pin the rule for **both** orderings, which is precisely what §4.0's construction rule already said and what nobody had applied to `requests-bad/`.
  - **So each is now the only thing wrong with its file.** `expires-at-ts` is patched and **re-signed**; `oversize-label-key` is re-signed over the *patched preimage*, since a 24-byte map key cannot canonicalise away. Both constructions assert their own signature before returning, so a future edit that forgets to re-sign fails `make vectors` rather than shipping.
  - **`46-bad-rotate-no-new-key-sig` carried no device signature at all**, so it died at the *current*-key check, three checks before D-032. It now carries a valid current-key signature and nothing by the incoming `device_pk`, which is the shape the name always claimed. The reason code is unchanged — both checks yield `no-device-sig` — so the manifest, the index and the replayed state are untouched.
  - **Why that one matters most of the three:** the fixture turns `rotate_needs_signer` on at index 29, and under that setting an implementation missing D-032 lets two signers install an attacker-chosen identity key on a device with nobody proving they hold it. That is the entire property the rule exists for, and it was untested.
  - **The Go tests assert reachability, not refusal.** For the requests: the file's signature must verify over the bytes that actually ship. For the rotate: a valid signature by the current key must be present and none by the incoming one. A test that only checked "is refused" would have passed against all three broken vectors, which is how they survived since Phase 0.
  - **`checkpoints-bad/` was unclaimed, and 6.5b did not notice.** The Python generator refuses to run when a directory is in neither `OWNED` nor `FOREIGN`, because a path in neither escapes pruning and rots. I added the directory to the Makefile in 6.5b and not to the generator; `make vectors` caught it the first time it ran. The guard did exactly its job, one checkpoint late.
  - **Changing one rejected entry changed the Bitcoin anchor.** Entry 46's bytes moved the tree root, which moved the checkpoint body, which invalidated `anchors/checkpoint-90.ots` — a file deliberately written by `python-opentimestamps` and never regenerated by us. It was rebuilt with the reference implementation from its own clone, which is the only way that vector is allowed to change, and is a coupling worth stating: **no log vector can be touched without re-stamping the anchor.**
  - **Two mutations, two reds — and the restore failed for the second time in two checkpoints.** `git checkout` returns a vector to its *committed* state, which mid-checkpoint is the broken one I am replacing. In this repository the generators are the source of truth and `make vectors && make vectors-log` is the restore. Having now walked into both halves of this trap — untracked files in 6.5b, stale tracked ones here — the rule is simply: never restore a vector from git during a checkpoint that is regenerating it.


- **6.5d-i G9 — a limit stated in a document, contradicted by the format, and enforced by an encoder that wrapped.**
  - **§4.0 allowed an entry of 65536 bytes and §6 cannot serve one.** Entry bundles are uint16-length-prefixed, so 65535 is the largest entry that can exist in a `tlog-tiles` bundle at all. The document permitted an entry that was valid and unpublishable.
  - **And `EncodeBundle` did not refuse it — it truncated.** `uint16(len(raw))` on a 65536-byte entry writes a length header of **zero**, so the bundle decodes as a zero-length entry followed by garbage: a different log, silently, from input the spec said was fine. That is worse than the off-by-one run 5 reported, and it was reachable only because the two numbers disagreed. `MAX_ENTRY_BYTES` is now 65535 in the spec, in the Go and in the Python, the encoder refuses what it cannot frame, and a test asserts the profile's limit **equals** the bundle's — because the failure was never one constant, it was two constants nobody compared.
  - **The depth convention was never stated.** "Nesting depth is at most 8" says nothing about whether the top-level map is 0 or 1, nor whether an `enroll`'s embedded request continues the count or restarts it — two implementations differing by one or by four, both believing they conform. §4.0 now fixes the top-level map at depth 1 and says the embedded request **restarts at 1**, with the reason: the two documents are decoded by two calls, and a limit that leaked across them would make an entry's validity depend on the shape of something it merely carries.
  - **The justification had outlived the schema.** §4.0 explained the depth of 8 by "an entry's `body.labels` value", and §4.3 moved the device's claims into the request in Phase 0 — §12.1 even says so, two hundred lines further down: "no *entry* body has a free-form map left". The same stale sentence sat in the Python reference's own comment. Both now name the request's `labels` at depth 3 of the inner document.
  - **Both limits are now vectored, in `entries-hostile/`.** §4.0 says neither is reachable by a valid entry, which is *why* nothing tested them and why run 5 deleted both and passed 597/597. They exist for input that is not an entry, so that is what they are tested with: a header promising 65536 bytes, which must be refused on length before anything is allocated, and 200 nested arrays that are deterministic CBOR throughout, so nothing but the depth rule can refuse them. Built as raw bytes rather than through the encoder — an encoder that enforces the profile cannot express the hostile case, which is the point of the case.
  - **Four mutations, four reds**, two of them run 5's own: the profile limit drifting back above the bundle's, the encoder truncating again, `NORN_NODEPTH` and `NORN_NOLIMITS`. The last two now fail where they passed against every vector in the suite.


- **6.5d-ii G10 — §5.1 rested a normative claim on vectors that did not exist.**
  - **"The order is normative: several vectors are simultaneously wrong in more than one way, so the first matching step decides the reason code."** For four of its precedence pairs there was no such vector, and run 5 swapped each order in turn and reproduced the whole manifest. Only the governance step-7-before-8 case was genuinely pinned, by `85-bad-undersigned-malformed`.
  - **Four entries, each wrong in exactly two ways**: an unknown type inside an envelope that is also not closed (step 3 before step 4); an `after` from the future on an entry with no signatures at all (step 6 before step 7); an `enroll` for an active `device_id` signed by one signer where two are required (`threshold` before `dup-id`); and a `rotate` taking another device's live WireGuard key with one signer while `rotate_needs_signer` is on (`threshold` before `dup-wg`).
  - **The fourth was wrong in only one way, and my own test said it was fine.** It took `nas`'s *original* WireGuard key — which `nas` rotated away from at entry 35, so the key was not active for anybody and `dup-wg` could never have fired. The structural check I had written only counted signatures. **The mutation caught it**: swapping the rotate rule's order left the manifest unchanged, which is the exact signature of a vector that pins nothing. The vector now uses `nas2`, and the test asserts the second fault is *real* — that the key is in `active_wg` and owned by another device.
  - **That is the defect of this whole checkpoint, committed by the person fixing it, and found only because the mutation was run.** A precedence vector that is wrong in one way is indistinguishable from an ordinary negative, and no amount of reading it tells you which you have.
  - **Four mutations, four reds** — run 5's `NORN_TYPE_FIRST`, `NORN_THRESHOLD_FIRST`, `NORN_ENROLL_ORDER_SWAP` and `NORN_ROTATE_ORDER_SWAP`, each of which passed 597/597 before today.
  - **The reference's own guard objected, correctly.** `test_after_never_exceeds_the_index` forbids a stale `after` on any negative except the one whose purpose it is, because gate run 4 found four vectors rejected by a leftover `after` rather than by their own rule. My new entry is the second vector whose purpose *is* a stale `after`, so it joins the allowlist by name — a guard that had to be told, rather than one that was weakened.
  - **The fixture is 94 entries now, and that number was written down in fourteen places.** Tests asserting `size == 90`, tile paths ending `.p/90`, `anchors/90.ots`, `checkpoint-90.ots` in three packages, a status test counting "ago" lines, a submission test expecting 91, a concurrency test expecting `90+n`, SPEC §12, both READMEs and `DECISIONS.md`. Every one of them is now **derived** — from the manifest, from the served checkpoint's size line, from `len(entries)`. The OTS generator derives the anchor's name from the body it stamps and deletes the stale one, because §6 says the file is `<size>.ots` and a constant there would keep stamping a name the log no longer serves.
  - **One thing deliberately left constant:** `sample.ots` is a proof over an arbitrary digest, exercising the parser against bytes this project did not write. I briefly tied it to the fixture's size, which would have churned it on every future entry for no gain. It stays pinned to its original string.


- **6.5d-iii G5's fixture — the rule that decides who is on the VPN had no vector at all.**
  - **`state.json` is clock-independent on purpose**, which is the right design and the reason nothing in 300-odd files exercised expiry. Run 5 called it the one gap with a direct production consequence, and it was right: `expires` is what ejects a device, and the only thing pinning it was a Go unit test — the exact evidence a second implementer does not receive.
  - **`proven/cases.json` is the admitted set at four evaluation times**: no clock, one second before the deadline, **exactly on it**, and one second after. Both implementations compute it independently and agree, including the boundary where `expires == T` and the device is **out**.
  - **The deadlines are read from the state, not written down.** A fixture that hardcoded the instant would stop testing the boundary the moment the entry behind it changed — which is how `checkpoint-90.ots` and fourteen other literals went stale one checkpoint ago.
  - **Three of the four cases are load-bearing against this implementation.** `expires < T` instead of `<= T` — the off-by-one two implementations actually make — turns `at-the-deadline` red, and so does ignoring expiry altogether.
  - **The fourth cannot be, and saying so is the point.** Removing the explicit zero-clock check changes no answer here, because Norn represents "no clock" as the zero time, whose Unix seconds are more negative than any `expires` a uint64 can hold. No fixture can catch that, and a mutation test that quietly passed would have let me claim four where there are three.
  - **What the no-clock case *does* catch is the mistake a second implementer will actually make**: finding no witness quorum and quietly falling back to the machine's own clock, which is precisely what A11 forbids. The fixture's deadline is in the past, so such a verifier drops a device the fixture says is proven — and a test now asserts that difference exists, so if the deadline ever stops being in the past the suite says so rather than silently losing the property.


- **6.5e-i G6 — the one artifact meant for strangers was the one with no format.**
  - **§11 required a node to write fork evidence and never said what evidence is.** Seven CBOR keys — `version`, `held`, `held_root`, `conflicting`, `size`, `proof`, `policy` — and not one of those names appeared in `SPEC.md`. §15 deferred the format to `GOSSIP.md`, which is not shipped beside the vectors and is not in the normative-reference list. Run 5 could not check the evidence vector **at all**, and said so plainly.
  - **The format is §11.1 now, in the section that requires it**, with the four-step reading procedure and the reason it lives here rather than in `GOSSIP.md`: evidence is the one thing this protocol produces *for somebody else* — a witness operator, a mailing list, a stranger — so it must be checkable by a reader holding the trust file and nothing more, with no running log and **no reason to trust whoever handed them the file**.
  - **The filename was not a contradiction, and the document should have said so.** A node names its own files `<unix>.evidence`, which is what §11 describes; the suite ships the same bytes under stable names so they can be referred to. One sentence, and a reader stops wondering which is wrong.
  - **`fork/bad-consistent` is the accusation a node must never be able to publish.** Every signature valid, both heads cosigned, and the roots agreeing at the smaller size — which is exactly why it is *not* a fork. `forkevidence.Build` refuses to produce it, which is correct and is also why no vector existed, so it is assembled by hand in the generator. It is the only thing in the suite that pins "conclude a fork **only if** the roots differ".
  - **`fork/bad-other-origin` is a real fork, of a network this anchor is not about.** Perfectly valid evidence, and none of this reader's business. It pins the step that is easiest to skip: applying §10 to **both** checkpoints in the file rather than taking the file's word for them.
  - **Two mutations, two reds** — accepting a log and its own earlier self, and trusting the checkpoints a file carries instead of verifying them. A third mutation I tried first was worthless: I changed the origin string but not the key, so the check that actually fires was never disabled. A mutation that passes proves nothing about the vector until you have checked it disabled what you meant.


- **6.5e-ii G7 — the table this specification asks to be judged on was wrong in six places.**
  - **§12.1 opens by saying an inaccurate coverage claim is "the same failure wearing a different hat"**, and then made six. A count of nine where twelve ship; four rows naming negatives that existed only as Go tests; a vector cited for a rule it did not reach. Four of the six were closed by 6.5b, 6.5c and 6.5e-i; this checkpoint closes the rest and stops the class recurring.
  - **The header contradicted itself, two paragraphs apart.** Line 6: "Everything normative here is vectored (§12)". Line 10: "§12.1 lists what is vectored and what is not. It does **not** claim every MUST is vectored". The second is the true one, so the first is gone, and §12.1's closing line with it.
  - **In its place, the list of what is not vectored**: §6's tampered and missing entry bundles, §8's four proof-bundle negatives, `quorum none` accepting an uncosigned head, §4.3's three independent authorisations for `revoke`, §14.1's ordering above the basic multilingual plane, §6's "no extension lines", §5.1's non-empty `params`, and the trust file's mandatory keys other than `name` and `policy`. Each of those is a rule a second implementer could drop and still pass everything shipped.
  - **The build now judges the table, in both directions.** Every vector path §12 or §12.1 cites must exist; every directory in the suite must be cited as a path. The first direction is what four rows of invented negatives would have failed; the second is what kept `SHA256SUMS` out of the file table for three checkpoints.
  - **Scoped to paths rooted in the suite.** The first version flagged `transparency-dev/formats`, `x/mod/sumdb/tlog` and `internal/interop` as missing vectors — libraries this project checks itself against, not files it ships. A checker that cannot tell somebody else's repository from its own invents work, which is the third time that exact correction has been needed.
  - **"Described" meant "the letters appear somewhere", and that was not enough.** The mutation that removed `proven/` from the table passed, because the word *proven* occurs in ordinary prose two hundred lines away. Both this check and the older README one now require the name to appear as a **backticked path**. A test that cannot fail is a test that was never run against a wrong document.
  - **Four mutations, four reds**: citing a vector that does not ship, dropping a shipped directory from the table, restoring the total-coverage claim, and the same drop against the suite's own README.
  - **§12's file table also gained what it had been quietly omitting**: `checkpoints-bad/`, `entries-hostile/`, `proven/`, `fork/`, `anchors/` and `SHA256SUMS` — and the provenance paragraph, which still said **two** provenances after `anchors/` made it three in 6.2.
  - **What I did not do, and said so in the document rather than here:** ship §6's and §8's negatives. They are a vector-writing job of the same size as 6.5b, so they are **6.5e-iv**, and until then §12.1 states plainly that those refusals are checked by this project's own tests and do not ship as files. Admitting a hole in the place a reader looks is the whole point of that section.


- **6.5e-iii G11, G12 and the cheap half of G13 — four sentences that could not all be true.**
  - **§8.1 claimed a domain the bundles do not carry.** "Deterministic CBOR per §4.0, domain `norn/proof/v1`" — over a six-key table with no domain in it, no signature for a prefix to separate, and the string appearing nowhere else in the document. The bundles are byte-canonical six-key maps, so it was emitted by nothing, and an implementer could not tell whether they were required to produce it or to check for it. A bundle is **not signed** — its authority is the checkpoint inside it — which is exactly why there is nothing for a domain to separate, and the section now says so.
  - **A test compares §8.1's table with a shipped bundle**, so the section cannot describe a format the suite does not ship. The six keys already agreed; it was only the sentence above them that did not.
  - **The freeze guard had the right idea and the wrong vocabulary.** `TestAFrozenSpecMakesNoPromises` has looked for "TODO", "TBD" and "pending a decision" since 6.1 — and §8.1's note said "**Reconcile before freezing v1**", which is none of those. It survived the freeze in a document whose §13 reads "Open items: None". The list now names that phrasing too. A guard is only as good as its vocabulary, and the thing it missed was a caveat written more politely than the ones it knew.
  - **So the deferral was decided rather than deferred again.** §8.1 is Norn's own format; byte compatibility with `c2sp.org/tlog-proof` was never established, so v1 does not claim it, and the reference list says the entry is there for comparison only. A later version may adopt that format outright. What it may not do is what earlier drafts did — name a specification the bytes had never been checked against.
  - **G11: §4.3 stated a rule that belongs to replay.** `thr (uint ≥ 1)` in a schema table, against §5.2's "the value is a well-formed uint, so it is never `decode`". An implementer taking the table at face value returns `decode` where `bad-thr` is required. The table now says `uint`, with a paragraph explaining that the range is a replay decision and naming the vectors for both halves.
  - **G13: `entry_hash` was defined over bytes that may not exist.** §4.0 said "the full envelope, deterministically encoded" while §5.1 records the hash at **step 1**, before the entry is decoded at step 2 — so an entry that never decodes had no definition at all. It is now "the entry **as received**, byte for byte", which is what both implementations already did and neither document said.
  - **§6's "no extension lines" is vectored now**, and it was enforced-but-unpinned: `checkpoints-bad/09-extension-line` carries a fourth body line, signed and cosigned exactly as correctly as the real checkpoint, so only the rule can refuse it. Tolerating a fourth line turns it red.
  - **What is left of G13 is 6.5e-v**, and §12.1's not-vectored list already names each item, so the hole is admitted where a reader looks rather than only here.


- **6.5e-iv — six refusals §12.1 had promised since Phase 2, shipped as files.**
  - **`proofs-bad/` is §8's four**, each differing from a valid bundle in exactly one thing: a bundle whose proof is for this index and whose entry is a different one; one carrying a perfectly valid checkpoint for **another network**; one with a single hash of the inclusion path flipped; and one whose checkpoint is signed by a key the anchor does not name. A verifier that skips one check accepts exactly one of them.
  - **`served-bad/` is §6's two**, and a served log is a *directory*, so the shape had to differ: each case names the path to replace with `file` or to delete with `remove`, over an otherwise honest copy of `served/`. The tampered bundle changes one byte inside one entry, so the bundle still frames correctly and only the tree it builds is wrong.
  - **The reason strings were guesses, and all four were wrong.** I wrote what I expected the refusals to say — "does not match", "not signed" — and the suite came back red four times over. The messages are read from the implementation now, which is the only way a `says` field means anything.
  - **Mutation found the served vectors are defended twice over.** Disabling the tile check left them refused; disabling the head-versus-entries comparison left them refused; only **both** together let the tampered bundle through. Two independent mechanisms catch it, which is worth knowing and is not something reading the code would have told me — I would have concluded after the first mutation that the vector was not load-bearing.
  - **And the proof vectors split cleanly**: carrying the inclusion proof without checking it admits `wrong-entry` and `tampered-proof`; skipping the checkpoint signature admits `foreign-checkpoint` and `wrong-log-key`. Four vectors, two independent rules, no overlap.
  - **§12.1's not-vectored list is two entries shorter**, and the §6 and §8 rows now name files instead of describing tests nobody receives. That list is the thing §12.1 exists for, and it is shorter because the hole was filled rather than because the claim was softened.


- **6.5e-v G13 — four rules stated and never refused by a file.**
  - **`revoke` has three *independent* authorisations** — a signer quorum, a root quorum, or the device itself — and the fixture's `revoke-by-roots` met the root quorum outright, so pooling all three counts into one gave the same answer on every entry. `bad-revoke-neither-quorum` carries **one signer of two and two roots of three**: short on every rule taken separately, and a quorum only if they are added together. Pooling them turns entry 94 from `threshold` into accepted.
  - **§5.1's `params` must be non-empty**, which was true in the code and refused by nothing. `bad-params-empty` is `decode`.
  - **§14.1's key order needed a character outside the basic multilingual plane.** UTF-8 bytewise order and UTF-16 code-unit order agree on every key the suite had, because they disagree **only across U+E000**: an astral character is a surrogate pair beginning `0xD800` in UTF-16, *below* U+E000, and begins `0xF0` in UTF-8, *above* it. `enroll-astral-labels` carries one key of each kind, and sorting in UTF-16 order now makes `state.json` differ byte-for-byte.
  - **Seven mandatory trust keys were mandatory by inference.** §9.1 marks exactly one key OPTIONAL, and only `name` and `policy` had ever been vectored missing — so an implementation treating `version`, `net`, `origin`, `genesis_hash`, `log_key`, `log_urls` or `witnesses` as optional passed the whole suite. `trust-bad/` is 25 cases now.
  - **The astral enrolment resurrected a device two reference tests depend on.** I enrolled `spare`, which is revoked in the fixture, and §5.3 allows re-enrolment to replace the record wholesale — so the Go suite stayed green while `make ref-test` failed twice with `'active' != 'revoked'`. The Python reference's tests are not a formality; they hold the fixture's *meaning*, which the manifest alone does not. The entry has its own device now.
  - **Four mutations, four reds**: pooling `revoke`'s quorums, allowing an empty `params` body, sorting object keys in UTF-16 order, and — from the other direction — the existing `TestSpecLogSizeIsCurrent` catching §12 still saying 94 entries.
  - **The disk filled mid-checkpoint**, and six tests failed with `no space left on device` from the linker rather than from anything in the code. 139 MiB free, a 1.8 GB Go build cache. Worth writing down because the failures looked like six unrelated regressions and were one full disk.


- **2026-09-20 · 6.5f cold-model gate run 6 — `SPEC INSUFFICIENT`, and the verdict got worse on purpose.**
  - **285 of 285 checks passed on the first run, every negative for its named reason, `SHA256SUMS` clean, and no vector disputed.** The sixth fresh agent, a sealed copy, its own CBOR codec and signed-note code, ~55 variant knobs. It confirmed the repairs hold: blinding entry signatures costs 12 checks, request signatures 11, note signatures 1, cosignatures 1, inclusion proofs 2, the genesis hash 2, the quorum 3, the role field 3, a lenient decoder 11. Everything runs 1–5 attacked is now attacked back.
  - **So it went one layer up, and the verdict fell.** Run 5 said `SUFFICIENT WITH GAPS`; run 6 says `INSUFFICIENT`. Severity has still not decreased monotonically after six rounds, and the reason is the same each time: the suite gets strong where it was last hit, and the next reader looks somewhere else. This round's somewhere-else is **the artifacts that cross trust boundaries** — fork evidence, checkpoints, policies, log keys — and the sentences that describe them from two places at once.
  - **H1 is mine, from 6.5e-i, and it is the worst thing in the report.** I wrote §11.1's four reader steps and shipped negatives for steps 1 and 4. Steps 2 and 3 — the conflicting checkpoint verifying, and the consistency proof chaining — are forced by nothing: `blind-consistency`, `skip-conflicting-verify` and `skip-size-equality` each pass 285/285. Run 6 forged an evidence file with an invented `held_root` and a corrupted proof; an honest build refuses it, a blind build calls it a genuine fork. §11 rule 4 makes a FORK permanent until an operator ships a new trust file, so that is **a remote unauthenticated permanent denial of service, through the one artifact §11.1 says arrives from strangers.** Writing the four steps and vectoring two of them is precisely the failure this project keeps finding in other people's documents.
  - **H2 is fatal on the shipped fixture and I never ran it.** §4.5 says a `policy` entry supersedes `norn.trust.policy` inside the log. Replaying the 97-entry fixture leaves `state.policy` holding `49-policy-escaping`'s text — the canonical-JSON escape torture string, which is not a `tlog-policy` at all. **Following the specification literally makes the reference network unverifiable**, and the vector that proves it has been in the suite since Phase 0, serving a different purpose. There is an unaddressed circularity underneath it: a node needs a policy to accept the checkpoint and the checkpoint to learn the policy.
  - **H10 is the one that should sting.** Run 5 reported two request negatives refused by their own broken signature rather than by the rule they name; 6.5c fixed **those two**. Run 6 found two more, and checking the whole directory afterwards found **at least eleven of fifteen**. 6.5b's own log says a fix should "close the class, not the instance", and one checkpoint later I did the opposite — fixed the named instances, never asked whether the class extended, and shipped it with a test that only knew about two names. 6.5g-vi enforces the property over the whole directory.
  - **Verified before accepting, as ever**: `state.policy`'s contents after replay, the two fork negatives and which steps they reach, §4.0's sentence naming `no-device-sig`, and the eleven request negatives — each checked against the files rather than taken from the report.
  - **What the report says the spec does well** is worth keeping in view while repairing it: §14's canonical JSON, §4.1's paragraph that shows the wrong order explicitly, §5.1's numbered precedence with the `bad-precedence-*` vectors behind it, §4.0's note on how to test its own rules, and §9.1's algorithm-`0x01`-versus-`0x04` trap.


- **6.5g-i H1 — I wrote four reader steps and vectored two of them.**
  - **The exact inversion of this project's thesis.** Both shipped evidence files use the non-degenerate proof branch, so the suite forced a reader to *not reject* a valid consistency proof and never once forced it to reject an invalid one. Run 6 put it better than I would have: "no finding without proof", backwards.
  - **And the consequence is the worst in the protocol.** §11 rule 4 makes a concluded fork **permanent** until an operator installs a new trust file. A reader that skips step 3 accepts an accusation any stranger can write — a remote, unauthenticated, permanent denial of service, delivered through the one artifact §11.1 itself says arrives from strangers. §11.1 now states that consequence where the steps are listed, because the argument for checking them is not "correctness", it is "otherwise anyone can turn your network off".
  - **Three vectors, one per unforced step, each of which must get *past* the earlier ones**: `bad-forged-proof` (both checkpoints verify and the sizes agree, so only the proof can refuse it — an invented `held_root` at a size the accuser cannot speak for), `bad-unverifiable-conflicting` (the held head is this network's and valid; the conflicting one is cosigned by nobody, so a reader that only checks the accuser's own head accepts it), and `bad-size-mismatch` (both verify; the file claims a disagreement at a size the conflicting head does not have).
  - **A test asserts each one reaches its own step**, rather than asserting it is refused. A negative that fails at step 1 proves nothing about step 3, and that is precisely how the two vectors I shipped in 6.5e-i managed to cover half the procedure while looking like coverage.
  - **Three mutations, three reds, one each**: `blind-consistency` fails only on `bad-forged-proof`, `skip-conflicting-verify` only on `bad-unverifiable-conflicting`, `skip-size-equality` only on `bad-size-mismatch`. Four steps, five negatives, one-to-one — which is the shape a reader can check at a glance and I did not build the first time.


## Decision needed — 6.5g-ii: does v1 apply in-log policy and log-key rotation, or only record it?

**The question.** `D-020` says `log_key` and `policy` entries "evolve trust
inside the log", with the log signing under old and new keys for an `overlap`
window so stale trust files still bootstrap. §4.5 repeats it: the `policy`
entry "supersedes" `norn.trust.policy` inside the log.

**Nothing implements it.** Every verifier path in this repository —
`logclient`, `agent`, `monitor` — takes `LogKeys` and `Policy` from the trust
file and never reads `state.log_keys` or `state.policy`. Those two fields are
recorded by replay and used by nobody. Cold gate run 6 found this from the
prose alone and rated the specification INSUFFICIENT partly on it (H2, H3).

**Two answers, and they are different products:**

- **(a) v1 applies rotation.** Implement the overlap window: a checkpoint is
  acceptable under the trust file's key *or* under a `log_key` the log itself
  published, for `overlap` after publication; likewise for the policy. This is
  what `D-020` decided and what §4.5 promises. It is a real feature — key
  validity windows, re-verification after replay, a second acceptance path —
  and every one of those is a place a verifier can be tricked.
- **(b) v1 records rotation and does not act on it.** `log_key` and `policy`
  entries stay in the log and in the state as an auditable record; checkpoint
  acceptance and §5.3's `T` always use `norn.trust.policy` and
  `norn.trust.log_key`. §4.5 loses the word "supersedes", §5.3 loses "the index
  from which the key becomes valid", and rotation becomes a v2 feature with the
  log format already carrying it.

**What is settled either way, and is done:** a `policy` entry whose text is not
a policy is malformed (§5.1, `bad-policy-unparseable`), and checkpoint
acceptance must bootstrap from the trust file, because a node cannot replay a
log it has not yet accepted a checkpoint for. The fixture no longer publishes
the canonical-JSON torture string as this network's witness policy.

**Answered 2026-09-20: (a), v1 applies rotation.** `D-020` stands. The work is
6.5g-ii-a and 6.5g-ii-b.

**Why this was not mine to decide:** `D-020` is a recorded decision with a
rationale ("Rejected: re-issuing trust files to every device on every
rotation"), and (b) reverses it. It also changes what the Phase 6 gate is
claiming about, and `PUBLICATION.md`'s Gate A turns on the spec being
sufficient — which it cannot be while the document promises a mechanism the
code does not have.

- **6.5g-ii (part) H2 — the fixture was publishing garbage as its own witness policy.**
  - **§5.1's `policy` rule checked that the text was short, not that it was a policy.** So `49-policy-escaping` put the canonical-JSON escape torture into `state.policy`, and §4.5's "supersedes" made that the network's witness policy. A reader who followed the specification literally could not verify this network's own checkpoint; a reader who ignored §4.5 passed. Both read the same sentence.
  - **A `policy` entry must now parse as `c2sp.org/tlog-policy`**, in both implementations, with `bad-policy-unparseable` to pin it. That is true under every answer to the open question, so it did not have to wait for one.
  - **The reference gained a policy parser** — four directives, whitespace that is space and tab only, exactly one `quorum` line naming something defined. It never evaluates a policy; it only has to refuse a `policy` entry that is not one.
  - **The escape torture kept its job and changed address.** It rides in a label value now, where free text belongs, and still reaches `state.json`, so §14.2's rules stay vectored. The reference test that asserted `state.policy == ESCAPE_TORTURE` now asserts what it was actually for, in two halves: the torture survives into the state, and a `policy` entry replaces the state's policy.
  - **Inserting one entry renumbered sixty-one names**, and the guard from D-041 caught it — entry prefixes must equal their own index, and sixty-one of them were hardcoded. They derive from `b.index` now, which is what D-041 intended and had only half done. My own precedence test had the same disease: it looked entries up by `"90-bad-precedence-..."`. It matches by suffix now.
  - **What is left needs Franco**, and the question is written above: does v1 *apply* in-log policy and log-key rotation, as `D-020` decided and §4.5 promises, or record it for v2? Nothing in this repository implements it, and the two answers are different products. I have not guessed, and 6.5g-ii stays ⚠️ rather than ticked.


- **6.5g-iii H4 and H5 — two sentences that restated rules living elsewhere, and got them wrong.**
  - **H5 is the rarest failure this audit has produced: the prose, read faithfully, builds an implementation the vectors reject.** §5.2 defined a *valid* `device` signature as one whose `kid` the state **before this entry** maps to that device — and a `rotate`'s incoming `device_pk` is by construction not in the state beforehand. So §5.1's proof of possession on that key, which D-032 requires, was unsatisfiable by §5.2's own definition. Run 6 implemented §5.2 literally and lost three checks, `40-rotate-device-key-only` among them. §5.2 now says which key is meant, per type, including the incoming one and the enroll's **embedded request** — the second half of the same sentence said "the `device_pk` in the body", and an enroll body carries no such key.
  - **H4: §4.0 restated §5.1's ordering for empty `sigs` and got `enroll` backwards.** An envelope with `sigs: []` still carries an intact, device-signed request, so the device-signature check *passes* and the signer count is what fails: `threshold`, not `no-device-sig`. The only empty-`sigs` vector in the suite was a `note`, so nothing contradicted a reader who believed the sentence.
  - **And reason codes are consensus state.** They land in `state.rejected`, which §5.3 requires byte-identical between implementations — so one wrong sentence in §4.0 split `state.json` on identical log bytes. That is what made a documentation slip worth a vector rather than an erratum.
  - **§4.0 stops restating the rule.** It now says the answer is decided by §5.1's ordering and is not repeated there, gives the `enroll` case as the example precisely because it is the counter-intuitive one, and names both vectors. A sentence that duplicates a rule is a sentence that can disagree with it.
  - **`bad-enroll-empty-sigs` ships**, refused `threshold`, and both implementations agree.
  - **Two more hardcoded indices found and derived.** Two `add_raw` entries carried `"after": 45` and `"after": 52` — literal values that D-041 was written about and had missed, and that would have silently rejected their own entries for `after` the next time anything was inserted above them.
  - **Two mutations, two reds**: believing §4.0 turns `bad-enroll-empty-sigs` into `no-device-sig`, and reading §5.2 literally reproduces run 6's exact failure on entry 40.


- **6.5g-iv H6 and H8 — two checks the code had and the document did not, and one it had neither.**
  - **H6 is the better bug, and it is not about parsing.** The profile never said a decoder must consume the whole input. Append one byte to a governance entry and **every signature still verifies** — a preimage covers the decoded envelope, not the raw bytes — while the `entry_hash` changes, so `dup-entry` never fires. A decoder that reads one item and stops **applies the same entry twice**; run 6 grew `state.log_keys` from two to three that way, and reading one item is `cbor2.loads`' default, which is what the Python reference is built on.
  - **Our decoder already refused trailing bytes and the specification never said to.** §4.0 says it now, with the reason, because "the difference between reading one item and validating a document" is exactly the sentence a second implementer needs and would not have derived.
  - **`bad-trailing-byte` is `27-log_key` with one byte after it**, taken from the log rather than rebuilt, so the duplicate really is the entry the log applied — a freshly built one would differ in `ts` and prove nothing about replaying *the same* entry. Allowing trailing bytes turns the manifest red **and** `state.json`, which is the doubling made visible.
  - **H8 is the one the code did not have.** §10's procedure never compared the checkpoint's origin line with the network's, and neither did `logclient`: its `Anchor` had no origin field at all. `03-wrong-origin` is refused by the note name and key id — which bind the **signer**, not the subject. The origin line is the field that says *which log this is about*, and nothing was reading it.
  - **`checkpoints-bad/10-wrong-body-origin`** is signed by this network's log key, under this network's name, and names another log in its first line. Every trust-file consumer — `verify`, the agent, the monitor — now carries the origin and compares it.
  - **Two mutations, two reds.** Allowing trailing bytes accepts entry 55 and diverges the state; skipping the origin comparison accepts a checkpoint for another network.


- **6.5g-v H7 and H9 — the clock again, and the sentence I wrote to explain it was false.**
  - **`quorum none` is satisfied at *every* instant, not at none.** §5.3 said there is no clock under it "because the policy is satisfied at no instant" — my sentence, from 6.5a. A reader applying the definition literally gets an unbounded supremum, `T = ∞`, and drops every device carrying an `expires`. The **conclusion** was right and the **reason** was backwards, which is the worst kind of wrong in a normative document: it survives review because the answer looks correct. It now says what is actually true — a policy requiring nobody has nobody vouching for the time, and `T` is a corroborated instant or it is nothing.
  - **The derivation of `T` was executed by no vector at all.** All three of the fixture's witnesses cosigned at the *same* instant, so the newest timestamp, the median and the k-th newest agreed on every file, and `proven/cases.json` supplied `t` directly rather than deriving it. §12.1 claimed the area covered. The witnesses answer **seven seconds apart** now, which makes the 2-of-3 quorum time the *second* newest — `1760003607`, distinct from the newest (`…614`) and the oldest (`…600`) — and `proofs/cases.json` records the value a second implementer's derivation must produce.
  - **Two silences filled**: only a cosignature that **verifies**, from a witness the policy names, contributes a timestamp; and a witness counts **once**, its newest line, so it cannot meet a threshold by answering twice. Our `Satisfied` already builds a set, so the second was true and unsaid.
  - **H9: §8's fourth condition had no predicate**, deferring "not stale" to a document that does not ship. It has one now, and the A11 tension it raised is named rather than left to look like a contradiction: §5.3 forbids the local clock from supplying an **instant**, not from supplying a **duration**. A node's estimate of now is the quorum time it was given plus the monotonic time elapsed since — and monotonic time cannot be set wrong, only paused. That is exactly what `freshness.Clock` has always computed (`best + (mono - seenAt)`) and what no document said.
  - **Two mutations, two reds**: taking the newest timestamp instead of the k-th newest now disagrees with the recorded value, and letting `quorum none` produce a clock fails on its own test.
  - **A test that hardcoded "5m" found the change for me.** The status page's age is measured from the instant the *quorum* was reached, which moved by seven seconds; the test now derives it from the checkpoint's own quorum time, and derives the size too — it had been asserting the page mentions "90" since the fixture was ninety entries long.


- **6.5g-vi H10 — the instance, fixed twice, and the class, fixed once.**
  - **Run 5 reported two request negatives refused by their own broken signature. 6.5c fixed those two.** Run 6 found two more; checking the whole directory found **eleven of fifteen**. 6.5b's log says a fix should "close the class, not the instance", and one checkpoint later I fixed the instances and shipped a test that knew two names.
  - **So `mutate()` re-signs by default.** A patched request is signed again over the patched preimage unless the case is *about* the signature, which is three of them. SPEC §4.0 has stated that standard for the entry profile since Phase 0; nobody had applied it to `requests-bad/`.
  - **The declaration is computed, not written.** `cases.json` now carries `"signature": "valid" | "broken"` for every case, and the generator derives it by verifying the shipped bytes rather than by consulting a list — because a list is what failed. The two cases the strict decoder cannot read are named individually with the §4.0 construction route each relies on, and an unaccounted third would stop the build.
  - **The test enforces the rule over the directory**: every case must declare, the declaration must match the file, and **only a case about the signature may declare it broken**. A negative added later cannot avoid the question.
  - **One case cannot be fixed, and §12.1 says so.** `short-device-pk` carries a 31-byte key, and a 31-byte key verifies nothing — so no construction separates "refused by the length rule" from "refused because the key is unusable". It is refused-but-not-pinned, which is different from covered, and the not-vectored list now distinguishes the two.
  - **The proof it worked**: with the 32-label cap removed from both places that enforce it, `too-many-labels` is now **accepted**. Run 6's `no-label-count-cap` variant passed 285/285 precisely because the signature refused the file first; it would fail now.


- **6.5g-vii-a — three rules the prose pins and no vector did.**
  - **`skip-cosig-verify` is the one with an operational consequence.** §10 says every cosignature by a named witness must verify; a reader could instead decline to count a bad one and carry on. `08-forged-cosignature` cannot tell those apart — one valid line falls short of two either way, so both readings refuse it. The difference is whether **one witness with a bad build means fail-closed or business as usual**, on a network whose purpose is to notice.
  - **`11-forged-cosignature-with-quorum` separates them**: three named witnesses sign, two verify, so the quorum is met *without* the third and §10 refuses the checkpoint anyway. A reader that merely skips the bad line accepts it.
  - **`allow-proof-at-same-size` was a malleability in the artifact strangers exchange.** §11.1 says the `proof` is empty when the disagreement is at the held checkpoint's own size, because the two signatures say it alone — and `Check` *ignored* the field there rather than refusing a populated one. Two encodings of the same accusation, one of them undocumented. `fork/bad-proof-at-same-size` needed a branch with the same number of entries as the held log, which the generator did not have and now builds.
  - **§6 never said a verifier checks the hash tiles at all.** It says so now, with the reason that makes it a MUST rather than a nicety: a node serves the tiles it fetched onward to its peers, so one that accepted tiles it never checked would **pass on a lie it had the means to catch**. That is why `checkTiles` exists and why 6.5e-iv found the served negatives defended twice over.
  - **Two mutations, two reds — and the first tripped the wrong assertion.** Not counting a bad cosignature instead of refusing failed on a *structural* check before reaching the §10 one, because my probe treated "no error and no cosignatures" as a valid line. Reordered so the rule under test fires first, and the probe counts what it returns.


- **6.5g-vii-b — four replay rules the prose pins and no vector did, plus the boundary one.**
  - **§4.4's floor was never tested at its boundary.** The fixture's `after` values were 999, 5091 and 5 at indices 10, 91 and 75 — nowhere near it — so a reader rejecting only above `index + 1` passed every check. Two entries now sit on either side: `after` equal to the index, accepted, and one past it, refused. The smallest violation there is, and the one nothing had.
  - **`bad-request-net`: a properly signed request for another network.** §5.1 requires an `enroll`'s request `net` to equal the entry's, and the only net negative in the suite patched the **envelope**, so skipping the request check cost nothing. This one is signed correctly, so that check is the only thing wrong with it.
  - **`bad-dup-id-and-wg` is wrong in both ways**, which is the only construction that pins §5.1's enroll ordering: an active `device_id` *and* a WireGuard key active for another device. Swapping the two rules turns it `dup-wg`.
  - **`bad-device-sig-wrong-kid` is the subtle one.** The signature preimage excludes `sigs` entirely, so rewriting a signature's `kid` leaves the signature **verifying** — and a reader that searches for any key the bytes verify under, rather than resolving the `kid` §5.2 requires, accepts it. Strict: `no-device-sig`.
  - **Four mutations, four reds, one entry each** — `after-loose`, `skip-request-net`, `enroll-wg-before-id` and `ignore-kid-match`, all of which passed 285/285 for run 6. Two of them needed a second attempt: removing a check left the variable it used unused, and the build failed before the test could.
  - **And the reference's guard caught the new `after` vector**, as it should: `test_after_never_exceeds_the_index` forbids a stale `after` on any negative except the ones whose purpose it is, and `bad-after-by-one` is the third. It joins that allowlist by name. Worth noting that **I committed before `make ref-test` finished** and had to follow up — the four checks are not interchangeable, and the Python one is the slowest.


- **6.5g-vii-c — limits bounded from one side, and a directory nothing was checking.**
  - **A limit tested only from above is not tested.** `deep-nesting` is 200 deep, so a decoder enforcing 4, 16 or 64 in place of §4.0's 8 refused it just the same and passed every vector; `oversize-entry` was some 66 KB against a cap of 65535. Each limit now has a case sitting **exactly at it**, which must be accepted, and one a single step past. `entries-hostile/cases.json` carries `accepted: true` for the first kind, because a directory of things that must be refused cannot express the half that bounds the limit from below.
  - **The depth pair was wrong the first time**, and the vector said so: I built the at-the-limit case with `MAX_DEPTH - 1` containers, and our decoder — correctly, per §4.0's "the top-level map is depth 1" — allows `MAX_DEPTH` of them. The test caught the off-by-one immediately, which is the point of building both sides.
  - **§16.2's two padding rules were stated, enforced and unvectored.** `excess-padding` and `non-zero-padding` are the valid request's bytes with the *encoding* corrupted and the checksum recomputed, so the wrapper is sound and only the padding rule refuses them.
  - **And the mutation that removed both rules passed.** Not because the vectors were weak — because **nothing iterated `requests-bad/` asserting its cases are refused at all.** `trust-bad/` has had that loop since Phase 0; the request directory never did, so individual tests named individual files and a rule could be switched off in silence. That loop exists now, and with it the padding mutation turns red.
  - **Three mutations, three reds**: a depth limit of 16, a size limit one byte lower, and both padding rules removed.


- **6.5g-ii-a D-020 implemented: the log key rotates, and a node follows it.**
  - **Franco's call was (a): v1 applies rotation.** So `state.log_keys` stops being a record nobody reads.
  - **The rule, and why it is not circular.** A checkpoint must be signed by `norn.trust.log_key` **or** by a key in a state this node has *already accepted*. Reaching such a state requires having accepted a checkpoint under a key the node held beforehand, so rotation cannot bootstrap itself — and **a stolen log key cannot name its own successor**, because publishing a `log_key` entry takes the root quorum (§4.3). A key that vouched for itself would undo the whole arrangement, which is why §9.4 states the ordering before it states anything else.
  - **`overlap` is an obligation on the log, not a rule for the verifier.** It never belonged in the acceptance test: after publishing a rotation the log MUST keep signing with the superseded key for `overlap` seconds, so a node whose trust file predates it can still bootstrap and learn. A log that stops early strands exactly the nodes least able to complain, and a monitor watching the signature sees it.
  - **The trust file's key is kept alongside what is learned, never replaced.** A node whose trust file is current must never be worse off than one whose is stale.
  - **`served-rotated/` is the only vector in the suite with two correct answers**, and which one applies depends on what the reader has done rather than on anything in the file: refuse it holding only `trust.cbor`, accept it having replayed this log. That is D-020 in one artifact, and nothing expressed it before.
  - **Three mutations, and the second found a hole I had just made.** Letting a checkpoint name its own key, and stubbing `KeysFrom`, both turned red — but **the agent forgetting what it learned did not**, because nothing tested the agent across a rotation. That is the whole feature: a one-shot `norn verify` is *meant* to refuse an unseen key; only a node that keeps running has anything to follow. `TestTheAgentFollowsALogKeyRotation` exists now, and the mutation fails on it.


- **6.5g-ii-b D-020's other half: the witness policy supersedes, and one word separates it from the keys.**
  - **Keys accumulate; a policy replaces.** That asymmetry is the whole design note. An overlap window is the *point* of key rotation, so a node keeps the trust file's key alongside what it learned. A policy is one document, and two in force at once is exactly the disagreement about the quorum that §4.5 refuses to allow — so a published policy replaces the trust file's outright. Only the roots can publish one (§4.3), which is what makes that safe.
  - **Raising a quorum has to bind the nodes that were already watching.** Otherwise it protects only the ones who were not — which is the wrong way round, and is the sentence §9.4 now leads with. The fixture's roots raise it from two of three to three of three at entry 49, so the property is testable with what already ships.
  - **`served-superseded/` is the second vector with two correct answers, and the informed reader is the stricter one.** A head cosigned by two witnesses satisfies the trust file and falls short of what the roots published. `served-rotated/` runs the other way — there the informed reader accepts more. Between them they say what "supersedes" means in both directions, which no prose alone would have pinned.
  - **The roots can lower it too, including to `quorum none`**, and §9.4 says so rather than leaving it to be discovered: it is their prerogative, it is visible in the log to every monitor, and §5.3 already gives a node with no corroborated instant no clock at all.
  - **Two mutations, two reds** — the agent keeping the trust file's policy, and `PolicyFrom` returning nothing. Both fail on the agent test, which is again the only place the feature exists: a one-shot verify has replayed nothing and is *meant* to use the policy it was given.


- **2026-09-20 · 6.5g-viii cold-model gate run 7 — `SPEC INSUFFICIENT`, and two of the twelve are mine from this afternoon.**
  - **241 of 241 checks passed, every negative for its named reason, `SHA256SUMS` clean.** Seventh fresh agent, sealed copy, its own CBOR codec, bech32m, signed-note parser, Merkle, canonical JSON, `tlog-policy` parser and a TOML→CBOR reconstructor that rebuilt `trust.cbor` byte for byte from §9.2's rendering. It covered both two-answer directories correctly. Ninety-nine variants; **twenty-six still pass**.
  - **J1 is the one that stings: the specification contradicts a vector I wrote for it.** §4.0 says "the top-level map is depth **1**, so a value inside it is depth 2" — which counts scalars. `entries-hostile/at-the-depth-limit` is eight nested arrays around a `uint`, depth **9** by that sentence, and its `cases.json` says it MUST be accepted. I wrote the prose in 6.5d-i and the vector in 6.5g-vii-c and never held them against each other. Run 7's `depth-scalars` variant is **the only one in seven rounds that fails for following the document**, which is the worst way to fail.
  - **J7 is arithmetic I did not do.** 6.5g-v staggered the witnesses seven seconds apart and §12.1 now claims the fixture makes "the newest, the median and the k-th newest disagree". With three witnesses and a threshold of two, the k-th newest **is** the median. `t-median` passes; only `t-newest` and `t-oldest` are caught. The vector is right and the sentence about it is wrong.
  - **J2 is the gap I should have seen in 6.5a.** §9.1 restates the whole `tlog-policy` grammar because "a verifier cannot be written without it" — and §7 restates the *note* signature line, 4 + 64 bytes, "so an implementer needs nothing else". The `tlog-cosignature` line is 76 bytes over a different signed message, and this document says **nothing** about it. §5.3's evaluation time depends on it entirely. Run 7 got it right because it already knew the C2SP spec, which is exactly the kind of luck a cold gate exists to remove.
  - **J11 is the largest coverage hole found in any round.** §10's stored-checkpoint block — the size monotonicity, the recomputed held head, the FORK trigger — plus §11's fork *detector* and §8.1's condition 4 are exercised by **no vector at all**. Run 7's implementation contains none of them and scored 241/241. Those are the checks that stop a log serving a correctly signed view of its own past, and the material to vector them is already in the suite.
