  - **Verified before accepting**: §4.0's depth sentence against the vector's bytes, the median arithmetic, and that `cosignature/v1` appears nowhere in the specification.
  - **Seven rounds, and the verdict has not improved twice running.** That is not a plateau, it is the same pattern each time: the suite hardens where it was last attacked, and the next reader looks where nobody has. Runs 5 and 6 found holes in what the vectors covered; run 7 found holes in **what the prose says about itself** — a sentence contradicting its own vector, a claim about coverage that arithmetic disproves, and a restatement that was never written.


- **6.5h-i J1 and J7 — a sentence that contradicted its own vector, and a claim arithmetic disproves.**
  - **J1 is the worst kind of specification bug, and it was two of my own checkpoints disagreeing.** 6.5d-i wrote "the top-level map is depth 1, so a value inside it is depth 2" — counting scalars — and 6.5g-vii-c built the at-the-limit vector as eight containers around a scalar, which is depth 9 by that sentence and which `cases.json` says to accept. Run 7's `depth-scalars` variant is **the only one in seven rounds that failed for following the document**. I wrote both halves and never held them against each other.
  - **Containers win, and §4.0 says why.** The limit exists to bound recursion; a scalar recurses no further, so depth is the number of maps and arrays enclosing a value. Both implementations already counted that way, and the example moved with it: a request's `labels` **map** at depth 2, not its value at depth 3.
  - **The rule and the vector are tied together now.** A test counts the containers in the at-the-limit and past-the-limit vectors and requires them to be exactly `MaxDepth` and `MaxDepth + 1`, and requires the document to say which of the two things it counts. Either half moving alone turns it red — which is what was missing when a prose edit and a vector edit passed each other in the dark.
  - **J7: I claimed the fixture separates three derivations of `T` and did not check.** With three witnesses and a threshold of two, the k-th newest **is** the median. §12.1 now claims only what is true — the newest and the oldest are separated — and the not-vectored list says separating the median needs a fourth witness, which this network does not have. A test computes the three values from the shipped checkpoint and fails if the document claims a separation the arithmetic does not support.
  - **And the checker had the same disease as the prose it checks.** My first version looked for a literal phrase that markdown had wrapped across two lines. Whitespace-normalised now — the correction 6.3 already made once, for the same reason: a checker must not dictate where a sentence breaks.
  - **Three mutations, three reds.**
  - **The flaky test is fixed, and it was a real defect.** `TestTheLogCollectsACosignatureOverHTTP` failed under parallel load and passed alone, four times across this phase, and I re-ran it each time instead of reading it. `norn-log` asks its witnesses **in the background** — 2.9 made the first refresh asynchronous so a slow witness cannot stop the log starting — so the port opening does not mean the first round has finished. The test asserted on the first fetch after the port opened. It polls for the cosignature now, with the same assertion at a later moment. Three consecutive runs green.


- **6.5h-ii J2 — a restatement that called itself sufficient and described the wrong line.**
  - **§7 restated the signed-note framing "so an implementer needs nothing else" and gave one signature-line layout: 4 + 64 = 68 bytes.** That is a note signature — algorithm `0x01`, the log's and a receipt's. Every witness line is a `tlog-cosignature` v1 line: algorithm `0x04`, `key_id ‖ timestamp ‖ signature`, **76 bytes**, and signed over a *different* message — `"cosignature/v1\n" ‖ "time <n>\n" ‖ <note text>` — not over the note text at all. None of that was in the document, and §5.3's evaluation time is derived from those eight timestamp bytes and from nothing else.
  - **Run 7 passed because it already knew the C2SP spec**, which is exactly the luck a cold gate exists to remove. A reader who took §7 at its word — the document's only description of a signature line, presented as complete — parsed every witness line as malformed and could not verify `served/checkpoint`, any proof bundle, or the fork evidence. Not a failed suite: an unimplementable one.
  - **The document's own logic already said this was wrong.** §9.1 restates the whole `tlog-policy` grammar because "a verifier cannot be written without it" — a decision from 6.5a — and the one external format that is strictly harder to guess, and load-bearing for `T`, was left to a link. The asymmetry was the defect.
  - **Fetched, not recalled**, as with the policy grammar: algorithm byte, key-id derivation, the 8-byte big-endian timestamp, the two header lines, and that the note text runs through its final newline with no signature lines. Checked against `cosign.Message`, which had always done exactly this and said so in a Go comment nobody outside this repository could read.
  - **§7 now tells the two forms apart by algorithm byte**, gives both layouts, and says what a restatement is worth when it is incomplete. §5.3's `T` points at the eight bytes it comes from.
  - **Three mutations, three reds — on the second attempt.** The first run showed two of the three "passing", and I have been here before: both had gone through two layers of shell escaping around backticks and never touched the file. The mutation harness now **asserts the replacement count** before running the test, so a mutation that did not apply is an error rather than a false pass. That is the general fix for a trap I fell into in 6.5e-i and again here.
  - **And the checker needed the same correction the prose did.** It looked for `algorithm 0x01` and the document wrote ``algorithm `0x01` ``; formatting is not content, so the flattener strips code markers along with emphasis now. Third time that lesson has cost a red.


- **6.5h-iii J3 and J8 — a bundle that proved less than §8 sells, and a cap that refused a legal one.**
  - **J3: §8 promises a bundle is "self-contained, checkable by somebody holding only `norn.trust`", and §8.1 asked for the log's signature and nothing else.** No origin, no quorum. §11.1 asks all three of exactly the same kind of embedded checkpoint, and for exactly the same reason — so the two sections described the same job and disagreed about it. Run 7's `bundle-no-quorum` and `bundle-origin-after` both passed the suite.
  - **A checkpoint with no corroboration proves only that the log said so**, which is the one thing a transparency log may not be believed about on its own. `Bundle.Check` takes the anchor now — origin, log key, policy — and applies §10's three questions before it looks at the inclusion proof. `proofs-bad/no-quorum` and `proofs-bad/wrong-origin` force it.
  - **The clearest evidence it was wrong was already in the repository.** A test in `internal/proven` carried the comment "checkable by someone holding only `norn.trust` — which is the whole point of a bundle (§8)" **while passing the log key alone**. The comment was right and the call beneath it was not, and nobody read the two together.
  - **`Bundle.Check` has no production caller** — bundles are produced here and consumed by the gossip layer that Phase 3 builds — so the checker that was wrong was also the one nothing ran. That made the signature change free and is worth remembering when Phase 3 wires it up.
  - **J8: our decoder applied the entry cap to every artifact, so a bundle wrapping a maximal entry was refused.** §4.0 states `MAX_ENTRY_BYTES`, and §8.1, §9.1 and §11.1 each say "deterministic CBOR per §4.0" — so a reader inherits a cap that the largest legal bundle *necessarily* exceeds, because a bundle wraps an entry. `cbor.Decode` did precisely that for all five document kinds.
  - **Each artifact states its own bound now**, and none is unbounded: entries and requests keep 65535, because that is §6's uint16 framing and nothing else; bundles, trust files and evidence each carry their own. A cap that makes a legal document unrepresentable is not a safety limit, it is a bug with a reassuring name.
  - **Three mutations, three reds**, with the harness asserting the replacement landed — the fix from 6.5h-ii, used in anger.


- **6.5h-iv J11 — the suite had never shown one reader two checkpoints.**
  - **Everything in the suite was a single artifact judged on its own**, so §10's stored-checkpoint block, §11's four duties and §8.1's condition 4 were reached by nothing. Run 7's implementation contained **none of them** and scored 241/241. That is not a gap in a rule; it is a gap in the *shape* of the evidence — a whole class of behaviour that only exists across two artifacts, and the suite had no way to express one.
  - **`detect/` ships pairs**: the checkpoint a reader already holds, and the one it is then offered, each branch served with its own tiles as a real log would. Four outcomes, because a reader has to tell four things apart — accept a head that grows, refuse one that shrinks **without accusing anybody**, and prove a fork at the same size and on a shorter branch.
  - **The fourth is the one that matters and the one nobody would think to build.** A forking log serving a *shorter* divergent branch looks exactly like an ordinary rollback, and a reader that applies "a log does not shrink" first files it as one, leaves no evidence, and never raises the alarm §11 rule 1 requires. Mutating our code to check the size rule before the root comparison — the bug the code's own comment says it was written to avoid — turns that one case red and leaves the other three green. It is the only vector in the suite that catches it.
  - **And refusing is not accusing.** `refuse-shrunk` asserts the reader refuses *and* does not raise a fork: §11 rule 4 makes an accusation permanent until an operator installs a new trust file, so a reader that cannot prove a fork must not claim one. That half of the test is as load-bearing as the other.
  - **`detect/staleness.json` gives condition 4 its table.** `params.staleness` is carried all the way into `state.json` and, until now, was read by no file and no test — a number shipped to every implementer with nothing saying what to do with it. The table includes `elapsed` exactly on the boundary, which is the only value two implementations will disagree about, and the no-clock case, where a node with no corroborated instant reports `unknown` rather than guessing.
  - **Five mutations, five reds**, including two that bit the wrong case first: removing the stored block entirely, and comparing at the stored size rather than at the smaller of the two, both trip `refuse-shrunk` before they reach the fork cases. Worth knowing that a mutation landing on a neighbour is not the same as landing on its target — the fifth was built specifically to reach the shorter-branch case, and did.

- **6.5h-v J4, J5, J6, J9, J10 — five rules the prose states and nothing reached.**
  - **Seven new log entries, one genesis case, one request and one served negative.** The log is 112 entries now, 74 of them rejecting. Each vector is wrong in exactly the ways its rule needs and in no others, because a negative that dies early pins the rule it died to, not the one it is named for.
  - **J4, the hash tiles.** §6 says the served `tile/<L>/…` hashes "MUST also agree with the tree it rebuilt" and that "checking them is not optional", and `served-bad/` held two cases, both *entry bundle* cases. So `blind-tiles` — a verifier that never opens `tile/0/…` at all — reproduced the whole manifest. `tampered-hash-tile` changes one byte inside one leaf hash: the entries and the signed head stay honest, the verifier still reaches the right root, and only the tile rule can refuse it. Mutating `checkTiles` away turns that case red and leaves the other two served negatives green, which is the shape a negative should have.
  - **J5, `params`, the widest divergence in the machine.** The rule is "non-empty, only §4.5 keys, each of the right type" and only the first third was refused anywhere: the suite's one unknown-body-key negative is a *note*, and no genesis carried a `params` map at all. Two readings of the same log end with a different `staleness`, so a different freshness verdict — and at entry 0 one aborts to the zero state while the other replays every entry. `bad-params-wrong-type` puts a CBOR `true` where a uint belongs, which is the trap: **the Python reference accepted it**, because `isinstance(True, int)` is true and nothing had ever asked. The reference was fixed in the same checkpoint the vector was written.
  - **J9, the order *inside* `enroll` and `rotate`.** §5.1 calls its per-type tables normative, §4.0 explains one of them a second time — "an unsigned `enroll` is `threshold` and not `no-device-sig`, because the embedded request is still signed by the device" — and the vector that paragraph names has a *valid* request signature, so it cannot tell the two orders apart. The rule was explained twice and pinned zero times. Three entries now sit at the three boundaries, each wrong in both of its ways at once: an unsigned request with no signers (`no-device-sig`), a foreign-net request that is also unsigned (`decode`), and a rotate that takes another device's WireGuard key while carrying an unproven incoming `device_pk` (`dup-wg`).
  - **J10, the seen set's timing.** §5.1 step 1 states two things: the hash is recorded *before* the entry is decoded, and entry 0's is recorded too. The suite's repeat was of an entry that decodes cleanly, and its `dup-entry` copy was of entry **1**. So `seen-after-decode` and `no-genesis-seen` both passed. `bad-repeat-of-undecodable` copies the first entry that fails §4.0 outright — found by *trying to decode*, never by index, because an index would rot — and `bad-repeat-of-genesis` copies entry 0 byte for byte. The divergence is `rejected` itself, which §5.3 makes byte-identical between implementations.
  - **J6 was worse than run 7 described.** The finding was that §16.2's one positive MUST — "an all-uppercase form MUST also be accepted, because QR alphanumeric mode has no lowercase letters" — had no vector. It also had no *reader*: `testdata/vectors/requests/` shipped four positive cases and **nothing in this repository ever opened them**, not a Go test and not a Python one. A vector nobody reads is a file, not a check (D-043). `requests/cases.json` now carries a `form` per case, `req-minimal-upper` is the same request in uppercase, and a test walks all five — text, CBOR and JSON mirror, signature, preimage hash — so the whole positive directory is load-bearing for the first time.
  - **Two more of §12.1's numbers were stale, and nothing counted them.** It claimed 51 rejecting entries where the fixture had 67, and 23 request refusals where it had 25. That is J7's failure again, one round later, in a table whose own opening sentence calls an inaccurate coverage claim "the same failure wearing a different hat". Every countable claim there is now written `**N <noun>**` and checked against the suite, and the check fails if a noun it guards stops appearing — so a number that goes stale, and a number quietly deleted, both break the build.
  - **Ten mutations, ten reds.** The first attempt was a *false* red: the mutation did not compile, and `cmd/norn`'s tests build the binary at runtime, so the failure looked like the vector working. The harness now runs `go build ./...` before the test and refuses to report anything if the mutation does not compile — which is the same lesson as asserting the replacement count, one layer further out.

- **6.5h-vi-a J12 (six of thirteen) — the tail, and two places the two implementations had already drifted.**
  - **J12 is not thirteen missing vectors; it is thirteen rules some *other* rule happened to refuse first.** Nothing dramatic happens when one is removed, which is exactly why they survive: every test on both sides still passes. Split into three rounds — the replay layer, the trust file and decoder, and the two the prose never actually decided.
  - **Two of the six were live divergences.** The Python reference never capped `revoke`'s `reason` at 256 bytes and never bounded the genesis `name` at 1–64; Go did both. The implementations had disagreed for some time and the suite could not see it, because no vector ever put a 257-byte reason or a 65-byte name in front of them. That is what "refused-but-not-pinned" looks like from the inside — not a hypothetical second implementer, but the second implementation in this repository.
  - **The `kid` one is the document arguing with itself.** §4.0 draws the distinction explicitly — a malformed `kid` is `decode`, one that merely fails to resolve is `unknown-key`, "that distinction matters because the two produce different reason codes" — and then vectored only the `sigs` half. `bad-kid-format-in-body` is the other half.
  - **The role one needed a signature that really was made.** `bad-device-sig-wrong-role` has `printer` sign its own rotate under `role: "signer"`, where its key resolves to nothing. An entry merely *missing* a device signature pins nothing about roles; this one is accepted by any reader that takes a valid signature at face value and ignores what it declares itself to be.
  - **`genesis-params-replace` was fixed in the fixture, not in a new file.** The genesis body set all four §4.5 keys, so overlay and replace reached the same state and §5.3's rule was unobservable. It now sets three; `checkpoint_interval` reaches `state.json` from the default and from nowhere else, so a reader that substitutes rather than overlays loses the field.
  - **One of the six is a *positive* vector, and it is the one worth the most.** §4.3 deliberately does not constrain the embedded request's `ts` against the entry's — a device is headless, its clock is advisory, and a request may queue. Every request in the fixture was older than its entry, so a reader adding the obvious `request.ts <= entry.ts` check diverged silently. `enroll-request-from-the-future` is dated ten days ahead and must be **accepted**. The mutation for it *adds* a check rather than removing one, which is the only way to catch a rule whose content is "do not do this".
  - **Six mutations, six reds.** The derived-number checks earned their keep in the same round: changing the genesis body moved the trust fingerprint, and the build named every stale copy of it in `SPEC.md` and `README.md` before a human could have noticed.

- **6.5h-vi-b J12 (five more) — and one the mutation refused to let me fake.**
  - **All four trust rules were already implemented in both languages.** The gap was purely that no file could reach them: `07-no-witnesses` also breaks the policy/witness correspondence, `08-duplicate-witness-name` lists `pi` twice and nothing else so the policy names two witnesses the file does not, and `name`/`log_urls` had only their *absent* case. Five new anchors, each with exactly one defect: `26-name-too-long`, `27-name-empty`, `28-empty-log-urls`, `29-no-witnesses-only` (whose policy is `quorum none` and names no witnesses either, so correspondence agrees) and `30-duplicate-witness-name-only` (all three witnesses **plus** a byte-identical second `pi`).
  - **The fifth one failed its mutation, and was right to.** Disabling the uniqueness rule left `30` still refused — by the correspondence loop, which struck each name off with `delete` as it matched, so the second `pi` looked like a witness the policy never names. §9.1's two rules had been conflated in **both** implementations: one about *which* names appear, one about how many times. An implementer who omitted uniqueness altogether was being saved by an unrelated rule, and no vector could have said so. Correspondence is now set-based in Go and in the reference, the two rules produce their own errors, and §9.1 says which is which. The mutation is red on the second run.
  - **The map-key cap was vectored in the wrong layer.** §4.0 caps every map key at 1–23 bytes and the only case for it was `requests-bad/oversize-label-key`, which the `labels` *field* rule refuses first — §12.1 half-admitted that and listed the rule as covered anyway. It belongs with the other two resource limits: `entries-hostile/at-the-key-limit` (23 bytes, accepted) and `past-the-key-limit` (24 bytes, a one-pair map with a uint value, so nothing but the cap can refuse it). That directory now carries **three** limits with both sides of each, and the boundary assertion in its consumer went from two to three.
  - **Five mutations, five reds, plus a control.** Removing the correspondence rule still turns `17` and `18` red, so separating it from uniqueness cost nothing.

- **6.5h-vi-c J12's last two — and the worst defect any gate round has found.**
  - **These two were never missing vectors. They were missing rules**, and writing the vector was the easy half.
  - **`fork/evidence.cbor`'s `policy` field had no validation rule, and `Check` verified both checkpoints under it.** The witness *keys* came from the reader's trust file and the *quorum* came from the accuser's — the split is why it survived seven rounds. §11.1 opens by saying a reader has "no reason to trust whoever handed them the file"; §11 rule 4 makes a fork conclusion permanent until an operator installs a new trust file. So an accuser who writes `quorum none` gets two **uncosigned** checkpoints accepted as proof of equivocation and freezes the network — from a file, with no keys, no access, and nothing to forge. A permanent remote denial of service, in the one artifact this protocol builds for strangers to check.
  - **The fix is the reader's own policy, and the field is informational.** `Check` now takes `readerPolicy`; the carried one must parse, because a closed schema with a stated grammar is part of the format, and decides nothing. Three vectors: `bad-policy-says-quorum-none` (the attack), `bad-policy-unparseable` (the format half), and `policy-not-the-readers` — a **positive** case whose carried policy requires a witness that cosigned nothing, so a reader that verified under the field would refuse a real fork and skip the alarm §11 rule 1 requires. Both directions have to be pinned or the fix has a mirror image.
  - **The cosignature one is a reading §10 and §5.3 never settled.** §10 says every cosignature by a named witness must verify; §5.3 says two verifying lines from one witness are its newest. Neither covered a witness supplying *both*. `checkpoints-bad/12-second-line-from-one-witness` gives `friend` a second, newer line that does not verify, and the mutation that reads §10 per *witness* rather than per *line* turns that case red and leaves `11` green — which is the proof the two cases separate two readings rather than repeating one. §10 and §5.3 now say per line, in both places.
  - **Four mutations, four reds**, each landing on its own case: the accuser's policy (both the negative and the positive), the unparseable one, and the per-witness reading.

- **2026-09-21 · 6.5h-vii cold-model gate run 8 — `SPEC INSUFFICIENT`, and the document now fails against its own vectors.**
  - **582 of 582 checks passed, 430/430 `SHA256SUMS`, no vector disputed.** The eighth fresh agent, a sealed copy, stdlib-only Go: its own CBOR codec, canonical JSON, signed-note and cosignature parser, RFC 6962 tree, tlog-tiles reader, `tlog-policy` parser, bech32m and Crockford base32. It covered both two-answer directories correctly and read `detect/` in both directions. **One hundred and eighty variants; fifty-eight still pass.**
  - **D1 is the worst defect any round has produced, and it is mine from three checkpoints ago.** §10's stored-checkpoint block begins `require cp.size >= stored_cp.size`. For `detect/fork-on-a-shorter-branch` — stored at 116, served a divergent branch at 41 — that require fails, so a reader following the prose answers **`refuse`** where `cases.json` demands **`fork`**. The literal reading files a forking log's shorter branch as an ordinary rollback: no evidence, no alarm, exactly the failure I wrote `detect/` in 6.5h-iv to catch. I built the vector, wrote a paragraph about why it is the case nobody would think to build, and never held §10's prose against it. Same shape as run 7's J1, one round later.
  - **D2 is the same class.** §10's pseudocode checks `cp.origin` *before* the log signature; §12.1 says "signature first" and `proofs-bad/cases.json` requires `foreign-checkpoint` to be refused about the signature. Two sentences of my own prose disagree, and the vectors side with the second.
  - **D5 is the bug I fixed in 6.5h-vi-b, in a file I did not check.** `trust-bad/15-witness-key-is-a-note-key` lists **one** witness against a policy naming three, so the correspondence rule refuses it and §9.1's witness-key-algorithm MUST is never reached — `trust-no-witness-key-alg` passes all 582. I fixed exactly this for `07` and `08` and never asked which other case had it.
  - **D24 is sharper than my mutation was.** §9.1 now *predicts* that a strike-out correspondence reader passes without a uniqueness rule, and `30-duplicate-witness-name-only` was added to close it. It does not: `trust-correspondence-strikeout + trust-allow-dup-witness-name` — a reader with **no** uniqueness rule at all — passes all 582. My mutation was red only because I had already made *my* correspondence set-based in the same commit. A mutation measures my implementation; a variant measures the space of conforming ones, and they are not the same test.
  - **D13 catches an overclaim from the round before it.** §5.3 now says `checkpoints-bad/12` is the vector for the newest-line tie-break. It is not: 12's second line does not verify, so §10's per-line rule refuses it first. Twelve pins the per-line rule, which §12.1 says correctly; the tie-break needs two lines from one witness that **both** verify, and nothing in the suite has that.
  - **Three counts and four cross-references are simply wrong**: `proofs-bad/` holds six and two documents say four; `04` and `08` stopped being "the only two a signature-blind verifier accepts" when `11` and `12` were added; §4.0 still says "two resource limits"; and §14, §12.1 and §4.3 cite `49-policy-escaping`, `policy-escaping`, `revoke-by-device` and `61-bad-thr-zero`, none of which exist — in a document whose preamble says every cross-reference resolves. The count check I built in 6.5h-v guards four numbers and none of these.
  - **Verified before accepting:** D1 against §10's text and `detect/cases.json` side by side, D2 against §10 and §12.1, D5 by decoding `trust-bad/15` and listing its witnesses against its policy, and D6, D7, D8, D13 and D23 by direct inspection. Eight of eight held. The report is in `SPEC-DEFECTS-8.md`.

- **6.5i-i D1 and D2 — the only normative procedure in the document was wrong twice, and nothing here could see it.**
  - **Both are prose-only.** `checkAgainstStored` already compares roots at `min(cp.size, stored.size)` before applying the size rule, and `logclient.Verify` and `proof.Check` already check the signature before the origin. The code was right, the vectors demanded right, and §10 — the one thing a second implementer actually follows — said the opposite in two places. That is the gap a cold gate exists to find and no test here can.
  - **§10 now compares before it refuses.** `at := min(cp.size, stored_cp.size)`, the served root against this node's own root at `at`, `FORK` on a mismatch, and only then the size rule, with "refusing is not accusing" stated where the rule is. A node that cannot speak for `at` refuses and MUST NOT accuse — the honest half of the answer, and previously implied by nothing.
  - **§10 now checks the signature before the origin**, which is what §12.1 has called the loop since Phase 4 and what `proofs-bad/` has required all along: `foreign-checkpoint` must be refused about "no valid signature from a trusted key", not about its origin. Until the signature is checked there is no reason to believe any field in the note, which is the argument the prose was missing.
  - **The guard is an ordering test, not a wording test.** `TestTheVerifierProcedureComparesRootsBeforeApplyingTheSizeRule` and `…ChecksTheSignatureBeforeTheOrigin` extract §10's fenced block and compare the positions of code-like tokens — `min(cp.size, stored_cp.size)` against `cp.size < stored_cp.size`, `verify log signature` against `cp.origin == norn.trust.origin`. The prose can be rewritten freely and cannot be reordered quietly. Three previous rounds broke checkers by matching wording; this one matches structure.
  - **Two controls, two reds, each on its own case.** Implementing the *old prose* — size rule first — turns `detect/fork-on-a-shorter-branch` red and nothing else. Checking the origin before the signature turns `proofs-bad/foreign-checkpoint` red and nothing else. That is what makes a prose fix load-bearing rather than cosmetic: the suite already distinguished the two readings, and only the document did not.

- **6.5i-ii D5, D6, D8, D13, D23, D24 — six things the document asserted about itself that were not true.**
  - **D5 is the same defect as `07` and `08`, in a third file I never thought to check.** `trust-bad/15-witness-key-is-a-note-key` listed **one** witness against a policy naming three, so correspondence refused it and §9.1's "a witness key is a cosignature key" was never reached — `trust-no-witness-key-alg`, dropping that MUST outright, passed all 582. It now lists all three witnesses and puts `pi`'s note key in the policy line too, so names and keys correspond exactly and the algorithm is the only thing wrong. The fix generalises the test: it asks the question of any negative that is *about* a witness key, not of one file.
  - **D13 needed a vector, not a sentence.** §5.3 claimed `checkpoints-bad/12` pinned the newest-line tie-break. It does not: 12's second line does not verify, so §10's per-line rule refuses the checkpoint first, and `quorum-time-oldest-line` passed all 582. `served-twice-cosigned/` is the head that separates them — `pi` answers again three spreads out, **both lines verify**, so the checkpoint is valid and only the tie-break decides. Newest-per-witness gives `1760003614`, oldest gives `1760003607`, and `cases.json` records both so a second implementer can see which reading it landed on.
  - **`freshness.QuorumTime` was right by construction, which is why nothing caught it.** It collects every cosignature and asks which instants a quorum vouches for, so the `>=` comparison picks a witness's newest line without anyone deciding that it should. A rule nobody implemented deliberately is a rule nobody will implement the same way.
  - **D6 was a sentence counting files.** "`04` and `08` are the only two a signature-blind verifier accepts" stopped being true when `11` was added and again when `12` was. The set is generated now — `signature_blind_accepts` in `cases.json`, with the generator refusing to emit a name that is not a case — and a test holds the vectors README against it in both directions.
  - **D8, D23 and D24 are bookkeeping.** `proofs-bad/` holds six and two documents said four; §12 still said "two resource limits" after the third was added in 6.5h-vi-b; and §12.1's "What is not vectored" list now admits the thing §9.1 predicts — that a strike-out correspondence reader passes the whole suite with no uniqueness rule at all, and that no construction can separate the two, because a policy cannot name the same witness twice.
  - **Two controls, two reds.** `trust-no-witness-key-alg` and `quorum-time-oldest-line` both passed all 582 checks in run 8; both are red now. And the path checker caught my own shorthand mid-checkpoint — `checkpoints-bad/12` without its full name — which is exactly the class D7 is about, one checkpoint early.

- **6.5i-iii D7 — four citations to vectors that do not exist, and a checker that reported success on finding nothing.**
  - **The four were symptoms; the checker was the defect.** `TestEveryVectorPathTheSpecCitesExists` has guarded citations since 6.5e-ii and missed all four, for two reasons: it read **only §12**, so §14's `log/49-policy-escaping` and §4.3's `log/61-bad-thr-zero` were out of scope entirely; and it skipped any citation without a `/`, so §12.1's own `policy-escaping` and `revoke-by-device` were never looked at.
  - **Two of the four are index drift**, which is the failure §12.1 warns about two sections later in the paragraph explaining why vectors are cited by name: `49` is `policy-rotated`, not `policy-escaping`, and `61` is `bad-kid-format`, not `bad-thr-zero`. The document gave the advice and did not take it.
  - **The new numbered check runs over the whole document** and requires anything shaped `NN-name` — with or without a directory — to resolve. Measured before writing: that shape flags exactly the two real defects and **nothing else** in 1,200 lines, so it is precise without an exclusion list.
  - **The bare-name check runs over §12 and derives its exclusions.** Reason codes come from parsing §5.2's own table, so a new one never has to be remembered twice; the only hand-written set is external specs and libraries — `tlog-policy`, `signed-note`, `python-opentimestamps` — which is the same "is it rooted in the suite" judgement the path check already makes. Measured first: four tokens flagged, two real and two external.
  - **And the bug underneath both: a naive `` `([^`]+)` `` scan over a whole Markdown file is wrong.** Backticks pair left to right across the entire document, and every fence marker contributes **three** of them — an odd count — so one fence shifts every pair after it. The first version of this check captured **zero** tokens containing `policy-escaping` and passed cheerfully. A checker that finds nothing and reports success is worse than no checker, and it is the same failure as a mutation that never applied: the answer looks like the one you wanted.
  - **Three controls.** Reintroducing the index drift turns the numbered check red; reintroducing the bare name turns the other red; and scanning the **raw** file with a late dangling citation present turns the numbered check **green** — the checker goes blind, which is what makes the fence-stripping load-bearing rather than tidy.

- **6.5i-iv D3 and D4 — two things the vectors demand and the document never defined.**
  - **D3: three bounds promised, none given.** §4.0 said a bundle, a trust file and fork evidence "each state their own bound" and §8.1 said "the bound is this artifact's, not the entry's" — and no section anywhere gave a number. So `bundle-entry-size-limit`, `trust-entry-size-limit` and an **unbounded** baseline were all conforming, which is the same as having no rule at all. The numbers now sit where each artifact is defined: `MAX_BUNDLE_BYTES` 81919 in §8.1, `MAX_TRUST_BYTES` 65536 in §9.1, `MAX_EVIDENCE_BYTES` 65536 in §11.1, each with why it is not `MAX_ENTRY_BYTES` and each refused **on length, before anything it declares is allocated**.
  - **The evidence bound is the one that matters most**, and §11.1 now says so: evidence is the single artifact a reader accepts *from a stranger*, so an unbounded parse of it is a denial of service anybody can post. That is the same argument as 6.5i-ii's carried-policy defect, one layer down.
  - **The reference did not bound trust files at all.** `validate` took a decoded map, so nothing checked length; `trust.parse(raw)` now does, before decoding. Go enforced it and the reference did not, which is a divergence no vector would ever have shown because no vector is 64 KB.
  - **D4: the ladder lived in a file that does not ship.** `detect/staleness.json` demands `fresh`/`degraded`/`hard_stale`/`unknown` and supplies an `S_hard`; §8.1's condition 4 is a yes/no, §5.3 never names `unknown`, and §15 deferred the machine to `GOSSIP.md`, which is not in the sealed copy. Run 8 reproduced the table only because the **vector file carries the labels** — which is the definition of a suite teaching what the prose should. §8.1 now has the four-row table, and §15 says the states are §8.1's, for the same reason §11.1's evidence format is here rather than in `GOSSIP.md`.
  - **`S_hard` is stated node-local and opt-in**, with the asymmetry argued rather than asserted: crossing it drops every peer, so a node never configured for it stays `degraded` for ever instead of going `hard_stale`, because a log unreachable over a weekend must not take a network down by itself. A reader that took it from the log's `params` would do exactly that.
  - **Four controls, four reds — after the first two were false.** Drifting either constant away from the spec's number turns the bound check red in both directions. But the ladder check first passed with its row **deleted**, because `hard_stale` also appears in the prose around it: presence-anywhere is not a test. It requires a table row per state now, and the `S_hard` clause verbatim, and both mutations are red.

- **6.5i-v D27 — the specifications a reader had to already know.**
  - **The preamble's list is the promise**: these are reused verbatim and "where they conflict with this document, they win". Three the body depends on were not on it. **BIP-350**, cited once in §16.2 — which gives the HRP, the checksum constant, the padding rule and the case rule, and so reads as though that is the whole encoding. **RFC 4648**, named in §2 and §9.1 with the alphabet never given, while §9.3 gives Crockford's in full — an asymmetry a reader notices and draws the wrong conclusion from. And **RFC 6962's proof subsections**: §11.1's `proof` field said "consistency proof" and cited *nothing at all*.
  - **Run 8 implemented all three from prior knowledge and said so**, which is why the report asks for that section. A rule a reader has to already know is a rule this document failed to state, and the gate exists to remove exactly that luck.
  - **§16.2 now draws the line explicitly**: it states Norn's choices within bech32m and nothing more, and names what it is *not* giving — the charset, the HRP expansion, the generator polynomial and polymod, the separator being the **last** `1`, the six-character checksum, the printable-ASCII range. A reader who does not fetch BIP-350 cannot implement the section, and now knows that before starting rather than after.
  - **§11.1 cites RFC 6962 §2.1.2 and its node ordering**, because a reader who implements their own ordering does not fail loudly — they reject sound evidence and skip the alarm §11 rule 1 requires.
  - **§2 gives the base32 alphabet**, checked against RFC 4648 and against the shipped `origin` before it went in.
  - **The guard is self-maintaining.** Every `BIP-nnn`, `RFC nnnn` or `c2sp.org/...` token in the body must appear in the preamble, so a future dependency mentioned in passing breaks the build. BIP-173 needed no exception: the preamble names it on a "referenced but **not** reused" line, which is the honest description of a limit §16.2 explains does not apply.
  - **Four controls, four reds — and the third was false first**, in the same way as the last checkpoint's. Deleting §11.1's citation left the check green because the *preamble* still named the RFC. The needles are scoped to their own sections now: presence somewhere in the document is not a citation where the reader is standing.

- **6.5i-vi-a D9 and D12 — six of the nine were not open questions but live disagreements.**
  - **D9 is a rendering bug with teeth.** §4.3's type table was split by the `thr` paragraph, so `note`, `log_key` and `policy` rendered as literal pipe text outside the table — three of the fourteen entry types, invisible to anyone reading the document rather than its source. The paragraph now follows the table it is about.
  - **D12 was reported as nine unpinned readings. Six of them were places the two implementations in this repository already answered differently**: a whitespace-only line, a `#` comment, a vertical tab as a separator, a duplicate witness name, a forward reference, and a `group` with no members. Each parsed in one and was refused by the other, and nothing in the suite could see it, because the only policy text anywhere was the fixture's own and one negative that is not a policy at all.
  - **One was prose against code.** §9.1 has said "fields are separated by ASCII spaces and tabs **only**" since 6.5a, and `Parse` used `strings.Fields`, which splits on every Unicode space. The prose was right; the parser is tightened to match, and any whitespace other than space or tab anywhere in a line is now an error rather than a separator.
  - **The comment rule went the tolerant way, deliberately.** Go allowed `#` comments with a test asserting it and no recorded rationale; the reference refused them. Nothing in the repository settles what `c2sp.org/tlog-policy` does, and I cannot consult it — so the choice was made on consequence rather than on guesswork: neither reading is a security difference, because a comment carries no meaning under either, and the tolerant one reverses no existing behaviour. §9.1 states it **and states the cost**: if upstream has no comments, a Norn policy carrying one is text other tooling will refuse.
  - **`policies/` ships fourteen whole policies**, six accepted and eight refused, each differing from a valid one in exactly one way, with the generator refusing to emit a case whose table disagrees with the reference parser. §9.1 now states all nine rules — blank lines, comments, the optional final newline, the separator set, leading whitespace, unique names, group members, nested groups, whether `log` is required.
  - **Five mutations, five reds — after one was false.** `bad-vertical-tab` first put the vertical tab in the *keyword*, where the entry is refused for not being a keyword and the whitespace rule is never reached: deleting the rule left it green. It goes in the **URL** now, which nothing else constrains. That is the third time in four checkpoints that a check passed for a reason other than the one it was written for.
  - **And a fourth checker had D7's blind spot.** `TestSpecVectorCitationsResolve` matched `bad-` names against the *log* manifest only, so a `bad-` case in `policies/` was a name it could not see. It reads the whole suite now.

- **6.5i-vi-b D15 and D26 — the profile was vectored in the wrong layer.**
  - **Every banned item had a negative, and every one of them was refused twice.** `65-bad-cbor-null` puts the null in `after`'s **value**, so a decoder that tolerates null hands the envelope check a non-uint `after` — and the entry is `decode` either way. The reason code is identical, so `manifest.json` cannot tell the two readings apart. I confirmed it by mutation before writing anything: allowing null left **every replay vector green**, and only a test calling `cbor.Decode` directly — a test of this repository's internals, not of the suite — went red.
  - **That is the shape of the whole defect.** The fixture has machinery (`decode_leniently`) whose stated purpose is to prove each encoding negative "decodes to the SAME valid entry under a lenient decoder". It cannot deliver that here: there is nowhere in an entry to put a null that a closed schema will not object to. The rule belongs at the decoder boundary, where `entries-hostile/` already keeps the resource limits — the same move the map-key cap needed in 6.5h-vi-b.
  - **Eight `bare-` cases**, one banned item each, whole inputs with no schema above them. Four mutations, four reds, **each on its own case** — null, negative integer, invalid UTF-8, float.
  - **Floats now say "float".** They are major 7 like null and undefined, so all three produced the same "simple value …" sentence and `says` could not tell them apart. §4.0 bans floats by name; the refusal names them, in both implementations.
  - **The reference is checked against the same file.** `entries-hostile/` was generated by the reference and read only by Go. A Python test now asserts every case behaves as its own table says — sixteen cases, both implementations, one file.
  - **D26 needed positives, which is the point of it.** §4.0 bounds `text` above only and `reason` at 0–256, and §4.3 marks `reason` OPTIONAL — so an empty `text`, an empty `reason` and an absent `reason` are all legal, and every `note` and `revoke` in the fixture carried a non-empty string. Three readings that **reject entries this document accepts**, invisible until something is accepted. `note-empty-text`, `revoke-empty-reason` and `revoke-no-reason` ship; both controls are red.

- **6.5i-vi-c D11, D14, D10 and three of D16–D22 — and one defect I argued myself out of correctly.**
  - **D11 is the security-relevant one.** §9.4 says a checkpoint is acceptable under the trust file's key **or** one a replayed state names — both, and *replacing* is the weakening, because a reader that drops the anchor key trusts only what the log said about itself. The fixture's second `log_key` entry republished **the anchor's own key**, so a replacing reader still held it and `log-keys-replace-trust-key` passed. The log now publishes two keys that are neither the anchor's, which makes the difference the acceptance of `served/checkpoint` itself.
  - **And the rule had no production coverage either.** `norn verify` holds only the trust key, which §9.4 permits for a node that has accepted nothing; the combination lives in the monitor, and nothing tested it — dropping the anchor key there passed every test in the package. A ten-line test closes it and the mutation is red.
  - **D14 is where I got it wrong first.** My mutation disabled the *Signers* check inside `addKey`, which is shared by both roles, so it broke same-role detection too and went red for the wrong reason. The variant run 8 actually named is "check only the role being added to" — `if _, ok := into[kid]` — and that one passes. Run 8 was right and my first mutation was the false one. `bad-root_add-existing-signer` adds a current signer as a root; the correct mutation is now red.
  - **D10 is the one that cannot be vectored, and saying so is the fix.** `dup-wg` for `enroll` means "active for **any** device"; the narrower reading differs only when the key is active for the enrolling `device_id` itself, which requires that id to be `active`, which makes `dup-id` fire one step earlier. Confirmed by mutation: the narrow reading reproduces the whole manifest. §12.1's "What is not vectored" list says so now, with the argument, rather than leaving a reader to discover it.
  - **Three cheap rules that had simply never been written**: a fourth key in a `sigs` element, an entry with `v: 2` — no entry in the suite had ever carried another version — and a `rotate` on a device that is known and revoked, where the only other `not-active` vector is a `revoke`.
  - **Six controls, six reds.** D25, the signed-note framing details and the trust-file members move to 6.5i-vi-d rather than pad this one.

- **6.5i-vi-d D22 and D20 — three more live disagreements, and a silent fallback nobody would have seen.**
  - **§9.1's table shows seven members it never constrains**, and three of the seven were places the two implementations already answered differently: neither capped a witness `name`, the reference accepted an `anchor` missing `max_lag` where Go refused it, and **neither constrained `anchor.mode` at all** — a file could say `enforcing` and two readers would do different things with it.
  - **The anchor's two halves get opposite rules, argued rather than asserted.** An unknown `type` is **ignorable** — anchoring is advisory (D-009), so a reader that cannot check it simply does not — and an unknown `mode` is **refused**, because guessing what an unrecognised enforcement mode means is how a node ends up enforcing something nobody asked for. Both directions are vectored, and the control that matters is the *over-strict* one: an implementation that refuses an anchor type it does not know is caught by `trust-good/anchor-unknown-type`.
  - **`trust-good/` is new and the suite needed it.** Every trust vector was a refusal, so a reader that *required* an optional member — a witness `url`, a known anchor type — rejected files this document permits and passed everything. A directory of accepted files is the only way that shows.
  - **D20's genesis `params` was the quiet one.** Entry 0 checked the *keys* and not the values, while a `params` **entry** has type-checked since Phase 1 — so a bool `staleness` reached `state.params`, `freshness.LimitsFrom` ignored it as not-a-uint, and every freshness decision fell back to the §4.5 default with nothing to show for it. Both paths type-check now, and both require non-empty.
  - **Two helpers were wrong in ways worth recording.** `parseBad` insisted every trust negative reach `Validate`, which is false once a case is refused by the *schema* — the suite's own comment claimed all of them "decode cleanly", and that claim is corrected. And `isolated()` globbed `*-<suffix>`, so adding `32-witness-name-too-long` made `name-too-long` match two files; it requires a purely numeric prefix now. A helper that silently picks one of two tests whichever it sorted first.
  - **Seven controls, seven reds** — after one false start: the role control first targeted a string that appears three times, so the harness refused to apply it rather than reporting a red.

- **6.5i-vi-e D21 and D19 — and two of the six were not missing vectors but missing behaviour.**
  - **`note.Verify` returned on the first signature that verified.** A checkpoint carrying the log's valid signature **and** a forgery under the same key was accepted, and the forgery never looked at. That is the reading §10 settles for cosignatures — every line naming a key the policy lists must verify — applied to the log itself, and this implementation had the opposite one. `16-two-log-signatures` catches it.
  - **A note with an unterminated signature block was accepted too.** Signed-note ends every signature line with a newline, the last included; `Split` trimmed trailing newlines before parsing and so could not tell a terminated block from one that simply stopped.
  - **The other two framing rules were enforced and unvectored**: an ASCII hyphen where §7 has U+2014, and a size line with a leading zero. Neither is exotic — a hyphen is what a reader writes who never looks at the bytes, and two readers that parse `0123` differently disagree about which tree they are looking at.
  - **`37-log-key-id-mismatch` pins the verifier-key id.** A key id is a hash of the name, the key **and the algorithm** — which is exactly what makes a note key and a cosignature key over the same public key two different verifiers. Every shipped key had a correct id, so a reader that never checked one passed everything.
  - **One of D21's six is unobservable and now says so.** §10 wants a witness key to be a cosignature key, checked at verification; §9.1 refuses a trust file whose witness publishes a note key, so no policy carrying one ever reaches the cosignature check. Every vector path into `cosign.Verify` takes its keys from an already-validated trust file. It is defence in depth, and §12.1's not-vectored list carries the argument.
  - **D19's empty log is a whole field of difference.** §5.1: an empty list replays to the zero state with `rejected` empty — *not* `bad-genesis`, because there is nothing to reject. Every log in the suite had at least one entry. `log-empty/` ships the manifest and the state, and §5.3 makes that byte-identical between implementations.
  - **Six controls, six reds — after one was false.** The ASCII-dash variant first only *tolerated* the prefix without stripping it, so the line parsed to a nonsense key name and was refused for the wrong reason. A variant has to be the reading a real implementer would write, not a hole poked in one branch.

- **6.5i-vi-f D25 — a label nothing bound, and a closed map that was prose only.**
  - **§8.1 calls `device` "the `device_id` this bundle proves", and nothing checked it.** A bundle's authority is its inclusion proof, which shows the **entry** is in the log and says nothing about the label beside it — so a bundle reading `device: "nas"` around another device's entry proved exactly as much as an honest one, and a consumer keying on `device` believed something no proof established. `Check` decodes the entry now and requires it to name the device: an `enroll` through the request it carries verbatim, the other device types through their body.
  - **§11.1 says evidence is "a closed map of exactly these seven keys" and `Parse` read the seven it wanted**, ignoring anything else. The rule was prose. Evidence is the one artifact strangers exchange, so a field a reader silently drops is a field an accuser can hide something in — and `evidence-open-schema` passed the whole suite because no file had ever carried an eighth key.
  - **`size` had no stated bound.** An accuser can only speak for sizes its own branch reaches; above that there is nothing to compare, and the consistency proof refused it with a message about the proof rather than about the claim. It is a rule with its own refusal now.
  - **The other three were enforced and unvectored**: the bundle's closed map, its `v`, and evidence's `version`. Three files, no code.
  - **A unit test had to grow up.** `proof_test.go` built bundles over `[]byte{i, i>>8, 0x2a}` — arbitrary bytes standing in for entries. Binding `device` to the entry made that impossible, and rightly: §8.1 says `entry` is "the entry itself, so the bundle stands alone". The synthetic log is real `revoke` entries now.
  - **Six controls, six reds**, each on its own case. That closes every defect run 8 raised: D1–D27, across six checkpoints.

- **2026-09-21 · 6.5i-vii cold-model gate run 9 — `SPEC INSUFFICIENT`, and five of the nineteen are rules I vectored without writing down.**
  - **255 of 255 checks, 327 cases across 22 directories, `SHA256SUMS` verified both ways** — every file listed, nothing listed missing. Every negative refused for its named reason, including all 81 `log/*.reason` checked against the manifest **and** against the sidecars independently. `trust.toml` rebuilt to `trust.cbor` byte for byte. **One hundred and ninety-seven variants; forty-seven still pass.**
  - **D1–D5 are the same mistake made five times, and it is mine from the last two checkpoints.** In 6.5i-vi-d and -f I added rules to both implementations and shipped vectors demanding them — the policy's `log` key matching `log_key`, a witness-name bound, a non-empty `log_urls` string, the `anchor` type/mode asymmetry, and §11.1's held-size bound — and wrote them into **§12.1's coverage table** instead of §9.1's and §11.1's normative ones. A reader of the normative sections cannot derive any of the five. Run 9 got three of them right only because *the vector is named after the rule*, which it filed, correctly, as reverse-engineering from filenames.
  - **D1 is worse than unstated: it is contradicted.** §9.1's grammar restatement says of `log` lines "Norn takes the log key from `norn.trust`, **never from here**" — and `36-policy-log-key-mismatch` requires refusing a file where the two differ. I wrote both sentences, one checkpoint apart. And because the invented rule is load-bearing for that vector, it *masks* three rules the document does state: `trust-no-origin-check`, `trust-no-log-key-name` and `keyid-not-checked` each pass alone.
  - **D5 is the same shape as run 8's D1, in the section run 8's D1 was about.** §11.1 numbers its reader steps and calls the order normative; no step bounds `size` by the held head, so a literal reader refuses `bad-size-above-held` at step 2 as an ordinary size mismatch — which `bad-size-mismatch` already covers. I added the check and the `says` string and never touched the numbered list.
  - **D6 is the check I built in 6.5i-ii failing exactly as it was designed not to.** `signature_blind_accepts` is *generated*, and its contents are **hand-written**; the guard asserts every listed name is a real case and never computes the set. So adding `16-two-log-signatures` in 6.5i-vi-e — whose only defect is a signature that does not verify — silently falsified the claim that those four are the set "and nothing else here". Run 9 built the signature-blind reader the comment describes and got five.
  - **D7's three exploitable policy readings are the most dangerous finding.** A duplicated group member (`group home 2 pi pi`) makes a 2-of-3 quorum satisfiable by **one** witness; `k = 0` makes it satisfiable by **nobody**, so an uncosigned head passes; and a nested group counted flat changes the arithmetic again. §9.1 states all three rules and `policies/` vectors none of them — `nested-group.txt` is parse-only, and nothing in the suite evaluates a policy against cosignatures except the fixture's own.
  - **What held.** §4.1's map-ordering paragraph and §14.1's JSON rules survived every flip — `sort-bare-keys` costs 107 checks and `json-sort-utf16` is caught by precisely the astral-label vector §12.1 says was built for it. §5.1's numbered ordering caught every ordering flip tried, each by the vector §12.1 names. The only ordering **not** pinned is §11.1's, and that is because the prose and the vector disagree.
  - **Verified before accepting:** D1, D2, D3 and D4 by grepping §9.1 for each rule (none stated), D5 by reading §11.1's numbered list (no held-size step), and D6 by reading `cases.json` against case 16's own reason. Six of six held. The report is in `SPEC-DEFECTS-9.md`.

- **6.5j-i D1–D5 — five rules moved from the coverage table to the sections that own them.**
  - **The mistake had a shape, and the shape is the fix.** Each of the five was added to both implementations, given a vector that demands it, and written up in **§12.1** — which records what the suite *covers*. A reader implementing §9.1 or §11.1 never sees §12.1's table, and has no reason to. All five are in the normative rows now.
  - **D1 needed a contradiction resolved, not just a sentence added.** §9.1's grammar restatement said of `log` lines "Norn takes the log key from `norn.trust`, **never from here**" while `36-policy-log-key-mismatch` required refusing a file where the two disagree. Both are true once separated: the key a verifier *uses* comes from `log_key`; a `log` line that is *present* must still name that key. The grammar permits the line, the trust file constrains what it may say, and the two sentences now say so in each other's terms.
  - **D4 was a coin flip by construction.** §9.1 presented `anchor.type` and `anchor.mode` identically — each a text member with its value in parentheses — and one is open while the other is closed. §9.1 now argues both directions: an unknown `type` is **ignored** because anchoring is advisory, an unknown `mode` is **refused** because guessing at an unrecognised enforcement mode is how a node enforces something nobody asked for. A reader who gets either backwards is wrong in opposite directions, and the suite has a vector for each.
  - **The guard is the point of the checkpoint.** `TestEveryVectoredRuleIsStatedWhereItLives` maps each of the five vectors to a phrase its *normative* section must contain. It is not a spell-check: removing any one statement turns exactly that vector's row red. The next rule added to the code and the suite cannot be filed in §12.1 and left there.
  - **Four controls, four reds**, one per rule — the fifth, `log_urls`, differs only in case and was caught by the same test while I was aligning the needle.

- **6.5j-ii D6, D8 and D16 — a claim that is computed, two caps that are vectorable, and one that is not.**
  - **D6's fix is not "add case 16".** `signature_blind_accepts` was *generated* with *hand-written* contents, and the guard I built in 6.5i-ii asserts every listed name is a real case — it never computes the set, so adding a case could only ever make the claim too narrow, silently. The test computes it now: a reader that never calls Ed25519 sees the note's body and the *names* on its signature lines, so a case is blind-accepted iff its body is byte-identical to the honest checkpoint's and the witnesses it names still satisfy the policy. No list to keep in step.
  - **And the computation was wrong on its first run, in a way worth keeping.** It reported `14-unterminated-signature-block` as blind-accepted, because I had modelled "blind" as *ignores the note format too*. It does not: blind means it never calls Ed25519, and such a reader still enforces §7's framing. A wrong answer reached by computation is no better than a stale list, and this one would have put a fifth file in a claim that is meant to be exhaustive.
  - **Two of the three byte caps can be isolated; one cannot, and §12.1 now says which.** A trust file pads through `log_urls`, whose count nothing bounds; evidence pads through a `witness` URL, which the policy grammar does not bound. Each stays correct in every other respect, so only its size can refuse it. A **bundle** has no such field — `device` is bound to the entry, `entry` fixes the leaf hash, `checkpoint` and `proof` are what they are — so an oversize bundle is necessarily wrong twice and a reader without the cap refuses it for the proof. The cap still belongs in §8.1: it exists for hostile input, which is exactly what no valid vector can be.
  - **The size rule belongs to the decoder, and two reference tests said otherwise.** They called `validate(doc)` on a decoded document, so `38-oversize` — a document that validates fine and a *file* that must not be read at all — passed them. Both go through `parse(raw)` now. A trust file is bytes.
  - **D16 needed a positive.** `anchor` is the one member §9.1 marks OPTIONAL and all forty trust files carried one, so a reader that required it rejected nothing in the suite.
  - **Four controls, four reds — two of them mis-aimed first.** The D6 control mutated the *generator source* when the harness only runs `go test`, so the shipped `cases.json` was untouched and the mutation proved nothing; it has to mutate the file the test reads. The D16 one hit a string that appears three times and the harness refused to apply it.

- **6.5j-iii D7 — `policies/` asked one question, and it was the wrong one.**
  - **Every case in that directory tested whether a policy *parses*.** Nothing in the suite ever took a policy and a set of cosigners and asked for a **verdict** — so three readings that change the arithmetic of a quorum all passed. That is the same shape as J11: a whole class of behaviour the suite had no way to express.
  - **Two of the three are parse rules §9.1 states and no file reached.** `group home 2 pi pi` is satisfied by **one** witness, and `k = 0` is satisfied by **nobody** — a policy carrying it accepts an **uncosigned** head, which is the entire thing witnesses exist to prevent. Both are now negatives.
  - **The third cannot be reached by parsing at all.** A nested group is *accepted* under both readings; they differ only in what it counts for. `nested-group` is built so the two disagree: `outer` is `all` of **one** member, and that member is `any` of two — so `pi` alone satisfies it, and a reader that flattens the nesting needs both. The `evaluate` table is the only thing in the suite that can see the difference, and the flattening mutation turns exactly those two rows red.
  - **Three smaller rules got negatives too**: a carriage return (`bad-vertical-tab` covered one of the three forbidden whitespace characters), an uppercase keyword, and a leading zero in `k` — `02` and `2` are the same number, and two spellings of one quorum are two policies that look different and are not. Both parsers were accepting leading zeros; both are canonical-decimal now.
  - **And I had introduced a contradiction in 6.5j-i.** §9.1's grammar table said there may be more than one `log` line, while the `policy` row I wrote one checkpoint earlier requires a `log` line to name `log_key` — and no key may appear twice in one policy, so a second one cannot be written. The row says **at most one** now, with the reason.
  - **Four controls, four reds**, and the fourth is the round's point: the flattening reading is invisible to every parse case and caught only by the evaluation table.

- **6.5j-iv D9, D17, D11 and D12 — one safety-critical gap, one interop gap, two honest admissions.**
  - **D9 is the safety-critical one.** §8.1 makes `S_hard` opt-in and argues why: crossing it drops **every peer**, so a node never configured for one stays `degraded` rather than taking a network down because a log was unreachable over a weekend. All five ladder cases carried `hard: 3600`, so a reader treating `0` as *a limit of zero* rather than *not configured* passed the whole suite while doing precisely what the asymmetry exists to prevent. Two cases carry `hard: 0` now, and the table's shape grew a per-case override — which the older ladder test had to learn.
  - **D17 is the interop one, and it needed a decision.** §10 says every line naming a witness the policy lists must verify, and said **nothing** about a line naming a key the reader has never heard of. `07-unnamed-witness-only` is refused either way, for want of a quorum, so the suite separated nothing. The rule is **ignore**: a witness cosigning a log this node's policy does not list is normal in a shared log, and a reader that refuses over one stops accepting checkpoints everybody else accepts. `served-extra-witness/` is the honest head plus one such line and MUST be accepted; the refusing variant turns exactly that case red.
  - **D11 is a dead clause and is now marked as one.** `root_remove`'s "and ≥ 1 root" follows from "≥ `root_thr` roots" and `root_thr ≥ 1`, so an implementation checking only the threshold is conforming. Saying that is better than leaving a second test a reader will look for a way to fail.
  - **D12 cannot be vectored and §12.1 says why.** `entry_hash` is over the bytes *as received*; the only entry whose raw bytes differ from its canonical form violates §4.0 and is `decode` at step 2, before any hash of a canonical form could be taken. The *ordering* half is vectored; this half is unreachable by construction.
  - **And the citation checker learned something.** It flagged `unconfigured-hard-stays-degraded` as a name nothing ships — because it read case names only from files literally called `cases.json`, and `detect/staleness.json` carries a `cases` array too. It reads any `.json` in the suite now.

- **6.5j-v D10, D13, D14, D15, D18 and D19 — the last six, and one of them was simply false.**
  - **D19 was a factual error about the format's own shape.** §4.0 said "the deepest structure the entry schema itself allows is a request's `labels` map, at depth 2" — and **every signed entry in the suite is depth 3**: the envelope map, its `sigs` array, and each `{kid, role, sig}` element. Verified against the fixture before rewriting. The sentence now gives both numbers and says why the limit is vectored only by `entries-hostile/`.
  - **D18 is the map-key cap measured in the wrong unit by a reader who guesses.** §4.0 says 23 **bytes**, and both boundary vectors were ASCII — so "23 bytes" and "23 characters" agreed everywhere and `key-cap-runes` passed. The at-the-limit key is 23 bytes and **12 characters** now, and the past-the-limit one 24 bytes and 13, which a rune-counting reader accepts.
  - **D10 turned out to be reachable after all, just not where §10 looks.** Inside §10's loop a node always holds `at`, because it stores a checkpoint only after replaying to it — so the "MUST NOT accuse" clause is dead *there*. It is alive for a reader holding a stored head **without** the branch: a monitor handed a checkpoint, or a node restarted from its stored one. `detect/shorter-with-no-held-branch` is that reader, and it must refuse without accusing.
  - **D15 had two halves and they part company.** The entry layer's `expires > ts` is vectorable — an `enroll` decodes its request under §16.1, and no entry had ever carried one that breaks it. The request *size* cap is not: a request travels inside an entry, so a request over the cap implies an entry over the cap and the entry rule refuses first. The two can never disagree, and §12.1 says so.
  - **D13 and D14 are the document being honest about what it is.** The vectors README claimed `SPEC.md` was "the only document required to interpret any of it" while §16.2 says plainly that a reader who does not fetch BIP-350 cannot implement that section. It now says `SPEC.md` is the only document *of this project*, and that the standards it names carry the rest. §6 states the entry-bundle framing is **big-endian** — which §4.0 has been leaning on to justify 65535 rather than 65536 without ever saying so.
  - **And the path checker was too narrow again.** It understood `dir/file` and not `dir/case`, so citing a `detect/` case by name looked like citing a file nobody ships. That is the third checker this round-series whose model of the suite was smaller than the suite.

- **2026-09-21 · 6.5j-vi cold-model gate run 10 — `SPEC INSUFFICIENT`, and six of the sixteen are the failure 6.5j-i was written to end.**
  - **236 of 236 checks, `SHA256SUMS` exact both ways** — 507 files, nothing on disk unlisted — and every negative confirmed refused *by its own rule*, verified by disabling exactly that check and watching that vector and only that vector start passing. That is a harder standard than any previous round applied. **Two hundred and eight variants and fifty composites; fifty-two and nine still pass.**
  - **D1–D5 are one mistake, made five more times.** Every one is a rule I added to *both implementations* in this session, gave a vector, and wrote up in **§12.1's coverage table**: genesis `params` non-emptiness (6.5i-vi-d), the note's terminating newline and every-log-line-must-verify (6.5i-vi-e), the canonical size line, and the bundle's `device`↔`entry` binding (6.5i-vi-f). Run 10 says of the last one, unprompted: *"I got this rule from the vector's filename and `says` string, not from the document."* That is the definition of reverse-engineering a suite rather than implementing a specification.
  - **6.5j-i's guard did not stop it because the guard enumerates.** `TestEveryVectoredRuleIsStatedWhereItLives` maps **five named rules** to phrases their sections must contain. It cannot notice a *sixth* rule — nothing obliges a new vector to declare which section states it. A list is not an invariant, and I built a list while describing it as the fix for a class.
  - **D6 is the same stale-claim bug one layer over.** I corrected `signature_blind_accepts` and the vectors README in 6.5j-ii and left §12's own file table saying "which two" when the answer is five. Run 10 caught it by *running* a signature-blind verifier over all sixteen files — which is what 6.5j-ii's test does for `cases.json` and does not do for §12.
  - **D7 and D8 are the two that are exploitable rather than untidy.** §4.0's table requires shortest form of **integers** and says only "definite length" of strings, arrays and maps — so under the table's own reading one logical entry has several legal encodings, and since `entry_hash` is over the bytes as received, two encodings of one governance entry are two leaves that both replay. That bypasses `dup-entry` by a route §4.0's whole-input rule was built to close. And "sorted" is not "strictly increasing": `V16` (no duplicate-key rule) and `V200` (non-decreasing sort) each pass alone and only fail together — a reader who reads "sorted" loosely *and* resolves duplicates last-key-wins, which is what an ordinary CBOR decoder does, accepts a duplicated key.
  - **D9 is the same masking shape in the policy grammar.** §9.1's most emphatic rule — `\r`, `\v`, `\f` are errors — is pinned only by an **arity** rule the grammar never states: both whitespace vectors hide the character inside an optional URL, so a reader treating it as a separator sees a five-field `witness` line and refuses it for the wrong reason. `V112` alone passes everything.
  - **Verified before accepting:** D1 by reading §5.1 step 6 (says only "has only §4.5 keys", which an empty map satisfies), D2 and D4 by grepping §7 and §10 for either rule (neither present), D5 by reading §8.1's checking procedure (no binding), and D6 by reading §12's file table against `cases.json`. Six of six held.
  - **The trend, stated plainly.** Run 9: 47 of 197 variants passing, 19 defects. Run 10: 52 of 208, 16 defects. The *rate* is flat at about a quarter. What has changed is the kind: the structural holes are gone, and what remains is prose discipline — rules that exist in code and vectors and not in the document. That is a process failure, not a design one, and it is mine.

- **6.5k-i D1–D6 — and the mechanism, because the list was never going to be enough.**
  - **The six are written where a reader will look.** §5.1's entry-0 step 6 now requires `params` to be non-empty and typed, in the same words §5.1 uses for a `params` *entry* — an empty map trivially "has only §4.5 keys", so saying only that admits one. §7 requires every signature line, the last included, to end in `0x0A`. §6 gives the checkpoint body's three lines with **no leading zeroes** on the size. §10 requires every line whose name and key id match a trusted log key to verify, and notes that the canonical signed-note implementation does **not** impose this — Norn does. §8.1 binds `device` to the entry, with the argument: the inclusion proof binds the *entry* and says nothing about the label beside it.
  - **D6 was the same stale claim one layer up.** §12's file table still said "which two" after 6.5j-ii corrected `cases.json` and the vectors README. It now says the set is **computed** rather than naming a number, which is the only version that cannot go stale.
  - **The real work is the invariant.** 6.5j-i's guard maps named rules to phrases their sections must contain, and cannot notice a rule nobody added to the list — which is exactly what happened five times. `TestEveryNegativeDeclaresTheSectionThatStatesItsRule` requires each case in a manifest to name **the section that states its rule and a phrase that appears there**. The generator refuses to emit a case it has no entry for, so a vector cannot be added without answering the question, and the answer cannot be §12.1.
  - **Twenty-five cases declare so far**, all sixteen `checkpoints-bad` and all nine `proofs-bad` — the two directories run 10's missing rules came from. Writing them out was itself the audit: every phrase had to be found in its section or put there. The remaining hundred-odd cases are 6.5k-i-b, and the test names that debt rather than hiding it.
  - **Three controls, three reds**, and the third is the one that matters: a case with no declaration fails the build, which is what the previous guard could not do.

- **6.5k-i-b — all 136 negatives now name the section that states their rule.**
  - **Eight directories, every case**: `checkpoints-bad` 16, `proofs-bad` 9, `served-bad` 3, `fork` 12, `entries-hostile` 16, `policies` 19, `genesis` 18, `trust-bad` 38. Each generator refuses to emit a case it has no entry for, so the question is asked at the point a vector is created rather than by a list somebody has to remember to update.
  - **Writing them out was the audit, and it found two.** `bare-null` and four of its neighbours were declared against a phrase I had written from memory — "No floats, tags, null" — which appears nowhere. §4.0 states the bans in **two** rows, one for floats/tags/`undefined`/indefinite and a separate one for `null` ("an OPTIONAL field is *absent*, never `null`"), and `bad-unknown-keyword` pointed at a §9.1 sentence that does not exist either. Every phrase now had to be *found* in its section, which is the whole point: a declaration you cannot satisfy is a rule that is not written down.
  - **`genesis` was the satisfying one.** All eighteen aborts map onto §5.1's numbered entry-0 steps — 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 — with nothing left over and no step unexercised. That is the shape a well-specified section has, and it took eighteen declarations to see it.
  - **Two controls, two reds**: deleting §5.1's `params` clause turns `genesis` red, deleting §9.1's no-witnesses MUST turns `trust-bad` red. The rule and its vector are tied together in both directions now.

- **6.5k-ii — the encoding profile's two exploitable readings, closed.**
  - **D7: "shortest form" was in the *Integers* row, and the only vector for it patched an integer.** A reader who applied the rule to integers and to nothing else passed all 236 checks. Under that reading one logical entry has several legal encodings, and `entry_hash` is over the bytes *as received* — so two encodings of one governance entry are two distinct leaves that both replay, walking around `dup-entry` by exactly the route §4.1's consume-the-whole-input rule exists to close. §4.0 now states the rule for **every argument**, length headers included, with the two encodings spelled out (`0x6D …` not `0x78 0x0D …`), and `log/75-bad-cbor-nonshortest-length` is `profile-base` with one text-string header widened — nothing else about it is wrong, so a reader refuses it for the header or for nothing.
  - **D8: "keys sorted" and "no duplicate keys" were two rules that hid each other's gaps.** A reader with no duplicate test still refuses the one duplicate vector, because equal keys are not increasing; a reader who reads "sorted" as non-decreasing still refuses it, because of the duplicate test. Hold the lax half of **both** — non-decreasing plus last-key-wins, which is what a lenient CBOR decoder does by default — and a map with a key in it twice is accepted. §4.0 and §4.1 now say **strictly increasing**, one comparison, and §4.1 works through why the pair of rules was worse than the single one.
  - **Two controls, two reds, and the first is the one that matters.** Enforcing shortest form for `mtUint` only — precisely run 10's `V01_LenNotShortest` — no longer fails an in-repo assertion first: it fails the **manifest**, `entry 75 … got reason "", want "decode"`, which is the cross-implementation contract. Relaxing the key comparison from `<=` to `<` accepts `74-bad-duplicate-map-key` while still refusing `66-bad-cbor-unsorted-keys`, which is the demonstration that this implementation has no separate duplicate test — the comparison *is* the rule, and now the document says the same.
  - **The log is 125 entries, 83 rejecting.** One inserted vector renumbered everything after it, which is what §12.1 means by citing vectors by name: the two numbered citations that existed (`104-enroll-astral-labels`, `84-bad-repeat-of-rejected`) were both caught by their own guards. The OTS anchor had to be rebuilt too — it commits to the checkpoint body, and the body carries the tree size — so `anchors/checkpoint-125.ots` was regenerated by `python-opentimestamps`, the only file in the suite this project does not write.

- **6.5k-iii — the policy grammar's last two, and they turned out to be one.**
  - **D9: §9.1's most emphatic rule was pinned by nothing.** "ASCII space and tab **only** … any other whitespace character anywhere in a line is an **error**" — and `V112_AnyWhitespaceSep`, the reading that sentence forbids in bold, passed all 236 checks. Both whitespace negatives put the stray character inside an optional URL, so a reader treating `\v` as a separator saw a **four-field `witness` line** and refused it for arity — a rule §9.1 did not contain. One unstated rule was standing in for another, which is the worst shape a document can have: delete the rule the vector is named after and the vector stays green.
